#5095. XRP’s 10-Year RSI Low Fuels Debate Over a Bottom

Home » Trendsetter News » XRP’s 10-Year RSI Low Fuels Debate Over a Bottom

Analysts see possible recovery signals, but extreme oversold conditions don’t confirm a reversal: further deficit remains possible.

XRP’s daily relative strength index has fallen to its lowest reading since 2016, according to chart analysis cited by Matt, the cryptocurrency YouTuber presenting the new Moon Lambo video episode.

His central argument: extreme selling pressure may signal an opportunity, even as XRP price trades below $1 and investors remain deeply fearful.

In the recording, dated November 17, 2026, Matt puts XRP just below $0.96 and Bitcoin at $64,571. He also cites a Crypto Fear and Greed Index reading of 12 out of 100, firmly in “extreme fear” territory. Those figures are presented in the video rather than independently verified market data.

The most striking figure comes from Crypto Insight UK: an XRP daily RSI of 16.17, described as its lowest in a decade. Matt acknowledges the limitation directly: “Just because we’re oversold doesn’t mean we can’t go lower.”

The weekly chart offers a less straightforward signal. Citing analyst D Greg Crypto, Matt describes XRP’s weekly RSI falling below 20, recovering that level and then quickly testing it again—an unusually compressed sequence compared with previous recoveries.

Under that analysis, holding above 20 could support a potential double-bottom momentum structure. A sustained break below it would instead leave room for further capitulation. The distinction matters: an extreme indicator reading is not the same thing as a confirmed price bottom.

Matt also highlights Charting Guy’s view that XRP resembles a previous cycle’s bottoming process, with a weekly bullish divergence and a possible re-accumulation pattern. That analyst maintains a minimum xrp price target of $4.36, not a forecast for the eventual peak.

The timing is considerably less certain. Charting Guy suggests possible altcoin seasons in summer 2027 and late 2028, while Matt argues that Bitcoin’s direction will remain important for XRP and the wider market.

Matt’s own response is to keep holding rather than trade the volatility. He identifies himself as an enthusiast without a financial background, not a financial adviser.

For now, the consequential question is whether exhausted selling develops into sustained buying. Moon Lambo presents several arguments for recovery, but its own weekly-RSI discussion leaves a clear risk unresolved: XRP price could remain under pressure before any broader market rebound takes hold.

#5094. XRP Whales Snatch $127 Million off Binance Below $1: Main Crypto News This Morning

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Bitcoin was trading at $64,768 on Tuesday morning, up 1.27% over the past 24 hours, after the price of the cryptocurrency touched $62,000 over the weekend. Ether rose to $1,810, and Solana gained 3.92% to $73.18.

Exchanges liquidated $279.23 million in positions over the past 24 hours, and the amounts liquidated in longs and shorts were almost equal, according to CoinGlass.

Binance co-founder Changpeng Zhao blamed the drop to $62,000 on a combination of ETF outflows, high interest rates, and the Anthropic IPO. Anthropic is set to go public on Nov. 19 with a $1.4 trillion valuation.

VanEck's head of research, Matthew Sigel, also ties Bitcoin's pullback to liquidity flowing into the Anthropic IPO. Sigel pointed to SpaceX, where the market bottomed 18 days after the listing in June. He also mentioned the risk of a $500 billion cut in Nvidia's AI funding after Jensen Huang stepped down as Nvidia CEO on Nov. 12. Nvidia's quarterly report on Nov. 17 is the company's first since Huang's exit.

XRP was trading at $0.966 on Tuesday morning, up 1.73% over the past 24 hours. According to CryptoQuant analyst Darkfost, whales have withdrawn more than $127 million worth of XRP from Binance in net terms since Nov. 10.

Darkfost said that between $12 million and $33 million worth of XRP left Binance every day last week, and on Nov. 16, whales withdrew another $21.4 million. Since July 2025, whales had mostly been sending XRP to Binance, according to Darkfost.

Darkfost said that during the August rally, inflows of XRP to Binance reached $75 million, and on some days, deposits went up to $270 million. Some of the latest withdrawals may still be internal transfers by Binance, which was moving funds between its own wallets after the hack.

U.S. spot XRP ETFs recorded net outflows of $19.1 million between Nov. 9 and Nov. 13, according to SoSoValue.

Ripple-backed Evernorth filed its first quarterly report with the SEC on Nov. 13, ahead of its Nasdaq listing under the ticker XRPN. As of Sept. 30, the company had written down its XRP holdings by $505 million, or almost 60%.

The market value of the 473 million XRP held by the company stood at $516 million on that date, and Evernorth plans to use the reserves for yield strategies and ecosystem development.

Developer Jameson Lopp reminded Bitcoin holders of the core principle: "Focus on your hatred of governments and central banks rather than your love of wealth. Bitcoin will always be easy to hold if you prioritize sovereignty over price." — Jameson Lopp

#5093. Lighter’s exploit raises uncomfortable questions for Hyperliquid’s $5.7 billion market

The attack exposed how compromised trading keys can drain funds, raising concerns about what similar unauthorized activity could mean for larger exchanges.

Lighter's Nov. 16 security update described how exposed trading keys enabled an account drain through public-pool shares, putting a potential Hyperliquid exploit in perspective.

Lighter said that withdrawals had resumed after its safeguard allowed about 5 million USDC to be returned.

Hyperliquid has roughly $5.7 billion in open interest, representing about 55% of the open interest in the DefiLlama ranking as of Nov. 16.

Depending on the account permissions, a compromised agent could cause losses through unauthorized orders and liquidation in the accounts it can trade. Wider losses would depend on the affected positions, liquidity providers, and market depth.

Lighter said that affected accounts used unsafe signing code outside its official software kits. Reused cryptographic signing nonces allowed the attacker to reconstruct API private keys from public signatures, while the protocol, contracts, infrastructure, and official kits were unaffected.

The attacker created a public pool under the affected account, acquired shares through another account, then moved victim funds into the pool without creating corresponding shares. Redeeming the existing shares allowed funds to leave.

The account said wallet signatures were now required for public-pool transfers and that API signatures had been made non-public, with IP allowlists and configurable limits added as further protections.

Separately, Bitquery traced a 10,393,057 USDC withdrawal from Lighter's Ethereum contract on the same day. Its investigation could not inspect the internal rollup transactions or independently establish the reported second account loss.

Hyperliquid's API-wallet documentation says a master account can authorize agents to sign for itself or its subaccounts. Its official agent example describes trading authority without transfer or withdrawal permissions.

The exchange endpoint allows an agent-signed asset transfer when the destination matches the source address, an expressly permitted internal movement distinct from sending assets to another owner.

Hyperliquid also documents a vault-transfer function, and its official Python SDK uses a different signing path for it than for external USDC transfers and bridge withdrawals. The signing code alone does not establish which agent the exchange will authorize to request the exchange.

The derivatives venue's current overview describes customizable HyperEVM accounting and agent delegation, separately from legacy HyperCore vaults. Those distinctions prevent a blanket conclusion about every vault.

A comparable Lighter extraction would require both the relevant permissions and a matching share-accounting path. The documented internal transfers and vault functions leave no basis for a blanket assurance that every agent-authorized vault path is impossible.

A stolen agent with valid authority could open unwanted positions, change exposure, or cause adverse execution. The resulting losses could exhaust the affected account's collateral through trading and liquidation, without a withdrawal to the thief.

The Nov. 16 snapshot showed Hyperliquid open interest of $5.7 billion, against nearly $554 million for Lighter and a tracked total of roughly $10.5 billion. Hyperliquid was about 10.3 times Lighter's size on that measure and accounted for approximately 54.7% of the total.

DefiLlama's Hyperliquid and Lighter protocol pages reported the following activity and bridge snapshots on Nov. 16:

The first layer of a hypothetical agent compromise is the authorized account. The next layer appears if that account supplies substantial market liquidity: missing quotes or forced hedging could worsen execution for others. HLP losses would require relevant strategy or liquidation exposure.

Market depth also matters more than a venue-wide balance. DefiLlama's exchange snapshot showed about $1.4 billion of aggregate dollar-perpetual depth within 2%, with far less available in individual markets.

Cross-venue effects could develop through shared intermediaries, hedges, and collateral needs, but those connections would have to be established for the affected participants. Lighter's incident does not demonstrate that chain of events at Hyperliquid.

Hyperliquid's earlier episodes illustrate why a trader's loss, a vault drawdown and a platform interruption need separate treatment.

On March 12, 2025, approximately $4 million in HLP losses followed a large ETH liquidation, leading Hyperliquid to subsequently reduce maximum Bitcoin and Ethereum leverage to 40 times and 25 times.

The March 26 JELLY episode led to a reported loss of nearly $12 million and a later operator statement putting 24-hour HLP profit at roughly 700,000 USDC. As a response, validators delisted the market after suspicious activity.

There was also a November 2025 POPCAT incident that culminated in an estimated $4.9 million HLP loss and a temporary Arbitrum-bridge pause. The fourth episode happened on April 9, with an approximately $1.5 million daily HLP drawdown amid suspected FARTCOIN manipulation.

HLP's 

The clearest documented compensation process followed the July 29, 2025 API disruption. Hyperliquid's Aug. 4 announcement described 37 minutes of delayed orders and error responses even as transactions reached blocks, and its refund method covered specified fills and unusually high funding payments.

The process excluded the smallest calculated refunds and required business or identity verification for larger recipients to receive their full balance.

An Aug. 5 update said refunds had been distributed, describing a conditional response to one operational incident.

Lighter's token LIT weakened during the incident, dropping from $2.75 to $2.32 right after it, but recovering to $2.88 as of press time

A historical HYPE comparison from 2025 shows the token fell from $14.04 to $12.84 during the March 12 ETH-whale episode, before some recovery.

Relevant escalation signals for Hyperliquid would include an affected account's trading authority and positions, deterioration in the markets it supports, losses reaching HLP, or disruption to settlement and withdrawals.

Its $5.7 billion in open bets makes those distinctions consequential, but the dollar amount threatened by a particular compromise would still depend on the permissions, exposure, and liquidity involved.

#5092. Bet25.com Launches Zero Margin Sportsbook to Eliminate Hidden House Edges

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Every sports bettor deals with the same issue: traditional sportsbooks hide extra profit margins within their odds. Behind flashy ads and complicated bonuses, regular bookmakers quietly lower payouts. When building a parlay or combo bet, these hidden cuts stack up fast.

Bet25 is changing this with the Zero Margin Sportsbook, setting the new rules of sports betting. Built on honesty, clear numbers, and fair pricing, Bet25 removes traditional bookmaker tricks, so the real payout is received.

In a standard sportsbook, the odds you see are not the true odds. The bookmaker trims 5% to 10% off the price of each game to guarantee their own cut before you even place a bet.

On a single match, this might look small. But on combo bets, it becomes a serious problem. Because combo odds multiply together, the hidden house cuts multiply too. Every leg you add gives you a payout that is much lower than the actual math says it should be.

The Bet25 Zero Margin Sportsbook is not a temporary promo or a short-lived boost. It is a completely different way to run a sportsbook:

A normal bookmaker takes a cut on pick 1, pick 2, pick 3, and so on. Bet25 charges one single 5% fee whether you pick one game or five.

The difference is easy to see on a multi-leg ticket. Take a simple 5-leg combo where each pick has a true 50/50 probability. A regular bookmaker will usually offer around 1.87 on each outcome to keep their cut, but a zero-margin sportsbook will price it at 2.0, again because of no hidden margin inside.

Same five picks. Same $100 bet. Exactly the same risk. But Bet25 pays out $755 more because no hidden cuts were multiplied along the way.

Almost every betting site claims to have “the best odds.” Bet25 doesn’t need hype or marketing talk. Instead, the advice is simple: just compare.

Open a betting site side by side with the Bet25 Zero Margin Sportsbook. Check any match, build a 3-leg or 4-leg combo on both, and look at the final payouts. You will see the difference right away. It is not marketing – it is pure math.

Turning a concept this disruptive into reality depended on having the right technological backbone. Bet25 teamed up with Vegangster as its primary platform partner, working side-by-side from initial ideation straight through to full deployment. Vegangster’s flexible, cutting-edge architecture gave the team the technical freedom to execute bold and complex features without friction.

In an industry where legacy tech often limits innovation, having a responsive partner who can translate ambitious mechanics into reality has been essential to Bet25’s launch.

Sports betting should be about making smart picks and getting paid fairly when you are right. By creating the new rules of sports betting, removing the hidden house edge, and offering naturally better odds, Bet25 gives players a clear, honest alternative to old-school sportsbooks.

#5091. Half-Year Silence: Shiba Inu Community Demands Answers From Missing Shytoshi Kusama

Shiba Inu’s lead ambassador Shytoshi Kusama has stayed silent on social media, with his last post dated May 13, 2026, leaving many questions in the Shiba Inu community.

Kusama remains a prominent figure in the Shiba Inu ecosystem and before then he had frequently communicated with the community about developments, project direction and broader ambitions. His limited public presence, which goes back to mid-2025, has therefore become a talking point for holders who want updates from the project's leadership.

With no confirmed return date or fresh public statement from Kusama, speculation in the community continues. At the start of the year, Kusama revealed he was working on an independent AI project.

His silence on social media hasn't been a complete blackout, as Kusama updates his X location and bio in the meantime. As reported, Kusama changed his X bio to “polish” and his location to “close.”

A low profile on X, however, does not establish that Kusama has left the Shiba Inu project or stopped contributing behind the scenes. No such departure has been confirmed in the information available at press time.

The question remains as to when Shytoshi Kusama will address the community again, with the timing yet unknown.

But the Shiba Inu community might have something to look forward to on November 19, 2026, after a teaser was dropped by a longtime Shiba Inu community member, Mazrael, in an X post.

Mazrael wrote a brief post that said, “Here goes something,” with the “eyes” emoji and a time: November 19 at 11 a.m. UTC.

Though the post did not disclose what is in the works, the timing has sparked the curiosity of Shiba Inu community members, with speculation mounting about a possible announcement or update. At press time, no further details have been provided, and what the teaser leads to remains unknown.

In a separate post, Mazrael shared an update on the MetaMask X ticker widget, which showed copycat tokens for BONE and TREAT, saying that MetaMask was fixing the root cause, not patching tokens one at a time.

#5090. The Week Jensen Huang Fell: How a Spy Scandal Toppled Nvidia’s CEO

The Week Jensen Huang Fell: How a Spy Scandal Toppled Nvidia’s CEO

In the span of a few days, the most powerful executive in artificial intelligence went from untouchable to unemployed.

Jensen Huang, who built Nvidia from a graphics-card maker into the roughly $4 trillion engine of the AI boom, resigned Thursday after 33 years, felled not by a bad earnings report but by a scandal with the texture of a spy thriller.

Here’s how a defining week for AI, and by extension crypto, unfolded.

The crisis began with reports that Huang had grown close to Alice Huang (no relation), an executive at Singapore-based Megaspeed, one of Nvidia’s largest Southeast Asian customers, whom U.S. intelligence officials reportedly suspected of gathering information for Beijing.

For a company at the center of the U.S.-China chip fight and a looming debate over tighter export controls, the national-security overtones were impossible to ignore. Nvidia’s board opened an independent investigation into whether Huang had been the target of a foreign intelligence operation, though it said it had no reason to believe company secrets or national security were compromised.

On Nov. 8, Huang apologized for being “unfaithful” to his wife, acknowledging the relationship.

What might have been a single lapse quickly snowballed. The New York Times reported a second affair, with Kimberly Powell, Nvidia’s vice president of health care, who reported to Huang.

By Thursday, Huang was out as president, CEO and board member. Chief financial officer Colette Kress was named interim CEO, and longtime director Harvey Jones took over as board chair. The abruptness stunned an industry accustomed to treating Huang as synonymous with Nvidia itself.

Then, a day after his resignation, Reuters reported a third relationship, an “intimate” one with a second unnamed Nvidia employee, citing two people familiar with the matter. Reuters did not identify her, and Nvidia declined to comment. Huang has acknowledged only the Alice Huang affair, deflecting when the Times asked about Powell.

Nvidia’s stock cratered more than 14% at the open on Thursday before clawing back to close down about 4.6%. Wall Street quickly sorted winners from losers: rivals like AMD and Broadcom fared better, while companies tightly tied to Nvidia’s business took the brunt.

The reaction underscored how much of the AI trade, and the crypto market that increasingly moves with it, rests on a single company.

The scandal then rippled outward. Singapore barred Alice Huang from returning to the country, Alibaba shares slid on reports it was a Megaspeed customer, and congressional committees had already sought a closed-door briefing from Huang over his Megaspeed ties.

The loudest names in tech, AI and crypto called the ouster a blunder, with one founder branding it “a massive mistake.” Inside Nvidia, an internal suggestion-box post drew more than 1,600 comments, many pleading for Huang’s return. “If you believe in the mission, bring back THE boss,” one wrote.

The board has moved on regardless. According to the Financial Times, it approached Arm CEO Rene Haas, a former Nvidia executive who led Arm through its 2023 IPO, as a potential successor, though Nvidia called speculation about any candidate “premature.” Analysts have also floated Nvidia’s Ian Buck and AMD’s Lisa Su.

Nvidia’s chips power the AI data centers whose explosive buildout has become intertwined with crypto, from AI-agent commerce to the mining firms pivoting to host AI workloads.

Leadership turmoil at the company that supplies the picks and shovels ripples outward, and it lands amid a broader reckoning over AI’s role in everything from cyberattacks to data-center politics.

Nvidia reports earnings Nov. 17, with Kress at the helm and the permanent CEO search still underway, a nervous moment for a market already rattled by a sharp week-ending selloff.

For now, the company that defined the AI era is leaderless at the top, its founder’s legacy clouded by scandal, and the question hanging over Silicon Valley is whether anyone can fill the role Huang made uniquely his own.

Bitcoin [BTC] keeps slipping, and Strategy keeps buying anyway.

If old patterns still hold, this dip might not have much longer to run. However, trouble is, we don’t know if those patterns still hold.

Their latest buy was a small one… at least, by their standards.

According to a filing on 16th November, the company bought 485 BTC worth $33.4 million. It also bought back $228 million of its STRC preferred stock.

Their stash was worth 850,846 BTC as of 15th November, plus $4.9 billion in U.S dollar assets.

Most of the money actually went to the buyback since Strategy’s been doing this lately. When it thinks its own shares are cheap, it buys those back instead of just grabbing more Bitcoin.

Last time was pretty similar too, with $82 million on BTC and $188 million on STRC.

Meanwhile, Bitcoin’s price has fallen down to about $64,800, 13% lower than a week ago. For his part, Saylor doesn’t seem too worried just yet. In fact, he just posted,

There’s a bigger problem on the horizon though.

In past cycles, Bitcoin broke out of its post-peak downtrend… right around 1,000 days after a halving. We’re at about day 935 now.

So, going by that, the bleeding could be close to done.

The chart doesn’t look friendly though. BTC has now closed six weeks in a row in the red, very bear market behaviour.

It also slipped below two big levels — The 200-week EMA at $69,500, and the 200-week MA near $68,200. BTC at $64,700 appeared to be under both. Until Bitcoin recovers, sellers will have the reins.

The old cycle says a turn could be about two months away. However, the chart says it’s not there yet.

#5088. How Bitcoin still competes with a 2.92% Treasury yield after inflation

The headline 10-year Treasury yield fell on Nov. 16, while its inflation-adjusted counterpart edged up and remained 29 basis points above its Nov. 6 level.

The 10-year Treasury yield after inflation rose to 2.92% on Monday, Nov. 16, up 29 basis points since Nov. 6. The move left Bitcoin competing with that yield even as the headline 10-year Treasury rate fell.

Treasury’s nominal yield data showed a decline to 5.12% from 5.15% on Friday, Nov. 13, a three-basis-point move. Its real-yield series moved the other way, edging up one basis point from 2.91%.

The real yield had stood at 2.63% on Nov. 6. The 29-basis-point rise therefore spans multiple sessions, with Monday adding only one basis point.

Bitcoin provides no contractual interest income. A higher yield on inflation-protected government debt can therefore raise the opportunity cost of holding it: investors have a stronger income alternative against which to weigh Bitcoin’s potential returns and volatility.

The 2.92% figure is also a long-term Treasury curve benchmark, not an overnight borrowing rate or a promised return for every investor. Treasury estimates its constant-maturity nominal and real par yields from indicative market quotations. An investor’s realized return on a particular bond can differ.

The November 16 bond rally lowered the nominal benchmark while the 10-year real yield reached 2.92%. Compare Monday’s small move with the rise since November 6.

Monday added one basis point to the real yield.

The real yield rose 29 basis points across multiple sessions.

The real-yield hurdle did not ease on Monday; its 29-basis-point rise spans November 6–16.

Curve estimates use indicative quotations. The calculated spread includes inflation risk and liquidity effects; it is not a pure forecast. The graphic establishes no Bitcoin price response. Comparisons are dated observations, not intraday moves.

Inputs are dated 10-year Treasury par yields. Spread = nominal minus real: 5.15−2.91=2.24 pp; 5.12−2.92=2.20 pp. Monday changes: real +1 bp, spread −4 bp, nominal −3 bp. Since Nov. 6: 2.92−2.63=0.29 pp=29 bp. Bars start at zero; both real-yield comparison views share a labelled 2.60%–3.00% scale.

Curve estimates use indicative quotations. The calculated spread includes inflation risk and liquidity effects; it is not a pure forecast. The graphic establishes no Bitcoin price response. Comparisons are dated observations, not intraday moves.

A simple subtraction of Treasury’s real par yield from its nominal par yield gives a spread of 2.20 percentage points on Monday, down from 2.24 on Friday. The four-basis-point narrowing explains how the nominal benchmark could fall while the real benchmark rose.

The spread is often described as inflation compensation. But the Federal Reserve’s explanation shows why it cannot be read as a pure inflation forecast: it also reflects compensation for inflation risk and differences in liquidity between nominal Treasuries and inflation-protected securities. The subtraction alone cannot show which component drove Monday’s change.

Historical research supports caution about the transmission to crypto. The authors of a 2023 IMF working paper found that U.S. monetary tightening reduced a broad crypto price factor through investor risk-taking. Their study does not estimate how Monday’s one-basis-point real-yield move affected Bitcoin.

Watch the inflation-adjusted Treasury yield for signs that competition from bonds is easing. The headline rate can fall while that yield stays elevated.

The firm has reported a massive write-down of its XRP holdings for Q3 2026 in a recent filing with the U.S. SEC, drawing attention from the broader crypto community.

Bitcoin ETFs Lose $2.1 Billion in November as Outflow Streak Hits Seven Days

Bitcoin ETF investors are still heading for the exits, and the price slide is speeding up.

U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs shed $185.4 million on Friday, according to Decrypt’s ETF tracker, extending their outflow streak to seven straight trading days.

The funds lost about $829 million this week after shedding nearly $1.3 billion the week before, bringing November’s outflows to roughly $2.1 billion.

The selling so far this month has been relentless. The funds bled $1.4 billion over five sessions earlier in the streak as investors cut risk. Cumulative net inflows since launch have slipped to $55.3 billion, and total net assets fell to $94.9 billion, below the $100 billion mark they held through much of October.

Other crypto funds haven’t escaped the rout. Ethereum ETFs took in a modest $2.3 million on Friday, snapping a six-day outflow streak, but still lost about $336 million on the week and roughly $702 million so far in November. Solana ETFs shed about $28 million this week, though they retain $1.6 billion in cumulative net inflows.

Spot crypto ETFs hold the underlying asset, such as Bitcoin or Ethereum, and trade on stock exchanges like ordinary shares, giving investors exposure through a regular brokerage account. Bitcoin funds have drawn $55.3 billion in net inflows since debuting in January 2024. Because issuers report flows daily and much of the money comes from institutions and financial advisers, the figures have become a closely watched gauge of investor sentiment.

The outflows have tracked a sharp sell-off. Bitcoin traded near $64,500 over the weekend, down 6% over 24 hours and about 15% on the week, according to CoinGecko, and it has lost more than 22% over the past month.

Ethereum fell 8.4% to about $1,790, and Solana dropped 12% to roughly $70. Total crypto market value slid 4.5% to about $2.3 trillion.

Bitcoin hit a 12-week low this week as fears of further Federal Reserve rate hikes erased its summer rally, and concentrated liquidations pushed the price below $69,000 earlier in the week.

Not every institution is retreating. JPMorgan’s holdings of Bitcoin and crypto-linked securities are approaching $1 billion, according to its most recent SEC filing. However, those purchases predate the more recent market downturn.

#5085. Tokenized stocks are exactly where stablecoins were in 2020 – How?

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Stablecoins started as a way to hold dollars on-chain. Then people started lending, trading, and earning with them. In time, DeFi grew up around them.

Tokenized stocks are heading down the same route now, and the resemblance is uncanny.

What’s worth noting here is how much of it is actually being used.

According to data from Token Terminal, tokenized stocks deposited into DeFi are now worth about $300 million. A year ago, that was at approx. $12.5 million.

This number didn’t move at all through late 2025. Things picked up in the spring, blasted through $100 million by around August, and then shot past a quarter million in September.

It’s also a bigger share of the market now. That $300 million is 6.3% of the $4.8 billion tokenized stock market cap. Last year, only 2.4% was being put to work.

Because once they’re there, holders can borrow against them, earn fees by providing liquidity, or chase yield.

And every new way to use them, is essentially one more reason not to cash out.

Tokenized stocks now have a market cap of about $4.8 billion. Stablecoins crossed that exact number in March 2020. Since then, stablecoins have grown, 59 times over! The number is at $285 billion now.

Tokenized stocks are moving fast too. They were worth only a few tens of millions in early 2025.

But why? A blockchain is basically a ledger for assets, and it gets more useful when it holds things people really want. U.S Stocks are about as in-demand as it gets.

Stablecoins didn’t get to where they are without the effort.

They made it through the COVID crash, the Terra collapse, FTX, and the USDC de-peg after SVB failed. Market cap fell by 28% between February 2022 and October 2023, then spiked up to a high by September 2024.

What kept them going though? Cash and T-bill reserves backed every token, plus people who actually wanted to use them.

Tokenized stocks follow the same 1:1 idea.

Except the risk factor is higher. So is the potential for gains.

#5084. Satoshi-Era Bitcoin Holder Wakes Up After 15 Years, Refuting $250 Billion ‘Lost’ Claim

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

A Bitcoin address that had not moved coins since February 2011 transferred 75 BTC on Nov. 17, according to Galaxy Research. At the current price of $64,935 per BTC, the coins are worth around $4.87 million.

The address is listed as a defendant in a $250 billion lawsuit in New York Supreme Court, in which the plaintiffs are trying to have early Bitcoin wallets declared "lost." Galaxy Research said that several other addresses from the same list have also moved coins in 2026.

According to Galaxy Research analysts, early on Nov. 17, in block 976,209, the address 1ALpEWtXApR19hLXvcG8NfkPyGsUMhFPMS sent coins for the first time. It was first funded on Feb. 8, 2011. At that time, Bitcoin was trading at about $0.90. After 15.8 years, the coins went to an unknown address with a 399-satoshi fee, or about 26 cents, for a paper gain of more than 7,000,000%.

Onchain databases list the wallet as Noah Doe #3409 (Salomon Client Dusted). That label is how it ended up among the defendants in the high-profile case ABC Company, XYZ Company, and Noah Doe v. John Does 1–39,069.

According to the lawsuit, an anonymous plaintiff using the pseudonym Noah Doe and two companies want the rights to 39,069 Bitcoin wallets that have not moved coins for more than five years. The plaintiffs said that these wallets hold about 3.8 million BTC, and at the current price, the coins are worth around $250 billion.

The plaintiffs filed the lawsuit under New York's lost-and-found law, Article 7-B of the Personal Property Law. Under the law, a person who finds lost property has to give it to the police, and the property goes to the finder if the owner does not claim it in time. In the lawsuit, the plaintiffs valued each address at less than $10, and they argued that the waiting period for such items was one year.

They "found" dormant addresses with an algorithm, saved them onto USB flash drives and took them to the New York Police Department (NYPD). Then they served the owners on the blockchain, through microtransactions with embedded messages (OP_RETURN). The practice is known as "dusting," and the campaign is tied to Salomon Brothers Strategic Advisors. Once the waiting period ran out, the plaintiffs asked the court to recognize the coins as theirs.

The case has been put on hold several times over motions to dismiss and amicus curiae briefs from outside experts. At a hearing on Sept. 8, the Bitcoin Policy Institute asked to intervene as a defendant, and The Digital Chamber asked to join the case as amicus curiae. Galaxy Research described it as a case "in which anonymous plaintiffs are seeking to claim legal title over Satoshi's coins."

Galaxy Research's Will Owens, who attended the hearing, said that The Digital Chamber's arguments were "well pleaded" and that "the judge sounded sympathetic to them."

Critics of the lawsuit argue that Article 7-B was written for things you can pick up and hand over for safekeeping. It wasn't written for blockchain records when the "finder" doesn't hold the private keys.

In September, Galaxy head of research Alex Thorn also said that the Lost and Found Property Law includes "protection from 'Noah Doe-style' dormancy attacks on legal ownership."

#5083. JPMorgan and DBS offer 24/7 dollar transfers… without stablecoins

Institutional clients can move dollars between the US and Singapore around the clock. One JPMorgan conversion step still pauses for three hours on Saturdays.

Singapore bank DBS and JPMorgan's blockchain business Kinexys have connected their blockchain-based dollar accounts in Singapore and the US, allowing institutional clients to transfer funds instantly around the clock as an alternative to stablecoins, according to DBS's Nov. 16 announcement.

The service gives corporate treasurers another way to move bank deposits beyond traditional banking hours.

Ant International is the first client of the commercially ready connection, which uses Partior to coordinate settlement between the banks' permissioned networks. The launch puts a practical service behind the interoperability framework the banks announced in November 2025.

The competitive opportunity for banks is to make deposits easier to move while preserving the customer relationship attached to them.

Stablecoins offer another way to transfer dollars digitally, but whether companies favor one route depends on where they can send the money, how they recover cash, and what the whole transaction costs.

A tokenized deposit represents a bank deposit, while USDC is issued by Circle affiliates and backed by cash and cash-equivalent reserves. Moving either on a blockchain does not make the customer's counterparty or redemption arrangements identical.

DBS says transfers within Kinexys's Blockchain Deposit Account network operate 24/7/365. However, moving money between those accounts and traditional demand-deposit accounts has a maintenance interruption every Saturday from 3 p.m. to 6 p.m. Eastern Time.

The banks did not disclose fees or how much cash Ant needs to keep on hand, so companies have no like-for-like cost comparison with stablecoin transfers.

Circle says standard banking-rail USDC redemptions can be free and typically settle in up to two business days, while qualifying Circle Platform and Circle Payments Network customers can obtain free near-real-time redemption.

Circle attributes fast redemption to cash pre-positioned at bank partners, which earns less than Treasuries, and its Mint product terms also make instant settlement conditional on participating banks offering it.

Direct Mint access is restricted to eligible institutions, while individuals obtain USDC through exchanges and wallets.

A Bank for International Settlements (BIS) working paper published Oct. 13 uses weekly US data from January 2019 through June 2025. Researchers Rashad Ahmed and Iñaki Aldasoro found that higher deposit rates were followed by slower stablecoin growth, and that the relationship was stronger for USDC than USDT.

The paper links a one-standard-deviation increase in deposit repricing to about 2.4 percentage points slower stablecoin growth over ten weeks. Stablecoin growth averaged 15% over a ten-week period in the sample.

The findings suggest that deposit pricing helps shape demand for stablecoins, but the authors say their analysis does not establish that stablecoin growth necessarily reduces total bank deposits or lending.

The DBS link is part of a wider effort to make bank money usable across more systems, though the products are distinct.

On Oct. 19, JPMorgan brought its JPM Coin deposit token to Ethereum, alongside Base, for institutional clients. That public-chain token is separate from the permissioned Blockchain Deposit Account network connected to DBS.

Using its internal data, JPMorgan reported more than $4 trillion in cumulative transactions and $7 billion in average daily volume across the broader Kinexys platform.

In September, DBS said it and Citi's New York office had completed a dollar payment via SWIFT Digital Ledger in minutes over a weekend.

Separately, The Clearing House selected Quant to support a tokenized-deposit network with connections to RTP and CHIPS. Participating institutions are expected to gain access in the first half of 2027.

A faster transfer inside one network only helps if the receiving bank can accept it.

The competition is consequently about more than whose blockchain runs all weekend. The decisive advantage for corporate cash would be money available where it is needed, with fewer costly balances waiting elsewhere.

#5081. Ripple-Backed Evernorth Takes Massive $505 Million XRP Hit

Ripple-backed XRP treasury company Evernorth Holdings has disclosed a significant decrease in the book value of its crypto asset holdings, with total impairment charges amounting to around $505 million before going public on Nasdaq.

Per an SEC filing on Nov. 13, the 346.3 million XRP tokens that Evernorth Holdings owned had a book value of just $341.9 million as of Sept. 30, 2026.

This is compared to the firm's initial accounting of the crypto assets at roughly $846.6 million, with an average acquisition or contribution price of $2.44 per XRP. This represents a nearly 60% decline in book value.

As per the latest filing, Evernorth recorded around $271 million in XRP impairment charges in the first nine months of 2026. This is in addition to the $233.7 million in impairment charges recorded in 2025.

Interestingly, only $6.9 million of the recent impairment charges occurred in the third quarter.

This also explains why Evernorth's XRP assets had a book value so far below their market value at the end of September.

Evernorth calculates impairments based on the lowest observable intraday price of XRP for each batch of tokens acquired. Once the assets are written down, the company is unable to reverse the previous losses if the price rises.

For reference, on Sept. 30, XRP closed the day trading at around $1.49 on Coinbase. At that price, Evernorth's 346.3 million XRP tokens would have been worth roughly $516 million, much higher than the disclosed book value of $341.9 million.

The recent disclosure comes around a month after Evernorth completed its business combination with Armada Acquisition Corp. II on Oct. 9. It started trading on Nasdaq under the symbol XRPN on Oct. 12.

Following the business combination, Evernorth held around 473 million XRP tokens and had also raised gross cash proceeds of around $300 million.

Its investors include Ripple, SBI Group, Arrington Capital, Pantera Capital, Kraken and GSR.

On Oct. 14, Evernorth announced collaborations with Ripple, Kraken, Coinbase and BitGo to implement institutional investment strategies starting in Q4 2026.

Meanwhile, its latest SEC filing did not include details on the amount of XRP deployed to generate yield or the returns generated from it.

#5080. How Wall Street Is Racing to Claim the Onchain Dollar

How Wall Street Is Racing to Claim the Onchain Dollar

For years, stablecoins were crypto’s business, dominated by Tether and Circle and used mostly to trade digital assets. That era is ending.

Over the past few months, and especially the past few weeks, the biggest names in traditional finance have moved en masse to claim a piece of the onchain dollar. Here’s the map of a land grab that’s accelerating fast.

The clearest signal came this week, when Goldman Sachs, Citigroup, Santander, and Deutsche Bank were reported to be among banks committing roughly $270 million to a joint dollar-stablecoin venture codenamed “Project Faro.”

The capital funds five years of operations for a U.S.-incorporated issuer targeting a 2027 launch, part of a 21-institution coalition that has swelled from an initial group of 10. The goal is explicit: challenge Tether and Circle, which together still control about 85% of a roughly $290 billion market.

While the consortium plans, SoFi has shipped. Earlier this week, its SoFiUSD, billed as the first stablecoin from a U.S. national bank, expanded onto Polygon this month alongside Ethereum and Solana, reaching the bank’s 17 million-plus members.

SoFi also launched SoFi Earn, a tokenized deposit paying 4.40% yield that converts into SoFiUSD, and already settles its entire card program over Mastercard’s network.

Meanwhile, the federally chartered crypto bank Anchorage Digital has positioned itself as a settlement backbone.

Anchorage’s tokens can now settle transactions on Visa’s network, starting with Western Union’s USDPT, and it recently anchored both sides of the U.S.-Mexico stablecoin corridor, custodying tokens across one of the world’s largest remittance routes.

Mastercard has been the most aggressive incumbent among payment giants.

It launched a Global Money Platform with acquired stablecoin firm BVNK, rolled out a Stablecoin Security tool to screen payments for fraud, and unveiled Agent Pay for Machines, letting AI agents transact in stablecoins.

Visa, meanwhile, has expanded its own stablecoin settlement program across multiple blockchains.

The crypto-native incumbents, not to be left out, are also racing to entrench.

Circle struck a deal to bring USDC settlement to banking rails in Latin America, starting with a Colombia pilot, part of a broader expansion into dozens of emerging markets.

The catalyst is regulation. The GENIUS Act, signed in 2025, created the first U.S. framework for dollar-pegged tokens, giving regulated institutions a clear path into a business they had largely watched from the sidelines.

The Donald Trump administration has framed dollar stablecoins as a tool to extend the dollar’s reach and global hegemony, and banks that once dismissed crypto now see a settlement business they can’t afford to cede.

Much of this remains early. Project Faro has no token, no CEO, and no final name. Tether and Circle still dominate, and bank-issued tokens face a steep climb to win share.

But the direction is unmistakable: the onchain dollar, once crypto’s turf, is now contested ground, with Wall Street, the card networks and the stablecoin incumbents all racing to own the rails money moves on.

#5079. Hyperliquid’s $3.4 billion HYPE burn machine faces new test as USDC reserves shrink

Trading volume surged nearly 16%, but a 7.8% decline in USDC supply raises questions about the sustainability of a second source of HYPE buyback funding.

Hyperliquid's perpetual futures trading volume surged nearly 16% over the past week, even as the network's USDC holdings declined, exposing competing trends in the revenue streams supporting HYPE token buybacks.

Data from DeFiLlama showed that the decentralized exchange processed $75.72 billion in perpetual futures transactions over the seven days ending Nov. 16, an increase of 15.87%.

However, USDC capitalization on the network fell 7.76% to $6.02 billion during the same period. The stablecoin accounted for approximately 98.85% of Hyperliquid's total stablecoin supply.

The divergence puts Hyperliquid's token-buyback model in focus, particularly as the protocol increasingly relies on both trading fees and income generated from stablecoin reserves to support HYPE purchases.

Hyperliquid has already accumulated about 49.03 million HYPE tokens through its Assistance Fund, representing 4.9% of the cryptocurrency's initial one-billion-token supply.

Under the protocol's rules, the holdings were treated as burned and were valued at about $3.37 billion as of Nov. 17. Their estimated acquisition cost was $1.45 billion, with an average purchase price near $29.50 per token.

Hyperliquid's fee structure directs trading revenue toward liquidity providers, market deployers, and the Assistance Fund, which automatically converts its allocated fees into HYPE.

Weekly turnover rose while chain-wide USDC fell. The routes to HYPE purchases depend on different balances, rates and timing.

Actual fee receipts and reserve payments are the comparable inputs for assessing HYPE funding.

Chain-wide USDC is not the qualifying treasury balance. The retained sources do not reconcile current eligible daily balances, effective AQA rates or November payments. The timeline shows the recurring rule, not the implementation grace period or a specific payment.

Uses the November 16 reported seven-day dashboard figures and the documented fee and AQAv2 mechanisms. Backing split drawn 9:1; schedule lengths drawn 30:8. Route widths do not encode dollars; no income estimate or current rate is calculated.

Chain-wide USDC is not the qualifying treasury balance. The retained sources do not reconcile current eligible daily balances, effective AQA rates or November payments. The timeline shows the recurring rule, not the implementation grace period or a specific payment.

The accumulated tokens are permanently removed from the circulating supply, linking exchange activity directly to demand for its native cryptocurrency.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

This mechanism has become an important component of HYPE's token economics, with the protocol using revenue from its trading business to steadily accumulate tokens.

The latest increase in perpetuals volume strengthens one side of that arrangement by expanding the trading activity from which fees are collected.

However, the amount reaching the Assistance Fund also depends on transaction fee tiers, staking discounts, maker rebates and revenue-sharing arrangements with market deployers.

Hyperliquid has also expanded its funding model beyond trading fees through AQAv2, an arrangement that redirects income earned on eligible USDC reserves toward the protocol.

Under the framework, Hyperliquid receives approximately 90% of cost-adjusted reserve yield, providing another source of funds for HYPE purchases.

Coinbase serves as the treasury operator, while Circle provides technical support for the stablecoin infrastructure.

Unlike trading fees, which depend on transaction activity, this income is tied to qualifying USDC balances and prevailing interest rates.

That creates a potential vulnerability as stablecoin capitalization declines, even while activity on the exchange strengthens.

The importance of Hyperliquid's stablecoin reserves became more apparent in the third quarter, when the network began generating substantial income through the AQAv2 arrangement.

According to Bitwise and Allium, the protocol accrued approximately $17.26 million in USDC reserve income during the quarter, including $14.86 million in September.

At the end of September, Hyperliquid held approximately $7.35 billion in total USDC, with $6.77 billion backing HyperCore, its trading infrastructure. Of that amount, the treasury held approximately $6.09 billion.

Using those balances and a 3.22% annual reference rate, Bitwise estimated the arrangement could generate about $16.09 million over 30 days, or nearly $196 million annually.

That potential income adds another source of demand for HYPE, reinforcing the buybacks already funded by trading fees.

The latest contraction in USDC capitalization threatens to weaken that contribution if the decline extends to the qualifying reserves on which the income is earned.

The reserve arrangement calculates revenue from daily balances and interest rates over 30-day periods, with payments reaching the Assistance Fund eight days after each period ends.

Consequently, shrinking eligible reserves would gradually reduce the income available for HYPE purchases, while stronger trading activity could help compensate through higher fee collections.

For Hyperliquid, the emerging tension is between an increasingly active derivatives business and a contracting stablecoin base that supports a separate source of buyback funding.

The next reserve-income distribution will provide a clearer picture of whether Hyperliquid can sustain this additional source of HYPE demand as USDC balances fall.

Oluwapelumi values Bitcoin's potential. He imparts insights on a range of topics like DeFi, hacks, mining and culture, underlining transformative power.

#5078. Elon Musk Joins 160,000 Backing Petition to Bring Jensen Huang Back to Nvidia

Elon Musk Joins 160,000 Backing Petition to Bring Jensen Huang Back to Nvidia

Elon Musk has thrown his support behind a campaign to reinstate Jensen Huang as Nvidia's CEO, as the ousted chipmaker co-founder reportedly weighs a comeback just days after resigning.

"A petition is underway to reinstate Jensen and I'm signing it too," the Tesla and SpaceX CEO tweeted on Friday. Musk had previously said it would be "an honor" to hire Huang.

A petition is underway to reinstate Jensen and I’m signing it too

— Elon Musk (@elonmusk) November 13, 2026

The "Stand with Jensen" open letter calls on Nvidia's board to bring Huang back while an independent investigation establishes the facts. It has drawn more than 160,000 signatures, including Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney and Y Combinator CEO Garry Tan, CNBC reported, though the site notes that signatories' affiliations are self-reported and unverified. More than 7,500 signatories identified themselves as Nvidia employees, The Guardian reported, a figure it said was equivalent to 18% of the company's workforce.

The campaign follows internal calls from Nvidia staff for Huang's return.

Huang has discussed the possibility of returning with confidants, the Financial Times reported, citing multiple people familiar with the matter. One ally told the newspaper they were assembling a "war room" of Silicon Valley executives to lobby for his reinstatement and would seek support from the White House, adding that the effort had Huang's backing.

Even supporters acknowledged the obstacles. "Having an affair with a subordinate is a fireable offence," one person involved in the effort told the FT.

Two major Nvidia shareholders, Altimeter Capital's Brad Gerstner and Atreides Management's Gavin Baker, urged the board in an open letter on Friday to leave "all options on the table, including the potential return of Jensen Huang."

Huang resigned as Nvidia's president, CEO and board member on Thursday, capping a week of reports of affairs with Alice Huang, a former executive at Nvidia customer Megaspeed whom U.S. officials suspect of spying for China, and with an Nvidia executive who reported to him. Chief financial officer Colette Kress is serving as interim CEO.

The campaign faces resistance in Washington. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said on Sunday that Nvidia should look outside the company for its next CEO. "They need a dramatic shake-up," he told Fox News.

Bessent said on Sunday Morning Futures that questions over Nvidia's compliance with U.S. export restrictions were the bigger issue, and that the company should be judged on whether it had "intentionally ignored the warning signs" of shell companies in Singapore. He said he wanted answers "in the next 10 days from the board and from the outside counsel." Huang also faces congressional inquiries into Nvidia's chip exports to China, as well as a proposed shareholder class action.

Nvidia's board has approached Arm CEO Rene Haas as a potential successor, the FT has reported. "We are in the process of selecting a search firm for the NVIDIA CEO role. We have not formally engaged any potential candidates," an Nvidia spokesperson told CNBC.

Analysts are divided on Huang's chances. "We see no chance Jensen comes back to Nvidia as CEO," Dan Ives of Yorkville Ives & Co. said in a note cited by CNBC, while Seaport Research Partners' Jay Goldberg said Huang could stage "a second act" if he gets through his legal troubles. Former White House communications director Anthony Scaramucci was blunter: "The FBI doesn't take petitions," he tweeted.

Nvidia reports quarterly earnings on Tuesday, the first under Kress.

#5077. HKMA, Swift explore tokenized deposits for cross-border bank payments

HKMA and Swift will explore linking Project Ensemble with Swift’s ledger for cross-border payments using tokenized deposits in multiple currencies.

The Hong Kong Monetary Authority (HKMA) and Swift will explore connecting their payment infrastructure to support cross-border transfers using tokenized bank deposits, the authority said Monday.

The work will examine links between Swift’s blockchain-based ledger and the settlement infrastructure of Project Ensemble. Participating banks include Bank of China (Hong Kong), Standard Chartered Bank (Hong Kong) and HSBC, according to the announcement.

Tokenized deposits are bank balances represented on a blockchain. They remain claims on the issuing commercial bank, unlike central bank digital currencies, which are claims on a central bank. The proposed Swift connection would explore how separate payment systems could work together across borders in multiple currencies.

The collaboration follows a Nov. 2 upgrade to EnsembleTX, the pilot phase of Project Ensemble, enabling banks to settle tokenized deposit transactions around the clock using tokenized central bank money. The pilot launched in November 2025 and initially relied on Hong Kong’s dollar-denominated real-time gross settlement system.

In July, Swift said its blockchain-based ledger was ready for initial use and that it was preparing to pilot tokenized cross-border payments with 17 major banks, including Citi, DBS and HSBC.

#5075. Binance Pay Later offers credit backed by Bitcoin, Ether and BNB holdings

Eligible users can access up to 1,000 USDC in credit against their Bitcoin, Ether or BNB for purchases via Binance Card or QR Pay.

Binance said Monday it would offer eligible users in Argentina and Mexico up to 1,000 USDC in spending credit through Pay Later, a payment feature backed by their cryptocurrency holdings.

Pay Later allows eligible users to access up to 1,000 USDC (USDC) in spending power against their Bitcoin (BTC), Ether (ETH) and BNB (BNB) token holdings as collateral, without selling the underlying assets, Binance said in an announcement shared with Cointelegraph. Borrowing is limited to 50% of the eligible collateral’s value, meaning $2,000 in BTC is required to access a maximum $1,000 credit line.

Purchases are interest-free until the repayment deadline on the first of the following month. Binance’s published FAQ lists a one-day grace period and interest of 0.1% per day, compounding daily, on overdue balances.

Borrowing lets users make purchases without first selling their crypto, but falling prices can put those holdings at risk. Binance can liquidate collateral when the outstanding loan reaches 90% of its value, even before repayment is due. It will initially launch for users in Argentina and Mexico, with a gradual rollout planned for additional markets.

“Traditional consumer credit often relies on income documentation and credit history, which leaves people who hold assets but have no formal credit file with fewer options,” said Thomas Gregory, VP of payments and fiat at Binance, adding that asset-backed credit offers an alternative.

#5074. Vitalik Buterin Now ‘a Total Bull’ on leanPCS as Fast Track to Quantum-Safe Ethereum

Vitalik Buterin Now ‘a Total Bull’ on leanPCS as Fast Track to Quantum-Safe Ethereum

Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin has thrown his weight behind a proposal he says could make the network quantum-safe much faster than expected.

"Over the past few weeks I've gone from thinking leanPCS is total bull to being a total bull," Buterin tweeted on Monday, in a lengthy explainer on how Ethereum could handle the much larger signatures and proofs that quantum-resistant cryptography requires.

Over the past few weeks I've gone from thinking leanPCS is total bull to being a total bull. So let me explain some of these buzzwords in more detail.

At the core, 8288 is about hyper-scaling data and execution for any computation that needs to get performed on a piece of data…

— vitalik.eth (@VitalikButerin) November 16, 2026

In short, leanPCS is a proposed way for Ethereum to bundle many large, quantum-safe signatures and proofs into a single compact proof, so that the full data never has to be stored onchain. It is built on polynomial commitment schemes, or PCS, the core mathematical building block of STARK proofs.

Quantum computers are expected to eventually break the elliptic curve cryptography that secures Ethereum and Bitcoin accounts today. The quantum-safe replacements are far bulkier: Buterin said signatures would grow from 65 bytes to between 1 and 6 kilobytes, while zero-knowledge proofs would balloon from a few hundred bytes to around 128 kilobytes. "This is unsustainable to put on chain," he wrote.

Buterin's answer is EIP-8288, a draft proposal he co-authored with developer Thomas Coratger in June, which would aggregate those signatures and proofs before they ever reach a block.

Under the design, users publish their signatures or proofs, and network nodes repeatedly bundle everything they have seen into a single STARK, a type of zero-knowledge proof, that attests the underlying data is valid. These aggregate proofs are themselves combined until a single "master proof" verifies everything in a block. Only short claims, rather than the full signatures or proofs, would need to be stored onchain.

He laid out three ways to build it, depending on what users submit. The simplest, leanSPHINCS, would support a single type of quantum-safe, hash-based signature, costing users only a small amount of gas per signature, but it couldn't do anything else.

Buterin's preferred option, leanPCS, offers a third route. It would offer the same generality as leanSTARK but "could potentially be implemented very quickly," he said, because far fewer design choices are needed, and leanSTARK could then be added later.

"So there is a route to getting 'the cryptographic proof singularity' much faster than any of us expected," Buterin wrote, "and that route runs through 8288 with leanPCS."

Buterin linked to SPHINCS-, a family of hash-based signature schemes optimized for Ethereum, which was published on the Ethereum Research forum in June with input from Buterin and Ethereum Foundation cryptographers. The post shows that a standards-aligned quantum-safe signature can already be verified on Ethereum for about 150,000 gas, without any protocol change, compared with about 3,000 gas for an ECDSA signature today, according to an earlier Buterin estimate.

Under protocol-level aggregation, a future "leanSPHINCS" verification would cost about 3,000 gas, according to the post.

#5062. German Lawmakers Back Customs Powers to Seize Crypto of 'Unclear Origin'

German Lawmakers Back Customs Powers to Seize Crypto of 'Unclear Origin'

Germany's parliament has approved sweeping new powers for the country's customs agency, allowing it to seize assets, including crypto, whose lawful origin is in doubt, even when no criminal investigation is underway.

The Bundestag passed the Customs Financial Justice Act, or Zollfinanzgerechtigkeitsgesetz, on Thursday, November 12, the German government said. Under the law, customs can more easily seize assets where there are "justified doubts" about their lawful origin, an instrument the government describes as a "suspicious wealth order." Customs will also be able to better monitor digital cross-border payment routes and secure crypto assets.

"With this, we are sending a clear signal to criminals: we are getting tougher," German Finance Minister Lars Klingbeil said, according to the government.

The powers apply to "significant" assets, which typically means those worth more than €100,000, according to the government's bill. Because owners of such assets are usually able to explain where they came from, while the state struggles to prove an illegal origin, the government argued that it is reasonable to ask owners to disclose that information. "Almost every law-abiding citizen will be able to explain where these assets came from," the bill's explanatory notes say, as summarized by the Bundestag's news service.

Klingbeil has said customs could hold assets for up to 180 days without a conviction, during which owners would have to show they were obtained legally. "The Porsche and the Rolex are then gone for now," he said, according to the Berliner Zeitung. Any permanent confiscation would require a court ruling. Following an amendment by the governing coalition, that decision will be made by a panel of finance court judges rather than a single judge.

The law also reorganizes Germany's customs administration, strengthens its Financial Intelligence Unit and gives customs a legal basis to use technologies such as artificial intelligence. It forms part of an action plan against organized crime that the government adopted on February 25, 2026.

Germany has seized large amounts of crypto before, and has been quick to sell it. In 2024, authorities in the state of Saxony began selling the nearly 50,000 BTC they had seized from operators of the movie piracy website Movie2K, moving hundreds of millions of dollars' worth at a time as the sell-off continued. Blockchain analytics firm Arkham later said authorities had failed to seize a further 45,000 BTC linked to the site. The new law would give customs a way to seize crypto of unclear origin without first having to tie it to a specific crime.

German authorities have also gone after laundering infrastructure. Last year, they joined Swiss police in shutting down Cryptomixer, a mixing service that had laundered more than $1.4 billion in Bitcoin since 2016. Separately, the finance ministry has drafted a 25% tax on crypto gains from 2027.

The Bundestag's finance committee backed the bill on Wednesday with votes from the governing CDU/CSU and SPD, as well as the far-right AfD. The Left Party voted against, and the Greens abstained.

The Greens said Germany's money laundering problem is estimated at €100 billion, and that the government's expectation of only €308 million in additional revenue showed the gap between its ambitions and the law. The Left Party said the confiscation process was "built for the cash courier, not the banker."

The law still needs to pass the Bundesrat, the upper house of parliament that represents Germany's states.

#5055. Senate Agriculture Committee to Hear Trump’s CFTC Nominees Tuesday

The Senate Agriculture Committee will hear President Donald Trump’s two nominees to the Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) on Tuesday, a confirmation hearing that comes as the derivatives regulator has only one sitting commissioner.

The committee scheduled the session for 3 p.m. Eastern in Room 106 of the Dirksen Senate Office Building. The White House sent the nominations to the Senate on Nov. 12. The nominees are Nathan Anonick of Virginia and Matthew Rowland of the District of Columbia. The hearing notice says to check back for live video, and the committee has not posted written testimony.

Trump nominated the pair last week as part of a bipartisan slate for the CFTC and the Securities and Exchange Commission, a step that would begin to refill two regulators that have been operating short-handed.

Anonick serves on the Republican staff of the Senate Agriculture Committee under Chairman John Boozman of Arkansas. He was nominated to the seat once held by former Chairman Rostin Behnam, for a term ending June 19, 2031. A 2023 committee release said Anonick joined the panel in 2022 to manage a portfolio that includes cryptocurrencies, futures markets and derivatives, and that he had previously worked at the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation as a resolution policy specialist.

Rowland is a CFTC attorney detailed to the committee’s Democratic staff under Ranking Member Amy Klobuchar of Minnesota. He would fill the remainder of former Commissioner Summer K. Mersinger’s term, which expires April 13, 2028. Both nominees come from the committee that oversees the CFTC.

CFTC Chairman Michael Selig called them “two strong bipartisan picks” and said he looks forward to working with them “upon their confirmation by the US Senate.”

Kristin N. Johnson and Steven Levine, the two SEC nominees from the same White House list, are on the agenda for a Senate Banking Committee nominations hearing at 10 a.m. Thursday in Dirksen 538.

The CFTC can seat up to five commissioners, with no more than three from the same party. Chairman Michael S. Selig, sworn in Dec. 22, 2025, is currently the only sitting commissioner listed on the agency’s website. Even if the Senate confirms both nominees, two of the five seats would remain vacant.

Each nominee still needs a committee vote and then confirmation by the full Senate. The Agriculture Committee has not scheduled that vote.

The hearing lands in a lame-duck Senate, weeks after Democrats swept both chambers of Congress in the Nov. 3 midterms. The new Congress is seated in January. If the Senate does not confirm the nominations before then, they would typically be returned to the president and have to be sent again.

After the election, TD Cowen’s Jaret Seiberg told clients he does not expect Democrats to confirm Trump nominees unless the president fills Democratic seats at agencies such as the FDIC and the SEC, a step he has called unlikely. The White House slate does that at both the SEC and the CFTC.

The nominations also arrive with crypto market structure legislation stalled. The Clarity Act failed a Senate cloture vote 49-50 on Sept. 15 and dies on Jan. 3 unless the lame-duck session revives it. The bill would formally divide oversight of digital assets between the SEC and the CFTC, distinguishing which are securities, commodities or stablecoins.

Without new law, the CFTC has set much of its crypto policy on its own this year. Selig’s agency opened the door to equity perpetual futures, and a federal judge later said the CFTC likely overstepped its authority while letting trading continue.

The open question is whether a Senate heading for Democratic control confirms the CFTC picks before January, or leaves the agency short-handed into 2027.

#5051. Banks are banning AI agents over security... and competition concerns

AI agents are set to automate everyday banking tasks — but banks aren’t ready to give up control of the customer relationship just yet.

Banks in the United Kingdom have drawn a line over how much access artificial intelligence should have to their customers’ money.

Four banks, including HSBC, Barclays, Lloyds, NatWest and Nationwide, have all moved to block or restrict third-party AI agents from accessing customer accounts, especially where they can make transactions without customer involvement.

There’s no evidence yet of AI agents accessing UK bank accounts just yet, but large lenders are worried about what happens if an automated system mismanages customers’ money — and who would be on the hook.

The banks might have other, unspoken concerns too.

Edgars Nemše, chief executive of GenLayer Foundation, an AI-focused blockchain infrastructure project, says routine banking tasks like paying bills, moving money and finding better rates could all be handed to agents.

Unfortunately for banks, this means agents would be able to seamlessly find customers a better deal elsewhere as soon as it’s published on the web.

“For plain accounts, that removes the switching costs and makes the space even more competitive,” Nemše tells Magazine.

That scenario could threaten more than just customer loyalty. In September, Apollo chief economist Torsten Slok warned about a possible “agentic bank run.”

If swarms of agents start moving household cash from low-yield checking accounts to alternatives offering higher returns en masse, that would drain banks of the low-cost funding they rely on to make loans. And the speed and scale “would be a problem for the entire financial system.”

Related: Coinbase CEO touts agentic finance as Base tops 100M AI payments

JPMorgan Chase chief executive Jamie Dimon has also warned against allowing external agents unrestricted access to bank accounts. “You don’t want rogue agents,” he said on an October earnings call.

Is an agentic bank run coming? Source: Apollo

Regardless of short-term moves to hold back the tide, AI agents look more and more likely to revolutionize banking within a few years.

Neeraj Makin, group head of strategy analytics at Emirates NBD, predicts agents will usher in a new era of around-the-clock banking and near-instant payments as normal by 2036.

“Forget about waiting for a payment for 24 hours or inter-country payments taking three days… all of that will be in seconds, atomic,” he says, with AI agents taking over most financial decisions.

Ferdinand Dabitz, chief executive of Augustus, an AI-native banking and payments company, sees a similar shift in how customers interact with financial institutions.

“Every consumer will have 10s and every business will have 100s of agents talking to their bank,” Dabitz says.

A human interface will still remain important for managing those agents and providing a layer of trust, “especially in commercial banking,” he says.

Related: Agents can trade 24/7 — but Yat Siu warns you could lose access at any time

Emirates NBD’s developer experience team argued in July that financial institutions should build their own frameworks for agentic AI, rather than allowing individual teams to develop agents without common controls.

Its Leap framework is designed to govern how agents authenticate with core banking systems, access sensitive data and carry out actions, with audit trails, monitoring and human oversight built in.

Emirates NBD says banks should build their own agentic frameworks. Source: Emirates NBD

However there are still major concerns around the reliability of agents to make major financial decisions on their own.

A recent Bank of England and FCA survey found that while 80% of responding firms had deployed AI agents, just 2% used agents with broad autonomy, identifying data security, third-party dependencies and risks like hallucinations among their biggest concerns.

The AI takeover likely won’t happen overnight; “it will come in steps,” Nemše says.

“Today I use an agent to review my own and my company’s finances every month and queue the payments, and I approve them. The next step is the natural one: once you trust it, you let it spend small amounts without asking.”

Routine bills, subscriptions and the agent’s own running costs would be among the first tasks to become autonomous, he says.

Dabitz says corporate treasury management could be an area delegated to agents, but says banks will need to be involved in developing these early applications.

While consumers may enjoy the upside of automating their finances, who’s on the hook when an agent makes a mistake?

Nemše says banks need to offer “delegated access as a normal feature” to avoid customers sharing logins with agents. Customers could set permissions and spending limits, and agents could make low-value transactions independently while requiring approval for larger amounts.

But existing dispute processes may struggle if agents begin making thousands of transactions on behalf of each customer. “Even a small share going wrong is a lot of disputes,” he says. “The processes for handling them were built for people making a few payments a week. That part still has to be built.”

The US Office of the Comptroller of the Currency issued a supervisory framework for banks’ use of what it calls “super intelligence,” including agentic systems, on Nov. 4.

OCC’s Super Intelligence: Supervisory Framework. Source: OCC

The framework is meant to help bank examiners assess how institutions use the technology, and does not introduce new binding requirements for banks. Scrutiny should reflect the potential consequences of an agent’s actions, its level of autonomy and the degree of human oversight.

So an AI system helping a bank employee draft a report would present a different risk from one authorized to move customer funds without approval, and the banks that get that balance right are likely to come out on top.

#5050. Ledger Brings Clear Signing to Solana, Ending 'Blind' Signing for Jupiter Swaps

Ledger Brings Clear Signing to Solana, Ending 'Blind' Signing for Jupiter Swaps

Ledger has brought clear signing to Solana, allowing users of its hardware wallets to read exactly what they are approving before they sign a transaction.

"Until now, most Solana DeFi on a Ledger meant signing blind," the company tweeted on Monday. Users can now verify a swap on decentralized exchange aggregator Jupiter "in plain words, showing what you send and what you get before you approve," it said.

Would you approve a swap you can’t read?Clear Signing is finally live on Solana. Until now, most Solana DeFi on a Ledger meant signing blind. Now you can verify a Jupiter swap, in plain words, showing what you send and what you get before you approve.

Clear Signing now covers… pic.twitter.com/BuW3m7y5GG

Blind signing, in which users approve transactions displayed as unreadable data, has long been a weak point for hardware wallet users, since it leaves them unable to spot a malicious request. Ian Rogers, Ledger's chief human agency officer, likened it to "signing a blank check" in a presentation at Solana's Breakpoint conference.

A Jupiter swap that previously meant scrolling through 13 hard-to-read screens on a Ledger device now takes three, showing what the user is giving, what they're getting and who they're dealing with, Rogers said. "It's a huge step for every Solana user," he added.

Clear signing now covers nine Solana apps and 18 of the 22 most common actions users take on the network, according to Ledger. It was built with the Solana Foundation and Anza, the developer of Solana's Agave validator client, with Jupiter and lending protocol Kamino as the first supported apps.

Rogers also pitched the company's tools for AI agents. Ledger's approach is that an agent "never holds the keys; you do," he said, comparing it to a teenager who has to ask parents for the family car. The agent requests approval when it needs to move funds, and the user signs off on a Ledger device.

Because that means an agent has to stop and wait, Ledger last month launched Agent Intents, a single queue where approval requests collect so agents can keep working while users approve them when convenient. It works with Pay.sh, a service that lets AI agents pay for APIs on Solana, Rogers said.

He also showed off Ledger Atom, the touchscreen device based on Android's open-source code that Ledger announced last month. When it's time to sign, the device switches its screen to Ledger's secure chip, so secrets stay hidden from Android, Rogers said. Atom will be the first hardware beyond the Solana Seeker phone to carry the Seeker platform, bringing its more than 1,500 apps to the device, he added.

Ledger has previously teamed up with MoonPay on tools to let users control AI agents' crypto spending with its hardware wallets.

The launch comes weeks after Ledger investigated a suspected $87 million drain of user funds, which it later said was tied in all confirmed cases to a reseller, Cryptobilis.
