#5048. Bitcoin Price Holds Near $65,000 as Nvidia Turmoil Drags Nasdaq Down 2.5%

Bitcoin price traded near $65,000 on Monday afternoon, according to Bitcoin Magazine Pro data, up more than 1% from where it sat at the time of this morning’s report. The world’s largest cryptocurrency held its ground even as a fresh round of selling in Nvidia dragged the Nasdaq down about 2.5%.

The session was choppy. Bitcoin started the day near $64,000 after a weekend plunge to $62,000, then jumped to about $65,600 shortly after the U.S. stock market opened. The bounce faded within the hour, and the bitcoin price slipped back toward $64,000 as tech stocks extended their losses. Buyers stepped in there, and bitcoin has spent most of the afternoon trading around $65,000.

Nvidia was again the heaviest weight on markets. CNBC reported shortly after noon that the chipmaker’s board is not considering bringing back Jensen Huang as CEO after he resigned last week. The board is instead focused on an investigation into whether Huang’s conduct compromised national security or company secrets, the report said. Earlier Monday, President Donald Trump said he would not mind seeing Huang return.

Nvidia shares fell to a fresh intraday low after the report and closed down about 4%, a day before the company reports quarterly results. U.S. chipmakers dropped more than 5%. The Nasdaq Composite slid about 2.5% and the S&P 500 lost nearly 2%, with investors also bracing for Anthropic’s IPO later this week.

Bitcoin did not follow stocks to their lows this time. As the Nasdaq sank to its worst levels of the day after the CNBC report, bitcoin stopped going down and held near $64,800. That marks a change from the past week, when the bitcoin price traded largely in step with chip stocks.

The macro backdrop offered no relief. Fed Chair Kevin Warsh, speaking at a San Francisco Fed conference, avoided any guidance on the near-term path of rates. He said artificial intelligence could lift productivity but “could bring substantial labor market disruption as some jobs are displaced, rather than complemented, by technology.”

His remarks did little to change expectations for a rate hike next month. Fed funds futures imply a quarter-point increase in December, and the dollar index touched its highest level since April 2025. A stronger dollar tends to weigh on bitcoin and gold, and gold fell to its lowest level since October 2025 earlier in the session. The 10-year Treasury yield eased to about 5.12%.

Bitcoin-linked stocks took a heavier hit than the coin itself. Strategy (MSTR) fell nearly 8% and Coinbase (COIN) dropped about 6%, while Strive (ASST), which bought 251 bitcoin last week, slid about 12%. Strategy also added 485 bitcoin last week, but it faces about $2.8 billion of potential index-fund selling when MSCI removes it at the Nov. 30 close, JPMorgan estimates. Spot bitcoin ETFs shed $185 million on Friday, their seventh straight day of outflows.

The next test arrives Tuesday after the bell, when Nvidia reports its fiscal third-quarter results. Analysts expect earnings growth of 90% on revenue of $109 billion. Minutes from the Fed’s October meeting follow on Wednesday, the same day Anthropic is expected to price its IPO.

For now, bitcoin has bounced twice from the $64,000 area on Monday. A move above this morning’s high near $65,600 would put Sunday’s bounce toward $66,000 back in view, while a break below today’s low near $63,800 would bring the weekend low around $62,000 back into play.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $65,000.

#5039. Lighter recovers $5M after $10M exploit linked to compromised API keys

Lighter restored withdrawals after recovering approximately $5 million in USDC, while blockchain analysts traced $10.4 million in funds moved during the exploit.

Update 20:40 UTC: This article has been updated with details of Lighter’s $5 million fund recovery, the restoration of withdrawals and the cause of the exploit.

Decentralized derivatives exchange Lighter said it had restored withdrawals and recovered approximately $5 million after an attacker exploited compromised trader API keys in an incident involving more than $10 million in withdrawals Monday.

Blockchain analytics firm Bubblemaps said about $10.4 million in USDC had been withdrawn from the platform. It tracked funds being swapped into Ether (ETH) and Wrapped Monero XMR1, a token traded on Hyperliquid.

Lighter initially said its protocol and smart contracts were unaffected. The exchange activated Guardian mode, temporarily pausing withdrawals and new pool creation while its Security Council worked to secure the platform.

The security incident follows a string of high-profile cryptocurrency exploits this year, including the $6.3 billion Binance hack on Oct. 29, the industry’s largest to date.

Lighter and Bubblemaps identified wallet ‘0xb3f’ as the attacker’s address. Blockchain data platform Bitquery separately traced 10,393,057 USDC leaving Lighter’s Ethereum contract at 10:11 am UTC. It said most of those funds were converted into Ether through CoW Swap within minutes, followed by a series of 160 ETH transfers to wallets linked to XMR1 purchases on Hyperliquid.

Related: DOJ moves to seize $1.2B from Binance hack, disrupts FaceStealer scam

In subsequent analysis, Bitquery identified a total of 1,920 ETH transferred through 12 wallets, each receiving 160 ETH (about $290,000) before routing the funds through LI.FI toward Hyperliquid.

The 12 Hyperliquid accounts purchased approximately $3.46 million in XMR1 tokens. Another 3,845 ETH, worth about $7 million, remained in the original wallet as of 11:55 UTC on Monday, according to Bitquery’s latest snapshot.

Bitquery also identified similar fund movements following a separate $6.45 million Safe wallet theft at 11:35 UTC on Monday, about 84 minutes after the Lighter withdrawal, as well as a Kava exploit two days earlier.

All three involved funds routed through LI.FI into XMR1 purchases on Hyperliquid, but Bitquery found no direct transfers linking either of the other incidents to Lighter. The firm cautioned that similar transaction routes alone are weak evidence of a common attacker.

In a postmortem published Monday at 18:20 UTC, Lighter detailed how the attacker obtained private API keys through a trader’s custom signing software, which reused cryptographic nonces, allowing the keys to be calculated from publicly available signatures.

The attacker used the compromised keys to create a public pool, transfer funds into it and withdraw them through an attacker-controlled account.

Lighter said its Guardian security mechanism temporarily paused withdrawals, allowing its Security Council to return approximately $5 million in USDC to the affected trader. The exchange said its protocol, smart contracts and official software development kits were unaffected.

Lighter has since required wallet signatures for transfers to public pools and made API-key signatures non-public. It also plans to introduce IP allowlists and configurable permissions for API keys.

#5037. Morning Minute: Crypto Hits Extreme Fear as Bitcoin Plunges

Morning Minute: Crypto Hits Extreme Fear as Bitcoin Plunges

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

😱 Crypto Hits Extreme Fear as Bitcoin Plunges

The Crypto Fear and Greed Index printed 10 on Sunday. A month ago it read 63, which is Greed. A week ago it was 28. It has been sliding every day since, and 10 is about as low as that gauge goes.

This weekend’s price action certainly was extremely fearful. Bitcoin touched roughly $62,000 before stabilizing in the mid-60s, down about a quarter from its early-October high and down something like 9% in three days. ETH fell under $1,850. Solana, XRP and dogecoin are all down more than 25% on the month. Bitcoin is now trading below its 200-week moving average, a level that has historically acted as the floor beneath every cycle, and it lost its 50-week average a week earlier.

It wasn’t just majors. Zcash fell to $800. Hype fell to $62. VVV went sub-$17. Nothing was spared.

The billion-dollar question of course—why is Bitcoin (and crypto) selling off so hard?

CZ addressed the question directly on Sunday, saying “No one knows for sure. Correlation does not prove causation.” But he did give potential reasons.

No one knows for sure. Correlation does not prove causation. Here are some potential reasons.

Money shifting to the Anthropic IPO: https://t.co/jYwVi4huAY

ETF outflows: U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs saw about $1.9 billion in outflows over seven trading… pic.twitter.com/XL2aB1ZL9W

— CZ 🔶 BNB (@cz_binance) November 15, 2026

The reasons he listed: money rotating into the Anthropic IPO, ETF outflows, a run of security incidents including the Binance hack itself plus drained iPhone-linked wallets, a Chrome exploit and a Microsoft outage, hot US inflation raising the odds of another Fed hike and strengthening the dollar, and political uncertainty after midterm results that point toward stricter crypto policy.

Those are all pretty valid. Some of those overhangs remove soon (Anthropic IPO), some will fade over time (political uncertainty), but the cyber attack threat will remain until Bitcoin and broader crypto can present a real solution. And if we are faced with a hiking cycle in 2027 due to inflation, that will certainly be a continued headwind.

So where does this leave us? Nvidia reports Tuesday with an interim CEO while its former one openly campaigns to return. The Fed meets December 9 with inflation running hot enough to put another hike in play. And Bitcoin is sitting under a technical level that has held for three cycles.

Extreme fear has marked bottoms before. It has also marked the middle of declines. Now we wait for something other than sentiment to turn.

#5033. Privacy on Solana is ‘non-negotiable’ for institutional adoption, Mert Mumtaz says

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The Helius CEO says financial firms need confidentiality onchain and argues Zcash’s rise could push bitcoin to improve.

Mumtaz, whose company builds Solana infrastructure, said confidential lending was a big example. Financial institutions need to be able to transact without exposing private information, and moving onchain shouldn't mean accepting an inferior product, he added.

"If you derive a store of value from first principles, Zcash looks very close to the ideal solution," he said.

"Within a year, I think crypto will actually be much more secure than the alternative," he said. "I recognize it’ll be painful a little bit, but fundamentally speaking, this is what we’re prepared for."

#5032. “We Need to Change Our Management”: Ricardo Salinas on Mexico’s Future, Bukele and Bitcoin

•       Mexico: Salinas called Mexico’s political class “despicable” and said “I’m working on that,” pointing to 2030, when the presidency is “up for grabs.”

•       El Salvador: “They’re growing very fast here,” he said, crediting “the changes that Bukele has put in place.”

•       United States: America’s welfare-warfare state is “alive and well,” with Washington “borrowing like a drunken sailor.”

•       Bitcoin: “The last 12 months have been hell for Bitcoiners,” but it’s not over, as long as you can “take the blow.”

•       Banking: Paraguay’s crisis exposed “the original sin of banking”: borrowing short and lending long.

In an interview with Ricardo Salinas Pliego, the Mexican billionaire behind Grupo Salinas and one of Bitcoin’s most outspoken advocates in Latin America, recorded at Bitcoin Histórico in El Salvador this week, we discovered his views on Mexico’s political future, Bukele’s El Salvador, America’s debt spiral, and why he believes Bitcoin’s toughest year changes nothing.

Speaking in San Salvador’s National Palace on You’re The Voice podcast, Salinas didn’t hold back.

Asked about the biggest challenges facing Mexico, Salinas started with the country’s potential. “We’re 130 million Mexicans,” he said, plus another 40 million of Mexican origin living in the United States, who send home “$5 billion a month in remittances. We call that love.” Half of Mexico’s population, he added, is under 30.

“We have a problem: the terrible management that we have, the terrible government that we have. The political class is despicable. They’re incapable. They’re corrupt. They lie all the time. So we need to change our management. And I’m working on that.”

Asked whether he intends to be part of that change, Salinas drew a line between two fronts. On “the battle of ideas, what we call the cultural battle,” he said, “I’m all in.” The electoral battle is different: “The electoral battle has timing, it has windows, and it has rules, and I don’t set them.”

He then laid out the calendar. In June 2027, Mexico elects its entire lower house of Congress and 18 of its 32 governors. In 2030, “we have everything up for grabs: the Congress, the Senate, the presidency, and the other 18 governors.”

“So it’s going to be interesting in ’30,” he said. “But from here to there, it’s a long time, huh? We’ll take it one day at a time.”

The night before the interview, Salinas joined a group of Bitcoin Histórico speakers at a dinner with President Nayib Bukele. In a lighthearted moment, Bukele poked fun at himself, recalling how he had rushed to make El Salvador the first country to adopt Bitcoin, convinced other nations were about to beat him to it. Looking back, he found it funny.

Ricardo Salinas and President Nayib Bukele | Credit: Casa Presidencial

Ricardo Salinas and President Nayib Bukele | Credit: Casa Presidencial

“And it didn’t happen, right? He was the only one that did it,” Salinas said. “So these things take time. There’s a lot of entrenched interest in the financial world and in government circles that do not want to see the end of fiat. Because fiat, what I call the fiat fraud, is what enables all the spending, right? And if you live off of that, you don’t want it to stop.”

Salinas pointed to El Salvador’s unusual position: its currency is the U.S. dollar, which it cannot print. “So Bukele was saying last night, ‘I’m not in charge of the inflation.'”

Salinas is bullish on the country’s direction. “They’re growing very fast here,” he said, adding that he expects El Salvador to “do much better, with the changes that Bukele has put in place.” As for the criticism the country faces, he was dismissive: “You have to take things from who they come from.”

Bitcoin Historico 2026 speakers at President Bukele’s Dinner | Credit: Casa Presidencial

In a 2022 interview with Max Keiser and Stacy Herbert, Salinas argued that fiat money has allowed the “big state” to exist, funding both the warfare state and the welfare state. Four years later, he says nothing has changed, and the United States is the prime example.

“The United States is the maximum example of the welfare-warfare state. The exceptionalism of the United States is that they get to print the reserve currency of the world.”

Citing the Cantillon effect, the Austrian School idea that newly created money reaches some people before others, Salinas said the first in line are “all these defense contractors and the warfare state,” followed by welfare programs such as Medicaid and hospitals.

“So I think the welfare-warfare state is alive and well,” he said. By his count, U.S. debt has climbed from around $25 trillion to $40 trillion over the past four years, “and there’s no end in sight. They’re not going to stop.”

He described Washington as “borrowing like a drunken sailor” and warned that rising interest payments will squeeze everything else. “The amount of interest that the US government is going to have to pay on this debt is huge, and it’s going to leave very little money for all the rest of the stuff, including defense and welfare.”

Salinas didn’t sugarcoat the past year. “The last 12 months have been hell for Bitcoiners,” he said. “That’s the title of my talk: ‘Is It Over for Bitcoin?'”

His answer is no. “But you have to be able to take the blow. And not many people are able to take the blow, because it’s much more psychological than financial.”

For Salinas, price swings don’t touch the core thesis. “Governments are borrowing much more than they should, they’re spending much more than they should, and that’s causing the debasement of the currency. And that’s stealing from people’s pockets. Period. And we’re all being victims of this.”

He also took aim at conventional portfolio advice. The traditional 60% stocks, 40% bonds split for young investors is, in his words, “really bad advice. It’s self-serving, by the financial industry.” That, he said, “is where investing in Bitcoin comes in.”

Ricardo Salinas speaking at Bitcoin Historico 2026, El Salvador | Credit: Bitcoin Historico

The interview came days after Paraguay’s central bank took over the digital lender Ueno Bank, freezing depositors’ access to their money, and the country’s Senate voted to remove President Santiago Peña from office over the crisis. Peña, who held shares in Ueno’s parent company until last year, has denied wrongdoing.

For Salinas, whose Grupo Salinas owns Banco Azteca, the collapse exposed a flaw built into every bank on earth. “The original sin of banking, is borrowing short and lending long.”

Depositors believe their money is available on demand, he explained, but the bank lends it out for car loans, mortgages and office buildings with longer maturities. “All the banks in the world, in every country, operate the same way. They borrow short and they lend long, so they always have a liquidity problem. Always.” The fix would be full reserve banking, he noted, “but there are no full reserve banks in the world today.”

He said Paraguay’s case combined that structural flaw with bad decisions, and that “the more serious accusation is that the owners of the bank were using the assets of the bank for their own purposes, outside of the regulatory framework.”

Salinas called Peña “a good leader,” while adding he doesn’t know how involved the president was. He warned of the political fallout: “All the left-wingers are celebrating the demise and the fall of Santiago Peña, and populism is increasing its calls for more government intervention, more spending.”

On reports that pension money was held at the bank, he was blunt about government promises, which he said “are usually worth nothing.” Pension pools, he added, are a tempting target: “Governments love to put their hands on other people’s money. It’s a great target. And I think that’s what happened in Paraguay.”

For Salinas, the next generations are the ones paying for their elders’ promises. Before Mexico’s 1997 pension reform, he said, the government was “taking taxes from young people to pay the pensions of the old people. That’s a really bad deal for young people.”

He sees it as a symptom of a deeper problem: “this wrong idea that the government is like a parent.” In Asia and Latin America, he said, “the family is the life raft of people. Because you cannot trust the government.” In Europe and the United States, “people have been made to believe that they can trust the government, and it’s wrong.”

A father of six and grandfather of ten, Salinas described his own pact simply: “I’m going to try to leave them a better place than the one I found.”

He made his point with a provocative analogy: “So what happens in a family where the mother is a prostitute and the father is a drunk? What kind of family is going to come out of that? Well, nothing good, right?” The West, he argued, has been “deceived into thinking that some bureaucrats are going to take care of these children. They’re not.”

“That is the treason of the intergenerational pact,” he said. “It’s very serious. And that manifests itself in taking on debt, wars, spending, student loans…” His answer for young people starts with education: “Find out what they’re doing to your future. Find out what they’re doing to your money.” Next comes what he calls “the tax bite.” By his estimate, the government takes about 47% of labor income in Mexico and 60% in France, versus just 5% in Guatemala. “Don’t let the government steal the product of your work.”

Ricardo Salinas and Efrat Fenigson at El Salvador’s National Palace | Credit: Bitcoin Historico

Salinas also revealed he is looking at investments in the Dominican Republic, which he called “a well-managed country” with a successful focus on tourism and agriculture. Its neighbor got a harsher verdict: “Haiti is a basket case for governance and democracy.”

But he sees hope coming from El Salvador. Bukele recently deployed more than 2,000 Salvadoran troops to Haiti, and Salinas welcomed the move, describing it as “sending in 2,000 troops to put down the gangs. I think it’s a great start in the right direction.”

The Dominican Republic’s big problem, he said, is energy, since it has to import it. One option he has explored is small nuclear power plants, though President Luis Abinader is wary of the impact on tourism. Salinas disagrees. “You can’t have tourism if you don’t have energy, right? You need a lot of energy.”

Asked what keeps him optimistic, Salinas described a cycle he believes drives prosperity: freedom leads to creativity, success gets copied, and copying leads to improvement. “Copy, improve. Copy, improve. And that is what leads to prosperity.”

The obstacle, as always in his view, is the state. “They cut down on freedom. They cut down on innovation through regulation. They cut down on competition through all these protectionist and crony laws, right? And then they take the product from people’s hands and mouths and spend it on stupid things.”

His prescription is simple: “It’s freedom, innovation, competition, prosperity. Small, very small government, for law and order and defense. That’s it.”

Libertarian values, an Austrian economist’s grasp of money, a Bitcoiner’s conviction and a patriarch’s devotion to family: Salinas has made his recipe clear. What Mexico will find out by 2030 is whether he intends to serve it himself.

#5030. 'Nobody Knows Who's in Charge': Boris Johnson Stands by Bitcoin Skepticism at Breakpoint

'Nobody Knows Who's in Charge': Boris Johnson Stands by Bitcoin Skepticism at Breakpoint

Former UK Prime Minister Boris Johnson is sticking to his doubts about Bitcoin, telling a room full of crypto developers that he still can't see who is in charge of the world's largest cryptocurrency.

"As far as I can see, nobody knows who's in charge, apart from Yakimoto Nakasori, and I don't know where to find him," Johnson said at Solana's Breakpoint conference in London, deliberately mangling the name of Bitcoin's pseudonymous creator, Satoshi Nakamoto. "If six billion got stolen from some cryptocurrency, I wouldn't know who to apply to, or how to get the money back. That's my problem."

Johnson was asked about a column he wrote in March for the Daily Mail, in which he called Bitcoin a "giant Ponzi scheme." The column drew pushback from the crypto industry, including Strategy executive chairman Michael Saylor, who tweeted that Bitcoin has no issuer, promoter or guaranteed return.

Bitcoin is not a Ponzi scheme. A Ponzi requires a central operator promising returns and paying early investors with funds from later ones. Bitcoin has no issuer, no promoter, and no guaranteed return—just an open, decentralized monetary network driven by code and market demand.

— Michael Saylor (@saylor) March 13, 2026

On stage, Johnson admitted he is "not a great crypto buff" and holds little, if any, crypto himself. His view was shaped by a man he met in his Oxfordshire village who had lost thousands and kept paying more in the hope of recovering his losses, he said. "If the search for the greater fool had reached the wilds of South Oxfordshire, then probably there was something not quite right about this thing," Johnson said.

He also questioned the name itself, noting that "crypto" comes from the Greek for "hidden." "I don't think it's a good sell to tell people that this thing is concealed," he said.

Johnson drew a sharp line between Bitcoin and the technology behind it. "Please don't think that my Luddite anxiety about crypto in any way means that I wish to slow down the development of blockchain, or the lead that London should rightly have in that sort of technology," he said. When it comes to blockchain and tokenizing assets, the UK has "got to be at the forefront," he added.

Moderator Gareth Jenkinson, head of multimedia at The Block, pushed back when Johnson suggested Bitcoin was valuable to drug dealers, arguing that blockchains are far more traceable than cash. Asked by Johnson who guarantees Bitcoin, Jenkinson replied: "No one has to guarantee it. That's the beauty of the protocol."

Lord Ross Kempsell, a Conservative member of the House of Lords who previously worked for Johnson, said more than 8% of UK adults now hold some form of crypto asset, and that institutional adoption of blockchains is growing in the City of London. That ownership figure has fallen, however: the Financial Conduct Authority's latest consumer research, conducted in August and September 2025, found 8% of UK adults own crypto, down from 12% in 2024, though still double the level in 2021.

The UK is "on the precipice of creating a lot of regulation" for the sector, Kempsell said, pointing to the FCA's work on its regulatory perimeter and the Bank of England's coming stablecoin framework. "I share Boris's anxieties about the risks," he said, but within that perimeter, "there's a lot of scope here for a high growth industry."

In June, the FCA finalized its rulebook for crypto firms ahead of a mandatory regime due to take effect in late 2027, days after the Bank of England eased its stablecoin rules, scrapping planned individual holding caps in favor of a £40 billion issuance limit per coin. The Bank has since been handed a legal duty to foster stablecoin innovation, and it has set out a shared vision for tokenization with the FCA.

Kempsell also argued that being outside the EU's Markets in Crypto-Assets (MiCA) regulation allows the UK to set its own rules for crypto. The EU itself is set to revise MiCA in 2027 to cover foreign stablecoin issuers.

#5028. Zelle picks Solana for ZelleUSD stablecoin and international payments

Zelle plans to use Solana for its ZelleUSD stablecoin and international payments, with confidential transfers designed to hide payment amounts.

Zelle, the US peer-to-peer payments network operated by Early Warning Services (EWS), a consortium of banks that includes JPMorgan and Wells Fargo, is partnering with Solana to support its expansion into international payments. The company announced on stage at Breakpoint 2026 that its proprietary dollar-backed stablecoin, ZelleUSD (ZLUSD), will launch on the Solana blockchain to allow for peer-to-peer payments internationally.

The move brings one of the largest domestic payments networks in the US into the blockchain-based payments market. Zelle processed more than $1.2 trillion in US payments in 2025 and has since taken steps to establish an international business. In June, the company named India as its first international remittance corridor, with plans to allow US consumers to send money to friends and family in the country. In the same announcement, the company unveiled ZelleUSD, intended to underpin future international payment capabilities in additional markets. ZelleUSD’s issuer is Anchorage Digital Bank.

“Sending money around the world is a pain; it’s complex, it’s fragmented and it’s often unfamiliar. Over half of the Remittance services that are offered today don’t deliver money as fast as we’d like them to,” said Alexandra Lang, vice president of digital asset partnerships at Zelle. “Solana is a partner we chose because we’re here to deliver payments at scale.”

For Zelle, the decision to select Solana appears to have been driven in large part by the network’s infrastructure. “Zelle has such a massive scale in the United States, we needed a partner that can also deliver at massive scale internationally as well,” said Denise Leonhard, General Manager at Zelle in a video discussing the partnership.

“The number one priority is the responsibility that we have to make sure that payments are safe, secure, and they get to the right place [...] That piece around trust, security, proven infrastructure is the reason why we chose Solana.”

Following several years in which network outages raised questions about Solana’s suitability for financial applications, the chain has maintained 100% uptime since February 2024. This is despite a routing failure at infrastructure provider Teraswitch in August 2026, which took 29% of Solana’s network offline. According to blockchain intelligence platform Metrika, transaction finality slowed, but uptime was sustained.

On stage at Breakpoint 2026, the Solana Foundation announced the migration of Solana from beta into mainnet, bringing with it an improvement in finality speed from 13 seconds to 150 milliseconds.

Related: HKMA, Swift explore tokenized deposits for cross-border bank payments

In the payments wallet, customers will have the option to encrypt their transfer so it is shielded from the public blockchain. Institutions that are part of Zelle’s network will be able to decrypt the transfer to carry out the checks needed to maintain compliance.

“Features like this are paramount to really getting payments to scale,” said Lang.

Related: DBS, JPMorgan’s Kinexys launch 24/7 transfers of tokenized deposits

Although Solana is gaining ground in stablecoin activity, Ethereum and Tron continue to hold substantially larger pools of dollar-pegged assets. According to Token Terminal data pulled on Nov. 14, Ethereum held $150.5 billion in stablecoins while Tron held $93.2 billion, compared with $13.9 billion on Solana.

However, Solana’s stablecoin activity is growing faster in transaction frequency than on either of its larger competitors. The network recorded an 82% increase in monthly stablecoin transfers year-over-year in October, compared with 48% growth on Ethereum, while Tron’s transfer count declined by nearly 8%, according to Token Terminal data.

Zelle’s choice of Solana over both Tron and Ethereum follows other remittance providers that have expanded on-chain. Also at Breakpoint, MoneyGram outlined an integration with Solflare allowing Solflare wallet users to exchange UDSC to cash. The move expands MoneyGram Ramps on Solana, launched by the company in August.

“That’s where MoneyGram brings something different to the ecosystem: the ability to bridge digital dollars and physical cash at scale, making stablecoins more useful in everyday life,” said Luke Tuttle, Chief Product and Technology Officer at MoneyGram in a press release.

While Zelle did not provide a timeline for the launch of international payments on stage, earlier announcements indicated that the India corridor would be live by the end of 2026.

#5027. Standard Chartered says Japan’s bond tokenization plans could help drive RWA market to $2 trillion

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The bank said tokenizing Japanese government bonds could bring more long-term debt onchain and encourage other countries to follow.

Japan's plans to tokenize government bonds (JGBs) could help the tokenized real-world asset market reach Standard Chartered Bank's existing forecast of $2 trillion by the end of 2028.

The market currently stands at around $45 billion, Geoffrey Kendrick, the bank's global head of digital assets research, wrote in a Monday report. Its $2 trillion forecast has mainly relied on tokenized money market funds and equities, each growing to $750 billion. Longer-dated government bonds would provide another source of growth toward that target, Kendrick said.

"The current crypto sell-off will pass, and when it does, it will be the bringing of real-world assets on-chain that matters most," Kendrick said in an email shared with The Block.

"The more important implication of tokenizing longer-dated debt in one of the world's largest government bond markets is that others may follow suit," Kendrick wrote in the report.

Most government debt brought onchain so far has been short-term Treasury bills, with almost no longer-dated debt tokenized, according to the report. Kendrick put tokenized U.S. Treasury bills at around $13 billion, compared with just under $1 billion in tokenized government debt outside the U.S.

Japan has the world's third-largest government bond market, and BlackRock recently signed a tokenization MOU with SBI and Startale there. Kendrick said other governments may follow if Japan's program becomes large enough to demonstrate cost savings and easier movement of bonds as collateral.

Japan's Ministry of Finance held its first study group meeting on bringing government bonds onchain on Oct. 8. Kendrick noted that there are three options under consideration. Japan could tokenize existing government bonds, issue new bonds on a distributed ledger, or do both.

The first option would be simpler because the legal structure and cash flows would remain the same, Kendrick said. Issuing bonds directly on a ledger could save more work and costs. Japan is still gathering industry feedback.

"While the plan still lacks key details, such as the choice of blockchain and timelines, we think the proposal is a significant step forward for one of the world's largest government bond markets," Kendrick wrote.

Several pilot programmes to tokenize government debt are underway around the world, most notably the UK's Digital Gilt Instrument pilot, expected to go live in early 2027, Kendrick noted. He said a large Japanese program could encourage the U.S. and other major government bond markets to pursue tokenization too, following Wall Street's emerging tokenization model, adding: "This would help to drive overall tokenised RWAs towards our end-2028 market cap forecast of USD 2tn."

#5025. Russia Lets Everyday Investors Buy Bitcoin Starting Today

Everyday investors in Russia can legally buy bitcoin through regulated brokers and crypto exchange offices starting Tuesday, but two of the country’s biggest retail brokers, T-Bank and VTB, are not offering it yet.

The Bank of Russia ordinance that took effect Nov. 17 approves bitcoin, ether and Tether’s USDT for purchase by non-qualified investors, the legal term for ordinary retail buyers who do not meet Russia’s wealth or experience thresholds. From the same date, exchanges may admit those three assets to public trading.

Retail buyers can purchase through a broker, a trust manager or a registered crypto exchange office, on an exchange or over the counter. Purchases through a single intermediary are capped at 300,000 rubles a year, roughly $3,600 at the central bank’s official rate on Tuesday. The cap applies per intermediary, not per person.

Qualified investors face no cap and can access any crypto asset. Every buyer, regardless of status, must pass a knowledge test and read a risk disclosure before the first trade.

JUST IN: 🇷🇺 Starting today, Russian retail investors can now legally buy Bitcoin 👀 pic.twitter.com/EzifKUtcea

The legal door opened before the apps did. “The main reason T-Bank clients do not yet see cryptocurrencies in their banking app is the absence of a market standard for testing,” Alexander Kretov, head of blockchain technology development at T-Bank, told TASS on Tuesday. “As soon as the Bank of Russia publishes it on its official website, we are ready to launch external operations.”

Kretov said T-Bank’s crypto exchange office and digital depository are built and in the final stages of live testing, and that the service will roll out in several stages, “starting with the most active users.”

VTB’s brokerage arm told the agency it is testing the customer journey in its VTB My Investments app and is waiting for the remaining regulations. Once those land, it plans to open crypto trading first to qualified investors who pass the test. The central bank added VTB to its registries of digital depositories and crypto exchangers in October, and the broker ran its first crypto trades for corporate clients the same month.

The single test that brokers are waiting on has been drafted by two industry groups, NEXUS and NAUFOR. As of Nov. 10, the draft had been sent to the Bank of Russia for approval as part of a basic investor-protection standard, but it had not been approved. NAUFOR won self-regulatory status for crypto exchanges and digital depositories in October.

Tuesday’s rule is the first of several dates on Russia’s calendar. A separate central bank ordinance lets exchanges open organized crypto trading from Nov. 22, once the regulator has approved their trading rules. Moscow Exchange, the country’s largest venue, has said it plans to start bitcoin, ether and USDT trading on Dec. 1, initially as a pilot. The exchange and its clearing house are under U.S. sanctions.

The central bank said it limited the retail list to the most liquid assets, judged by market capitalization, average daily trading volume and at least five years of pricing history on foreign exchanges. “This approach protects non-qualified investors from significant losses during sharp and unpredictable price swings,” the regulator said when it announced the list on Nov. 6, as quoted by *Kommersant*.

Russian buyers are not new to bitcoin. They already trade heavily on offshore platforms, Dmitry Lesnov, Finam’s deputy CEO for brokerage, told *Vedomosti*, which reported his estimate that Russia accounts for 15% to 20% of traffic on the largest Asian crypto exchanges. Lesnov also said the 300,000-ruble cap will significantly limit retail interest, and that some buyers will likely stay on foreign exchanges, non-custodial wallets and decentralized platforms.

The framework rests on Federal Law No. 282-FZ, “On Digital Currencies and Digital Rights,” whose main provisions took effect Sept. 1. It licenses exchanges, brokers and depositories but still bans using crypto to pay for goods and services inside Russia.

The question now is less whether Russia allows retail bitcoin buying, and more how long the testing standard takes and whether the first apps can pull buyers home from the offshore desks they already use.

#5023. Winklevoss-backed Cypherpunk buys Zcash dip as mining debut delivers $2.6 million operating profit

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Cypherpunk mined $9.9 million worth of ZEC in 44 days and says its mining business is worth more than the value of its holdings.

Cypherpunk Technologies, the Zcash treasury company founded by Cameron and Tyler Winklevoss, got back to buying ZEC amid its recent pullback while its mining business recorded its first quarterly profit.

Nasdaq-listed Cypherpunk (CYPH) reported $2.6 million in operating income for the third quarter, following the August launch of its Zcash mining fleet. Net income totaled $306 million, entirely driven by $330.8 million in unrealized gains on its ZEC holdings.

"With the pullback in Zcash, we resumed our open market purchase strategy," CEO Dug Onsi said on Monday's earnings call.

Since quarter-end, Cypherpunk bought another 3,775 ZEC for about $3.5 million at an average price of $920.40. Tacking on October's mining production, its treasury reached more than 341,858 ZEC, which is just over 2% of the entire circulating supply. Cypherpunk aims to hold at least 5% of Zcash's circulating supply, much like the 5% ether supply ownership target set by the Tom Lee-chaired Bitmine.

ZEC was down a further 2% to $887 on Monday, extending its decline over the past month to roughly 22%. In late September, ZEC hit a new all-time high of $1,650.

Cypherpunk mined 8,967 ZEC during the first 44 days of operations, pulling in nearly $10 million in revenue. Hosting costs averaged out to about $104 per token, and all the ZEC it mined were routed into its treasury.

The company also raised $56.5 million through its at-the-market stock offering program in the third quarter. The funds went towards mining equipment and more ZEC purchases.

A portion also covered operating expenses "so we don't have to sell the Zcash that we mined," Onsi said.

CIO Will McEvoy said that mining changes how investors should value the company and that whether its shares trade above or below the value of its crypto holdings — a metric commonly known as mNav — "doesn't make a ton of sense nowadays."

"I think analysts should be sort of looking at the business from a sum-of-the-parts perspective," he said, pointing to its ZEC treasury and mining revenue and earnings.

#5022. DTCC, Barclays Say Tokenization's Real Prize Is Unlocking Idle Collateral

DTCC, Barclays Say Tokenization's Real Prize Is Unlocking Idle Collateral

The biggest payoff from tokenizing traditional assets won't come from simply putting them onchain, but from putting idle collateral to work, according to executives at the Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation (DTCC) and Barclays.

"You can tokenize an asset, but if you can't do anything with it, what's the point?" Val Wooton, DTCC's global head of equities solutions, said at Solana's Breakpoint conference in London. "For me, it really is around collateral and liquidity."

Scott Lucas, head of digital assets at Barclays, agreed. A lot of collateral sits "relatively idle" at central securities depositories, he said, and much of the early value for banks will come from moving it more efficiently between their own accounts, before extending that to clients. "There's a lot of money in good housekeeping," he added.

The pair spoke on a panel days after DTCC announced that its tokenization service will connect to Solana, with tokenized assets expected on the network in the second half of 2027.

The service, which launched on October 28, can tokenize assets held at DTCC's depository, starting with Russell 1000 stocks, ETFs tracking major indexes, and U.S. Treasury bills and bonds, Wooton said. The tokens carry the same legal and economic rights as traditionally held securities, and adding Solana reflects DTCC's strategy of being "open," "interoperable" and "multi-network," he added.

Wooton said the biggest opportunity lies in "liquidity optimization," which he said could save "millions, if not billions" of dollars by letting firms move assets that are currently stuck when markets are closed.

Large financial institutions today over-fund and over-collateralize every day because they treat collateral as "just in case," he said. Moving assets onchain, around the clock, would let firms manage collateral "just in time," freeing up "a significant amount of balance sheet" to put to other uses. He added that the ability to move assets and settle them atomically could be "extremely powerful" in times of market stress.

Lucas pointed to Barclays' participation in DTCC's tokenization launch last month, one of the bank's first public moves into the space, and described the efficiency gains from managing its own collateral as good housekeeping.

Barclays is also working with Ironlight on tokenized structured products, Lucas said, though he stressed "it's not a done deal." He added that he would like to see Solana adopted by other central securities depositories around the world, building a shared infrastructure layer across Asia-Pacific, Europe and the United States.

Both executives said adoption remains early. Wooton said the industry is "still in the crawl stage," and that traditional and digital rails will exist side by side "for quite a long time," though DTCC's clients are already tokenizing assets daily.

Lucas said he tries to stay "healthily skeptical" about the speed of adoption while making sure Barclays is ready for its clients. At a recent traditional finance conference in Miami, he said, "every institution we met had money on the table," making investments and building products, a shift from a year earlier, when most were still asking questions.

Barclays has been gradually expanding its crypto activities. The bank invested in U.S. stablecoin startup Ubyx earlier this year and has reportedly weighed a push into tokenized deposits and stablecoin payments, according to Bloomberg. It was also among the investors backing crypto custodian Copper in 2022.

#5020. Solflare CEO says AI security reviews are essential, touts ‘bunker mode’ and MoneyGram deal

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Vidor Gencel also said the company is focused on bringing more real-world assets and financial services onto its Solana-based wallet, including trading, earning, and spending features.

Solflare CEO and co-founder Vidor Gencel said AI-powered security reviews are becoming essential to protecting crypto infrastructure, arguing that AI models can identify and chain together vulnerabilities in ways human reviewers often miss.

Gencel said the Solana-based wallet firm is incorporating AI-enabled security throughout its development pipeline, from product specifications and code reviews to production monitoring, rather than relying on one-time audits.

"We've defined a really good threat model and what are the things that could get hacked and all of the prevention," Gencel told The Block's Gareth Jenkinson at Solana Breakpoint. "It's a tough problem, but I think we're holding strong."

He added that Solflare has adopted a "bunker mode" deployment process requiring extensive approvals across multiple teams before new code can be deployed, amid heightened security concerns following major industry hacks.

"Because what these AI models are really good at, that humans were not good at, is that chaining different attacks to get a bigger attack," Gencel said. "So this is how AI models should be at the end of the day, leverage, and we are doing that every day."

Gencel also said the company is focused on bringing more real-world assets and financial services onto its Solana-based wallet, including trading, earning, and spending features.

Gencel also said Solflare is integrating directly with MoneyGram, enabling users in more than 170 countries to convert USDC into cash at MoneyGram locations.

#5019. Twenty One Capital (XXI) Buys 154 Bitcoin, Its First Purchase of 2026

Twenty One Capital bought 154.32 bitcoin for about $10 million in cash after the weekend selloff, lifting its holdings to about 43,668 BTC.

Twenty One Capital bought 154.32 bitcoin for about $10 million in cash after the weekend selloff, the company said Monday, its first disclosed purchase of 2026 and a small addition to a treasury that had barely moved all year.

The Tether-controlled company said it paid an average of about $64,801 per coin, bringing its holdings to about 43,668 BTC as of Nov. 16. At Tuesday morning’s price near $64,900, that stack is worth roughly $2.83 billion.

“Bitcoin is our benchmark for every investment decision we make. Any business we acquire has to beat simply buying more Bitcoin, and this weekend’s drop in price made adding Bitcoin an easy decision,” CEO Rapha Zagury said in the release.

“Every dollar we spend, whether on Bitcoin, acquisitions, or buybacks, is weighed against maintaining flexibility to run our business well,” he added.

The buy breaks a long pause. Twenty One’s quarterly report, filed Nov. 13, shows it held 43,515 BTC at the end of 2025 and 43,514 BTC on Sept. 30. The only change in between was a single bitcoin transferred on March 31 as a vendor payment.

The same filing shows how much room the company has to keep buying with cash. Twenty One reported $102.8 million in cash and equivalents at Sept. 30, down from $117.7 million at the end of 2025. The $10 million purchase equals about a tenth of that September balance, although the company has not said how much cash it holds now.

Twenty One also carries $486.5 million of convertible notes issued when it went public. Those notes are secured by about 16,116 BTC pledged as collateral, coins the filing says are not available for general corporate purposes while they back the debt.

The company’s bitcoin cost basis was about $3.69 billion at Sept. 30, or roughly $84,900 per coin. The new coins came in about $20,000 below that average, but the treasury as a whole remains underwater at current prices.

Twenty One’s Class A shares closed at $4.56 on Monday, down 7.9% on the day, before the purchase was announced after the market close. The 10-Q lists 346.65 million Class A shares outstanding, which at that price are worth about $1.58 billion, well below the market value of the company’s bitcoin.

That gap has been the central problem for Zagury since he replaced founder Jack Mallers as CEO in July. In an August shareholder letter, he acknowledged investor concerns about the stock trading at a discount to its bitcoin and said Twenty One “must become more than a Bitcoin treasury.” Tether took control of the company in May after buying out SoftBank’s stake.

The company’s latest shareholder letter, released Nov. 13 with third-quarter results, laid out a plan to build cash-generating businesses around the bitcoin treasury. Twenty One reported third-quarter net income of $1.087 billion, almost all of it from a $1.093 billion gain in the fair value of its bitcoin as prices rose during the quarter. For the first nine months of 2026, it posted a net loss of $186.5 million.

Twenty One said further bitcoin purchases are core to its strategy, with the amount and timing set by market conditions, available liquidity and competing investment opportunities. The company did not commit to a size or schedule.

The purchase landed the same day Strategy (MSTR), the largest corporate holder, disclosed buying 485 BTC for $33.4 million, lifting its holdings to 850,849 BTC.

The test now is whether those purchases stay opportunistic and small, or whether Twenty One uses bitcoin, acquisitions or buybacks to close the gap between its share price and its coins.

#5016. Solana Drops 'Mainnet Beta' After Six Years: Here's What 1.0 Is All About

Solana Drops 'Mainnet Beta' After Six Years: Here's What 1.0 Is All About

Solana is officially out of beta, more than six years after launching a blockchain that has been handling real money the whole time.

The Solana Foundation on Monday retired the “mainnet beta” label and introduced Solana 1.0, a shared version number for the network's underlying rules. The change is primarily about how developers and validator operators coordinate software updates, not a fresh boost to transaction speed or any other meaningful improvement over what users already have.

Solana launched its mainnet beta on March 16, 2020, and kept the experimental-sounding name even as users traded tokens, built applications, and moved assets across the chain.

Nothing happens to those assets now. “Your SOL, accounts, assets, applications, and transaction history remain the same,” the Foundation said. Users don't need to swap tokens, move wallets or install anything to keep using the network.

Solana has two major validator clients: Agave, developed by Anza, and Firedancer, built independently by Jump Crypto. Each has its own code, release schedule and version numbers, which can make it harder to tell whether different software releases support the same network features.

Solana 1.0 creates a common reference point for the protocol itself. An Agave release and a Firedancer release can support the same protocol version without sharing a software version number.

That distinction matters as the network relies on more than one software implementation. A bug in one client need not affect every validator, although software diversity alone cannot guarantee the network will stay online.

“Announcing a protocol version does not activate every associated improvement at once,” the Foundation warned. Operators still need to check whether their particular software supports a planned upgrade and follow its activation instructions.

The Foundation said it coordinated the change with Anza and the Firedancer team. Its announcement does not describe a new voting system or a change in who governs Solana; the network's governance process for proposals remains separate.

The Foundation credits engineering work that preceded Monday's announcement, particularly Alpenglow and Firedancer. Solana's Alpenglow upgrade went live on October 21, replacing the system validators had used to reach agreement since launch.

Alpenglow targets transaction finality of roughly 150 milliseconds, compared with about 12.8 seconds previously. Finality is the point at which a transaction is considered settled; that improvement came from the October upgrade, not from calling the network 1.0.

Firedancer also reached mainnet in 2025, giving validators a separately developed alternative to Agave. That reduces dependence on a single codebase, though the extent of the protection depends on how widely different clients are actually used.

Solana has reasons to be careful with promises of reliability. Its network went offline for nearly five hours in February 2024, following an outage lasting almost 19 hours a year earlier.

Documentation and block explorers will gradually adopt the new mainnet name, according to the Foundation. Its upgrade tracker still lists Agave 4.4 as planned for November and Agave 4.5 for December, each with its own release and activation requirements.

#5014. Bitwise sees Solana reaching $1,100 by 2031 in bull case as onchain trading grows

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

This projection relies on Solana capturing 65% of tokenized stock trading volume, and further increases in onchain crypto trading and payments.

Solana's SOL token price could rise to $1,100 by 2031 in its bull case, according to Bitwise. Key drivers include growth in onchain trading, especially tokenized stocks, as well as general crypto trading and payments activity.

Bitwise's base and bear cases see the price of SOL at $385 and $80, respectively, according to a report published Monday by Bitwise's Kam Benbrik, head of onchain research, and Rafal Klich, protocol researcher.

"Most of the price increase depends on how quickly stocks move onchain and how well Solana competes against other blockchains on tokenization," the researchers wrote.

Adjusting the above figures to reflect the net present value of the tokens, with a 20% per annum discount rate applied over the next 5 years, Bitwise arrives at values of $442, $155 and $32.

Solana is trading at around $71, according to The Block's SOL price page. Bitwise is also among the issuers of U.S. spot Solana ETFs.

For the bullish scenario to play out, Bitwise assumes that 5% of global stocks will be tokenized by 2031, and that Solana will capture 65% of the associated trading. As a result, around $30.7 trillion will be traded on Solana per year in tokenized stocks.

Bitwise also assumes 50% annual growth in crypto trading and other segments, and 15x growth in payments. This would require Solana to clear around 3.6 billion transactions daily, about 23 times its existing throughput.

"This case asks the most of the network," the researchers wrote. "Solana would need to widen its lead in tokenized stocks, overtake Tron as the main stablecoin payment chain, and compete with NEAR for AI agent activity."

In its bull case, Bitwise projects around $12.83 billion in annual fees and tips and applies a 60x multiple, valuing the network at about $770 billion. It assumes a supply of 700 million SOL in 2031.

In the base case, a 40x multiple is used with only 4% of world stocks tokenized, and Solana takes 50% of the related volume. That would bring annual tokenized stock trading on Solana to $15.7 trillion.

As a proportion of Solana's transactions in the base case, Bitwise projects that tokenized stocks will represent around 66% as compared to less than 2% today.

A number of factors could impact Bitwise's predictions, including the pace of tokenization, the introduction of new laws, or competing chains gaining a greater market share.

Currently, Ethereum (including Layer 2 networks) dominates the tokenized asset market by value, largely comprising treasury and fund products. In addition, Base and Robinhood have significant customers.

Additionally, an increase in transaction capacity is required for Solana. In the base case, Bitwise estimates that tokenized shares will need around 2.5x the current maximum block compute, rising to around 5x in the bull case.

Finally, how much revenue Solana can actually earn is uncertain. Apps and validators may seek to bypass Solana's native mechanism of collecting priority fees and tips.

"Our valuation rests on assumptions that are difficult to assess today," the researchers wrote. "We don’t know the trade size when institutions arrive, how tokenized trading volume will grow relative to regular stocks, or what investors will be willing to pay for network revenue in five years."

#5013. Safe wallet reportedly loses $6.5M as Zodiac suspects key compromise

Zodiac’s co-founder said a likely compromised key and overly permissive wallet settings enabled the theft, rather than a flaw in its smart contracts.

A Safe wallet reportedly lost about $6.45 million in cryptocurrency Monday, with Zodiac’s co-founder pointing to a likely compromised key and the wallet’s permissions settings rather than a flaw in Zodiac’s contracts.

Blockchain security firm PeckShield reported the suspected theft, citing onchain investigator Specter. Ethereum blockchain records reviewed by Cointelegraph show that approximately 4.39 million USDC (USDC) and 1.72 million syrupUSDC tokens were transferred from the wallet in a single transaction on Monday.

Johannes Moormann, co-founder of Zodiac, told Cointelegraph that his team reviewed the transaction and confirmed it passed through the wallet’s Zodiac Roles Modifier, which checked the transfer against its configured permissions policy and allowed it. “There is no vulnerability in the module. The contracts worked correctly. The transaction came from the key of a long-standing role member, which was most likely compromised,” he said.

Moormann added that the wallet’s custom permissions policy permitted the transaction, almost certainly unintentionally. He said the wallet’s operators controlled its keys and permissions, and that Zodiac had neither access to them nor involvement in the setup. Zodiac has not been in contact with the affected team.

Specter first flagged the Zodiac Roles module as part of the suspected attack. PeckShield also said the attacker used approximately $813,000 to purchase Monero-linked tokens on Hyperliquid.

The Zodiac Roles module allows Safe users to assign permissions for specific transactions. Moormann said the incident did not indicate a vulnerability affecting other wallets using the module.

The identity of the wallet owner has not been established. Cointelegraph contacted Safe and Gnosis Guild for comment but had not received a response by publication.

Related: DOJ moves to seize $1.2B from Binance hack, disrupts FaceStealer scam

Blockchain analytics firm Bitquery traced funds from the Safe wallet through multiple addresses to Hyperliquid, where five accounts purchased approximately $1 million in XMR1 tokens.

The firm noted similarities with fund movements following Monday’s Lighter incident, including a 160 Ether (ETH) transfer and purchases of XMR1.

However, Bitquery said it found no transfers connecting the two wallet groups and could not establish whether the same actor was involved.

#5012. Bitcoin Policy Institute Renames to Finney Foundation for Open Technology

The Bitcoin Policy Institute announced Tuesday that it is now the Finney Foundation for Open Technology, a new parent organization whose brief spans open-source artificial intelligence, digital cash and encrypted messaging. The institute’s bitcoin work continues inside the foundation, and bitcoin remains a primary focus.

The name honors Hal Finney (1956-2014), a cryptographer and software developer who helped build the email encryption program PGP, ran one of the first anonymous remailers and received the first bitcoin transaction from Satoshi Nakamoto in January 2009. In a November 1992 essay, Finney wrote that the computer “can be used as a tool to liberate and protect people, rather than to control them.” He was diagnosed with ALS the same year he received that first transaction and died in 2014.

Washington is now writing the rules for artificial intelligence, the foundation said, and fear drives much of that debate. As the Bitcoin Policy Institute did for bitcoin, the Finney Foundation said it will argue that open systems outcompete closed ones, that individuals should control the tools they rely on, and that American strength comes from building in the open.

“We set out to make Washington think about Bitcoin differently — to educate them on the importance of open monetary networks,” said David Zell, president of the Finney Foundation. “Our fellows now serve in congressional offices for both parties and in the executive branch. Our ideas have shaped discourse and policy. Our expansion will help us stay at the forefront of today’s most consequential policy debates, especially those around open source AI.”

That argument was already on stage in September at Freedom Tech DC, the institute’s Washington summit, formerly the Bitcoin Policy Summit. The invite-only gathering put open-source and open-weight AI, data centers and energy infrastructure on the agenda alongside bitcoin and dollar-backed stablecoins.

Block co-founder Jack Dorsey said there that “the next and probably largest element [of freedom technology] is open intelligence and making sure that it’s not locked up in the hands of any one government or any one company… that’s where we’re going to get the best outcomes.”

Alongside the new name, the foundation published an Open Technology Declaration, titled “For Us, Not Over Us,” which argues for machines that serve people rather than rule over them. The text takes up Finney’s line about computers as tools of liberation and says the foundation will offer research, policy ideas and educational programs aimed at that goal. It asks who the new machines are for and who is in control of them, and it calls for people to build the tools they need without waiting for permission.

It is also launching an inaugural class of Finney Fellows, researchers with expertise spanning machine intelligence, free speech, national security, biotechnology and space. The foundation says that cohort will be named Wednesday at 10 a.m. ET. A separate roster of Bitcoin Policy Institute fellows remains listed on the same page.

The shift does not retire the institute’s bitcoin brief. The Bitcoin Policy Institute, founded in 2022, has run a congressional fellowship program to place bitcoin specialists in congressional offices, joined the State Department’s Freedom Tech Excellence Program as a founding partner, and urged Congress to include bitcoin in proposed de minimis tax relief. In August, it named Conner Brown executive director as it expanded its leadership team.

The Finney Foundation describes itself as a nonpartisan, nonprofit 501(c)(3) think tank in Washington, D.C. Zell is president. Brown remains executive director on the new staff list. For now, the bitcoin think tank still exists, just under a wider roof, with a new class of fellows due to be named in the morning.

#5010. 'Tokenization Is Not the Finish Line': Jupiter to Let Users Borrow Against Bullish Shares

'Tokenization Is Not the Finish Line': Jupiter to Let Users Borrow Against Bullish Shares

Jupiter, the largest trading platform on Solana, is bringing Bullish's shares onchain as collateral, as part of a push to put tokenized assets to work.

The company will launch a dedicated, permissioned market on its lending platform, Jupiter Lend, starting with Bullish's NYSE-listed BLSH stock, Jupiter tweeted on Monday. More issuer tokens will follow, though the market won't be available in all jurisdictions, it added.

A NYSE-listed company is bringing its own shares directly to Jupiter Lend.

Today at Breakpoint, we announced our partnership with @Bullish to launch a dedicated, permissioned $BLSH market.

For the first time, Issuer Tokens can be used as collateral inside Solana’s… pic.twitter.com/rWWqwOcWmK

— Jupiter (@JupiterExchange) November 16, 2026

Unlike many tokenized stocks, which give holders a claim on a share, Bullish's issuer-sponsored token "is itself the share," Jupiter COO Kash Dhanda said in a presentation at Solana's Breakpoint conference in London.

Users will be able to borrow against them with fully onchain liquidations in an "institutional-grade lending market," and integrations with qualified custodians, he said. Bullish acquired Equiniti, one of the world's largest transfer agents, earlier this year, Dhanda noted.

Dhanda framed the announcements around what he called the "post-tokenization era." Most tokenized assets today are "parked, not productive," he said.

Of the roughly $460 billion in tokenized assets tracked by data platform RWA.xyz, only about $39.5 billion is actually onchain and can be moved off the issuer's platform, and just $3.4 billion is used in DeFi, according to DeFiLlama, Dhanda said, a "utilization gap" of 99%.

"There is this myth, and this is the thing that's misunderstood: 'If you put it on chain, they will come'," he said. "This is not the field of dreams," he added, arguing that, "Tokenization is not the finish line. It is actually the starting line."

Jupiter launched two other products alongside the Bullish market. The Jupiter Global Passport lets users verify their identity to buy permissioned assets, while keeping their information as private as possible using zero-knowledge data sharing, Dhanda said. It is live with KYC verification, with onchain credit scores and proof of wealth to follow, and the company is exploring licenses in different jurisdictions.

Jupiter Liquidity Services, meanwhile, will help asset issuers design their markets, seed initial capital, find market makers and integrate with Jupiter's routing so that their tokens are available across the platforms that use it. "You integrate once, you will be available everywhere," Dhanda said.

"Shallow credit markets don't drive liquidity on chain," he added. "If you cannot do more with your onchain asset than you can do with the off-chain version of it, you're probably not going to switch."

Jupiter says it has handled more than $500 billion in trading volume so far this year and paid out $162 million in yield. The Breakpoint announcements follow the company's launch last week of token baskets, yield vaults and a rebuilt portfolio, and come as other venues bring tokenized stocks to Solana. Trading platform Formation has notified the SEC of plans to trade tokenized U.S. stocks on the network.

Jupiter powers token swaps on The Information Exchange, Decrypt's trading platform.

#5008. Nvidia turmoil tests Bitcoin miners’ AI funding plans

Nvidia’s leadership upheaval and Texas permit restrictions sharpen the divide between miners with funded projects and those still seeking capital and approvals.

Jensen Huang’s departure from Nvidia comes as Bitcoin miners are committing billions of dollars to risky AI infrastructure pivots.

For investors, questions remain around whether large AI contracts can produce cash flows that justify construction costs and support debt service and lead to a sustainable business model. The pivot replaces exposure to Bitcoin (BTC) with construction, financing and customer credit risks. Companies with already-funded projects, usable power and enforceable payment commitments could therefore deliver sharply different returns from peers whose expansion still depends on additional capital and regulatory approvals.

Huang’s departure reaches miners through Nvidia’s overlapping roles as supplier, customer and financier. IREN signed a five-year, $3.4 billion cloud services contract with Nvidia in May, while Nvidia invested $2 billion in CoreWeave in January.

IREN and Applied Digital fell during the Nov. 12 AI selloff following Huang’s departure. For companies seeking equity financing, lower share prices mean issuing more shares to raise the same amount.

JPMorgan credit analyst Erica Spear said “new deals could take longer or require more compensation.” For miners, a slowdown in Nvidia investments, guarantees or capacity purchases could raise financing costs and delay construction. Tighter funding for their cloud-provider tenants could also weaken demand for additional capacity.

Related: Core Scientific to quit Bitcoin mining by year-end as AI business surges

A financing retreat has not been confirmed. Nvidia’s board said it expects operations, customer commitments and its product roadmap to continue uninterrupted.

It is also important to recognize that the AI data center industry is in fact a diverse set of entirely separate business models with their own risk exposures. Leasing powered land, operating colocation facilities, and owning GPUs to sell computing services have different construction requirements, operating responsibilities and equipment replacement exposure.

#5007. Peraire-Bueno brothers acquitted of wire fraud in $25 million Ethereum MEV case

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

In April 2023, a group of MEV bots lost over $25 million in seconds during a sophisticated attack on the Ethereum blockchain.

A Manhattan federal judge acquitted MIT-educated brothers Anton and James Peraire-Bueno of wire fraud and related charges over a $25 million Ethereum maximal extractable value (MEV) exploit, finding that their trading strategy did not constitute criminal fraud under the statutes cited by prosecutors.

The brothers used bait transactions to manipulate other traders' sandwich bots before intercepting their trades, but the judge said the transactions were not conducted on a regulated market where the alleged conduct would be prohibited.

U.S. District Judge Jessica Clarke said the question was not whether the brothers' trading strategy was improper or unethical, but whether it constituted wire fraud. Prosecutors failed to show that the brothers made material misrepresentations in executing the scheme, the ruling said, granting their motion for acquittal.

Back in April 2023, a group of MEV bots lost over $25 million in seconds during a sophisticated attack on the Ethereum blockchain. The attacker replaced the normal MEV transactions of the bots with malicious ones, causing them to lose money.

In May 2024, the Peraire-Bueno brothers were arrested after prosecutors said they exploited the Ethereum blockchain. The case involved maximal extractable value, which is the maximum amount of value that can be extracted from a given DeFi protocol or smart contract by a user or group of users.

A mistrial was declared in November 2025 after the jury deadlocked in Manhattan federal court. Coin Center executive director Peter Van Valkenburgh told The Block that the case was a "massive overstep."

"A mistrial is not at all surprising to me because, gosh, if I wasn't a crypto person and I was on that jury, I would just be incredibly confused," Van Valkenburgh said at the time.

#5005. Goldman Sachs More Than Doubles Its Bitcoin ETF Holdings to $1.28 Billion

Goldman Sachs more than doubled its spot bitcoin ETF holdings in the third quarter to about $1.28 billion, driven by a 52-fold jump in Fidelity’s fund, while cutting its stake in Strategy (MSTR) by more than half, a Monday filing shows.

The bank reported the positions in a Form 13F covering the quarter ended Sept. 30. Its long holdings across five spot bitcoin funds were valued at $1.283 billion, up from about $580 million in its filing for June 30, an increase of roughly 121%.

BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust remained Goldman’s largest bitcoin fund holding, at 16,440,599 shares worth $778.3 million. The share count actually slipped about 5% from 17,232,201 at midyear. The value rose because IBIT climbed from $33.29 to $47.34 a share during the quarter, a gain of about 42%.

The bigger change was in the Fidelity Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund. Goldman held 5,477,583 FBTC shares worth $397.9 million at Sept. 30, up from just 105,840 shares, worth $5.4 million, three months earlier.

Goldman also reported 2,000,020 shares of the Morgan Stanley Bitcoin Trust, worth $47.9 million. The fund, ticker MSBT, launched in April as the first bitcoin product from a U.S. bank-affiliated asset manager, with a 0.14% sponsor fee. Goldman’s June filing did not list it. The trust reported 36.42 million shares outstanding and $873.4 million in net assets at Sept. 30, so Goldman held about 5.5% of the fund.

The rest of the bitcoin fund book was smaller. Grayscale’s Bitcoin Mini Trust rose to 1,383,498 shares, worth $51.1 million, from 22,905. Goldman also listed 120,600 shares of the Grayscale Bitcoin Trust, worth $7.8 million, alongside puts on the same number of shares, which suggests that position is hedged.

The bank’s options book in IBIT is large, too. It reported puts on 7,513,000 IBIT shares, valued at $355.7 million, and calls on 4,895,600 shares, valued at $231.8 million. Those figures reflect the value of the underlying shares, not premiums paid, so they do not measure money at risk. Goldman runs one of Wall Street’s biggest market-making and derivatives businesses, and its 13F does not say which positions are hedges for clients and which are its own views.

The bank went the other way on Strategy. Goldman’s long position in the bitcoin treasury company fell to 2,760,766 shares, worth $422.6 million at Sept. 30, from 5,810,162 shares at midyear, a drop of about 52%.

Its Strategy put options barely moved, covering 4,738,700 shares worth $725.4 million, compared with 4,735,800 in June. Calls rose to 707,200 shares from 575,900. Strategy closed at $103.03 on Monday, down from $153.09 at the end of September, as the company keeps buying bitcoin and buying back preferred stock.

The pattern mirrors the one at Wells Fargo, whose own 13F, also filed Monday, showed its IBIT stake more than doubling while its Strategy position fell by about a third. JPMorgan reported $540 million in bitcoin ETFs in its third-quarter filing last week.

The Goldman positions are a snapshot as of Sept. 30, before bitcoin’s fall from above $83,000 to about $62,000 over the weekend. At Monday’s closing price of $36.79, the bank’s 16.4 million IBIT shares would be worth about $605 million, roughly $173 million less than the value in the filing.

U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs have been bleeding money since the quarter ended. IBIT lost about $2 billion over nine straight sessions through mid-November before taking in roughly $76.5 million on Monday.

The filing does not show whether Goldman added to or trimmed its bitcoin fund positions in October or November. That answer arrives with the fourth-quarter 13F, due in mid-February.

#5003. Democratic senators warn SEC’s tokenized stock exemption has illicit finance gaps

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The SEC debuted its exemption in September that exempts tokenized securities venues from having to register as an exchange, opening the door for tokenized U.S. stocks.

Four Democratic senators are raising concerns about the Securities and Exchange Commission's newly unveiled innovation exemption to trade tokenized stocks, warning that it could open doors for illicit finance actors.

One of their main objections was that language in the exemption was too vague around illicit finance protections. The SEC debuted its long-awaited five-year exemption in September that exempts certain trading venues, called tokenized securities venues, from having to register as an exchange, opening the door for tokenized U.S. stocks.

However, the exemption has some gaps around illicit finance, Sens. Angela Alsobrooks, Ruben Gallego, Mark Warner and Catherine Cortez Masto said in their letter to SEC Chair Paul Atkins. For example, the exemption doesn't specify how know-your-customer and anti-money laundering apply to trading and also doesn't say if a platform needs to restrict who receives the tokens.

"As a result, a tokenized equity purchased by a verified individual could be transferred to an unverified wallet, including one held by a foreign person or illicit actor, beyond the reach of the U.S. regulatory perimeter," the lawmakers said in the letter.

The SEC declined to comment. Politico first reported on the letter.

All four Democrats had a significant role in negotiating broader legislation to regulate the digital asset industry called the Clarity Act. Following the midterm elections, Democrats will take the Senate and House, wielding control and oversight over key congressional committees that have jurisdiction over crypto. The SEC under the Trump administration, meanwhile, has moved forward with its regulatory agenda, which will likely draw oversight from Democrats in the coming year.

The four also had concerns about protections for retail investors around pricing and said issuers should be given the option to opt out of having their stocks tokenized.

In the letter to Atkins, the four senators asked Atkins to respond to questions about how the agency will enforce compliance with applicable AML standards and how it plans to verify disclosures and self-verification, among other things. The senators also asked that the SEC make its process more transparent by pursuing " any changes to the regulatory treatment of tokenized securities through a transparent rulemaking process."

"Though tokenization may well improve efficiencies in the market and broaden access, long-term success relies on investors and issuers trusting that this new regulatory regime upholds the safeguards that make the U.S.’s capital markets the envy of the world," they said.
