#5002. Saylor’s Strategy Keeps Buying Bitcoin, Adding 'A Little Orange' Through the Dip

Saylor’s Strategy Keeps Buying Bitcoin, Adding 'A Little Orange' Through the Dip

Strategy kept buying Bitcoin through a brutal market stretch, adding 485 BTC worth roughly $33.4 million and repurchasing $228 million of its preferred stock, as chairman Michael Saylor urged the market to rally with a characteristically cheeky nudge.

The company disclosed the purchase in a filing Monday. The modest addition, small by the standards of a firm that once bought tens of thousands of coins at a time, lifted Strategy’s total holdings to 850,849 BTC as of Nov. 15, alongside $4.9 billion in U.S. dollar assets.

Strategy, the largest corporate holder of Bitcoin, remains far ahead of any other treasury company.

The buy caps a stretch of smaller, steadier accumulation paired with stock buybacks. Strategy recently added $82 million in Bitcoin while repurchasing $188 million of its STRC preferred shares, part of a shift toward what it calls a capital-management approach, buying back stock when it views the shares as undervalued rather than relentlessly stacking coins.

Bitcoin has slid sharply in recent days, trading well below the six-figure highs it touched earlier in the cycle, and the downturn has squeezed Bitcoin treasury firms whose valuations rise and fall with the asset.

Saylor, undeterred, posted the holdings update to X alongside the firm’s trademark Bitcoin tracker, and followed it with a quip aimed at the slumping market: “This dip could use a little orange,” a nod to Bitcoin’s signature color.

The purchase lands during a precarious moment for the treasury-company model Strategy pioneered. Index provider MSCI has finalized rules that could eject Bitcoin treasury firms like Strategy from its widely tracked equity indexes, classifying companies whose balance sheets are dominated by a single asset as “non-operating” and therefore ineligible.

Removal would force index-tracking funds to sell the affected shares, potentially pressuring prices and shrinking the premium these firms have long commanded over the value of their Bitcoin.

For now, Strategy is doing what it has always done, buying Bitcoin and talking it up, even as the market tests its conviction.

The combination of a small purchase and a sizable buyback underscores the balancing act: keep accumulating, defend the stock, and weather a downturn that has turned the once-unstoppable treasury trade into a far more fraught proposition.

#5000. Coinbase and Broadridge propose standard for tokenized stock disclosures

The proposed standard aims to connect tokenized stockholders with corporate disclosures and voting materials across digital wallets, but would not create shareholder rights.

Coinbase and Broadridge proposed a technical standard Monday that would let digital wallets find and display shareholder notices linked to tokenized stocks.

The jointly developed Onchain Rights Standard would give issuers, custodians and wallet providers a shared format for communications about dividends, shareholder meetings and other corporate actions. Broadridge separately announced a supporting data service, known as an oracle, intended to help participating firms document the distribution of those notices.

The proposal’s technical documentation describes an onchain announcement that links a token to a standardized corporate document. A dividend notice, for example, could specify the date, payment terms and any choices available to holders. Compatible wallets could then display notices from different issuers using the same format, reducing the need for multiple other integrations.

“The proposed standard is a communications framework; it does not itself create shareholder rights,” Mark Nichols, Broadridge’s co-president of digital assets, told Cointelegraph.

Nichols said implementation would depend on the underlying structure of each tokenized stock, including whether holders have direct shareholder rights.

The companies said they submitted the proposal for review as an Ethereum Improvement Proposal. They did not name additional adopters of the standard or give an implementation timetable.

Broadridge launched its onchain shareholder governance platform in April, naming Galaxy as an intended user for its May shareholder meeting. The platform integrates Broadridge’s ProxyVote system with digital wallets, allowing investors to receive shareholder materials, confirm holdings and submit votes, with voting records maintained on an Avalanche-based blockchain.

#4999. Trezor hit by another shipping partner data leak, affecting 27,000 Skladon orders

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The breach exposed names, addresses and purchase details, following a similar leak at ShipMonk in August.

Trezor customer names, addresses, and other personal information were leaked in a breach at shipping partner Skladon, affecting roughly 27,000 orders. It's the second such incident for the crypto wallet manufacturer since August.

In a Sunday announcement, Trezor said Skaldon notified the company that its systems holding customer shipping data had been accessed by unauthorized parties. The breach reportedly occurred on Nov. 7, with the attacker believed to have exported information on orders placed between Aug. 9 and Nov. 7.

The stolen information included "full names, phone numbers, delivery addresses, email addresses and order details." Trezor reassured users that its own systems were unaffected and that no wallet backups, PINs, or passphrases were leaked.

The report said that some customers were already reporting that they received scam calls from bad actors using that data to impersonate support staff.

"Please be alert to potential risks to your physical security," Trezor warned.

The incident follows an August breach at ShipMonk, another of Trezor's shipping partners, which exposed personal information on approximately 13,700 customers across seven countries. Most had their phone numbers and shipping addresses exposed.

"We know this is not the first time customer contact details and addresses have been exposed in an incident involving one of our partners," Trezor said.

The company said it is reviewing its third-party partners and working to cut the time it holds order data from 90 days to 45 days.

It also introduced anonymous pickup in 18 European countries, letting customers pick up their orders without giving their names or home addresses. Trezor said it is working on rolling out the feature to U.S. customers "as soon as possible."

#4996. CFTC backs blockchain-based derivatives infrastructure as Backpack, tZERO win approvals

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

On Monday, CFTC Chair Michael Selig said the agency had approved the applications, marking another sign of its increasingly crypto-friendly regulatory approach.

The Commodity Futures Trading Commission has approved applications from Backpack and tZERO to establish regulated derivatives exchanges and clearinghouses that use blockchain technology to execute and settle transactions.

On Monday, CFTC Chair Michael Selig highlighted the approval of a blockchain-based derivatives exchange and clearinghouse, which appears to correspond to Backpack's newly announced U.S. regulatory approvals.

"These approvals mark an important milestone in the evolution of American financial markets and demonstrate that there is a regulated pathway for a new generation of market infrastructure built using blockchain technology," Selig said.

Financial tech company tZERO received its exchange approval last week, following its earlier approval from the agency to operate a derivatives clearing organization, which it says will allow the firm to "support derivatives trading and clearing through its own regulated entities."

"The approvals establish a unique new derivatives market infrastructure vertical within tZERO's growing Infrastructure-as-a-Service (IaaS) business, enabling the company to develop exchange and clearing services for financial institutions and other market participants," tZERO said.

Backpack announced its approval from the CFTC on Monday. In a statement, Backpack US President Mark Wetjen called the approvals a "really big deal."

"This is an achievement that demonstrates that the highest standards of regulatory compliance and blockchain innovation can go hand in hand," Wetjen said.

Over the past year, the CFTC under Selig has taken significant steps for crypto regulation, including working with the Securities and Exchange Commission to clarify which digital assets are securities or commodities, creating an innovation task force and supporting 24/7 trading and bitcoin perpetuals.

Selig, who currently serves as the CFTC's sole sitting commissioner, could soon have fellow commissioners. Last week, President Donald Trump tapped Nathan Anonick and Matthew Rowland to be commissioners at the CFTC. The Senate Agriculture Committee will hold its nomination hearing for the bipartisan pair on Tuesday, after which their nominations could head to a full Senate confirmation vote.

#4994. Nvidia Stock Has a 20% Rebound in Sight—If Bulls Can Clear a Hurdle

Nvidia Stock Has a 20% Rebound in Sight—If Bulls Can Clear a Hurdle

If you were looking to buy Nvidia stock at a discount, last week may have been the best time to shop. Shares tanked following the Jensen Huang scandal involving an affair with an alleged Chinese spy, falling as low as $148.80.

On that drop, Nvidia shares touched a major support and then bounced after dropping 38% from its all-time high.

But if you didn’t buy the bottom, the second best time may be this week. The stock is trading at around $159, already up 8% from the lowest point last week. For traders relying on reading the charts, the current price of NVDA is running into another level that could decide whether the recovery has legs.

At around $159 on Monday, its weekly chart now places the $164.40 zone directly in the bulls' way.

That level previously acted as support, a price where buyers stepped in. After the breakdown, it is turning into a level to watch as investors who bought earlier may look to sell once they get their money back. But it could mark a bounce point based on the longer timeframes—as it has for at least three times since September 2025.

If Nvidia can reclaim $165 and hold above it, the next prominent resistance on the chart sits around $188 to $190. That would represent almost 20% upside from Monday's $159 price, although only about 16% from the breakout level, with possible selling pressure along the way.

Failure to clear $164.40 would put $152.62 back on the radar. Another break below that yellow support would weaken the case that last week's bounce marked a bottom.

The exponential moving averages, or EMAs, still offer some encouragement. Nvidia's 50-week EMA remains above its 200-week EMA, a bullish long-term configuration that gives more weight to recent prices but hasn't yet flipped bearish.

The shorter-term signals look less friendly. The Average Directional Index, or ADX, is around 23, suggesting a weakened trend (which is natural after the FUD episode). Its directional readings favor sellers. ADX measures trend strength, not direction by itself.

The Relative Strength Index, or RSI, is near 31.6, barely above the conventional oversold threshold of 30. That can make a relief bounce more plausible, but oversold stocks can keep falling. Neither indicator confirms a reversal yet.

Nvidia's underlying business hasn't suddenly stopped growing. The chipmaker reported $96.2 billion in quarterly revenue in August, up 106% year over year, including $89 billion from its data center business.

Investors will also be watching profit margins and the enormous financing commitments tying Nvidia to the wider AI infrastructure boom.

Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang resigned on November 12 amid reports of extramarital affairs involving Alice Huang, a businesswoman U.S. intelligence suspects of spying for China, and Nvidia executive Kimberly Powell. Reuters subsequently reported a third alleged relationship with another employee.

Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress is serving as interim CEO while an independent investigation into possible foreign-intelligence targeting continues. The espionage allegations have not been established by an independent investigation, much less in court, but were powerful enough to oust the man who led Nvidia for 33 years in record time.

The Financial Times reported that Huang has spoken with allies about returning, while a petition calling for his reinstatement has attracted more than 178,000 signatures, including Elon Musk. The board has not announced plans to bring him back.

The argument sounds familiar: Huang may be too important to Nvidia to keep out. Sam Altman returned to OpenAI days after being ousted in 2023.

Other cases of CEOs too big to let go have been Steve Jobs with Apple, Jack Dorsey with Twitter and Starbuck’s Howard Schultz. Many of these leaders have turned into the face of their companies, making it hard for the markets to trust in any successor.

For bulls, $164.40 remains the line to beat, and charts point to this outcome being quite possible. Assuming, of course, Nvidia's next big reveal is about chips and not a fourth love affair.

#4991. Boris Johnson says UK must lead blockchain development despite crypto doubts

Johnson was joined by Lord Ross Kempsell who called for regulators to work with industry participants to bring the UK to the forefront.

Former United Kingdom Prime Minister Boris Johnson has backed blockchain technology and tokenization as opportunities for the UK’s financial sector, despite persistent doubts about cryptocurrencies.

Speaking at Solana’s Breakpoint conference in London on Monday, Johnson maintained his position that Bitcoin (BTC) resembles a Ponzi scheme, following an opinion piece published in the Daily Mail in March. He questioned who investors could hold accountable if funds were stolen.

However, Johnson distinguished his criticism of digital assets from his view of the underlying technology, saying the UK should be at the forefront of tokenization.

Lord Ross Kempsell, who joined Johnson on stage, said although he shared his reservations on cryptocurrencies, “on the parts of the industry that can be brought into a regulated perimeter, which the regulators are working on, there’s a lot of scope here for a high growth industry.”

Kempsell, whom Johnson nominated for a peerage in 2022, told the panel that more than 8% of UK adults hold crypto, in line with Financial Conduct Authority-commissioned research published in December 2025. Speaking to Cointelegraph after his stage appearance, he said, “I’m a very big believer in making London and keeping London as the onchain capital for innovation.”

Related: UK financial watchdog opens consultations for DeFi guidance, crypto laws

He argued that consumer protection and financial innovation should not be treated as competing priorities, pointing to the UK’s established fintech industry as an example of how regulation can coexist with innovation in the country. He said the UK should avoid introducing a single, all-encompassing piece of crypto legislation, opting instead for “a more ad-hoc approach” to regulation.

He called for collaboration between the regulators and market participants to understand both the risks and opportunities. While he saw consumer protection as “paramount,” he felt it was unfair to single out the crypto industry as the only sector where consumer protection was at risk.

“Really what I care about is the economic growth potential that there is in this industry, the job creation potential, and also its wider links to tech, AI and traditional financial innovation. London’s always been at the center of those historically,” Kempsell told Cointelegraph.

When asked about the approach of the current administration in the UK, he highlighted that “they need to make sure that they get the fundamental market conditions right.” In his eyes, that meant making the UK a competitive player in regard to tax and facilitating business innovation.

Related: UK names 6 banks to lead first digitally native government bond

Tim Meggs, co-founder of London-based digital asset trading firm Lo:Tech, said Johnson “made a fair point about people getting burned,” but said most of the losses experienced by the industry stemmed from thin liquidity and a lack of visibility over how prices were made.

“If the FCA gets its perimeter right, the UK has a chance to make that kind of transparency the norm,” Meggs said. “But until the clearing and prime broking infrastructure catches up, good rules won’t be enough on their own to make London the place where serious digital asset trading gets done.”

Robin Nordnes, founder and CEO of Solana infrastructure firm Raiku, argued that Johnson’s question about who is in charge makes the case for blockchain, since a public ledger needs no guarantor. “Every transaction on Solana is public and can be checked by anyone, which is more than you can say for most of what happens in the City,” he said.

Nordnes warned that “if Westminster keeps treating this as retail speculation, the trading systems behind the next generation of markets will be built in New York and Singapore, and London will end up renting them.”

#4988. Bitmine edges toward 5% of ETH supply as treasuries report buys amid market slump

Publicly traded crypto treasury companies Bitmine, Strive and Cypherpunk Technologies disclosed increases in their cryptocurrency holdings on Monday, joining Strategy in accumulating digital assets despite a broader market downturn.

Bitmine, the largest publicly traded Ether treasury company, said it added 7,653 Ether (ETH) over the past week, bringing its total holdings to 6.08 million ETH, about 4.97% of its total supply and bringing it ever closer to its stated aim of accumulating 5% of the ETH supply, according to a Monday announcement.

#4986. Tesla accused of selling FSD to Korean owners who can’t legally use it

A South Korean consumer group is asking regulators to stop Tesla from marketing “Full Self-Driving” to customers whose cars can’t legally run it. By Tesla’s own numbers, that covers 99% of the Teslas it sold in Korea last year.

Citizens United for Consumer Sovereignty plans to file the complaint with the Korea Fair Trade Commission (KFTC) on Thursday, according to Reuters, which reviewed it. It comes just a week after a Seoul court threw out a refund lawsuit from Korean owners who paid for FSD.

It all comes down to where the car was built. The Model S, Model X, and Cybertruck come from US factories and enter Korea under the KORUS free trade agreement, which recognizes US safety standards. Those are the only Teslas that got “FSD (Supervised)” when Tesla switched it on in Korea late last year.

The problem is that those don’t represent the vast majority of Tesla vehicles sold in Korea.

The Model 3 and Model Y sold in Korea come from Giga Shanghai. There’s no equivalent deal with China, so those cars need Korean approval that FSD doesn’t have.

And that’s most of the fleet. Tesla Korea country manager Seo Young-deuk told lawmakers last month that FSD works on about 20% of Teslas registered in the country. Tesla has sold more than 170,000 Shanghai-built vehicles in Korea since 2023, per data it handed to the National Assembly, and sales of those China-made cars grew more than fivefold between 2023 and 2025.

Seo’s figure also clears up the conflicting eligibility numbers we flagged when Korean police booked 80 Tesla drivers for using illegal FSD unlock devices last month. A lawmaker’s data from May put it at 2.4%, while a data firm counted 20.7% in August. Tesla’s own testimony matches the higher one.

For years, Tesla charged Korean buyers a one-time 9.043 million won (about $6,726) for FSD. It moved to a 150,000 won ($112) monthly subscription in August.

The complaint points to Tesla’s “coming soon” promotion last year and a demo video shot on Korean roads. Neither made it clear that FSD would only work on a small slice of the cars Tesla was selling. The group says Tesla knew that and kept pitching the package to every buyer anyway.

“Tesla generated substantial revenue by selling an expensive driver-assistance system to mass-market buyers who cannot use it for even a single day,” said Choi Young-suk, who heads automotive issues at the group.

It wants the KFTC to temporarily order Tesla to stop marketing FSD for ineligible cars. It’s also asking for fines and for the case to be sent to state investigators.

The KFTC is already looking at this. An official told Reuters the agency opened a probe into Tesla’s FSD advertising earlier this year and has received written responses from the company. Tesla didn’t respond to Reuters’ requests for comment.

Tesla has collected FSD money from buyers of China-made cars in Korea for more than three years. But at an October 8 parliamentary hearing, Seo testified that Tesla had never formally asked the transport ministry to test FSD. Tesla filed for certification about two weeks later.

Approval won’t be a rubber stamp either. The ministry told a lawmaker that FSD doesn’t comply with local safety rules, including the requirement that drivers keep their hands on the wheel. Current FSD versions let drivers go hands-free as long as the cabin camera sees them watching the road.

None of this slowed sales. Tesla passed BMW this year to become Korea’s top imported car brand.

So far, owners haven’t had much luck in court. Last month, a Seoul court dismissed a lawsuit from Tesla owners seeking FSD refunds, with the judge pointing to Korean regulations, not Tesla, as the reason more features never launched.

#4985. Fed Is Modeling AI 'Super Intelligence' Scenarios, Including Job Losses and a Stock Market Slump

Fed Is Modeling AI 'Super Intelligence' Scenarios, Including Job Losses and a Stock Market Slump

Federal Reserve Chair Kevin Warsh said Monday that central bank staff have started modeling how artificial intelligence could reshape the U.S. economy, including scenarios where the technology raises unemployment even as the economy grows.

Speaking at the Fed's Productivity and Jobs conference in San Francisco, Warsh described three possible paths: a productivity boom benefiting workers, a boom that leaves workers behind, and an AI investment bust that drags down stocks and economic growth.

“These are illustrative scenarios, not forecasts,” Warsh said, explaining that staff are using simplified economic models to test what might happen over the next several years. The Fed is preparing for different outcomes, not predicting which one will materialize.

Warsh called the technology “super intelligence,” or SI. That language is now official Fed terminology after an October change following a Donald Trump administration executive order, rather than a scientific determination that today's AI systems have already surpassed human intelligence.

In the most optimistic scenario, AI helps companies produce more with the same resources, raising the country's economic output above its historical growth rate. Workers share in those gains, and unemployment stays close to the level economists associate with a healthy job market.

In the second scenario, AI still makes the economy grow faster, but businesses automate jobs more quickly than displaced workers can find new ones. Unemployment rises despite the productivity gains.

The third scenario assumes AI investments fail to deliver the returns Wall Street expects. Companies spend heavily on infrastructure, stock prices rise on expectations of huge profits, and those expectations eventually prove too optimistic.

“Equity valuations could fall, leading to an economic slowdown,” Warsh said. In that scenario, unemployment rises more sharply, and the economy eventually recovers without the promised productivity boost.

Those risks are already receiving attention elsewhere at the Fed. Governor Michael Barr warned Monday that the AI construction boom is increasingly financed through complex arrangements, including long-term computing leases, energy contracts and financial guarantees.

The Fed estimates that the potential maximum value of these off-balance-sheet obligations now exceeds $3 trillion. Those commitments are not the same as debt already owed, but they show why disappointing AI revenues could create problems beyond the technology companies building data centers.

Warsh did not propose an interest-rate change in Monday's remarks. The Fed's next policy meeting is scheduled for December 8 and 9.

Grayscale Head of Research Zach Pandl said crypto still has strong fundamental demand, which makes the current prices attractive entry points for mid-to-long-term investors.

Grayscale Head of Research Zach Pandl said current crypto prices represent “compelling entry points” for medium- and longer-term investors after a sharp decline in early November.

The Block's price data show that bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) fell about 13% over the past week and 20.8% over the past 30 days. The GMCI Index, which measures the performance of the top 30 cryptocurrencies by market capitalization, is down 13.8% in the past seven days. This aligns with Pandl's report that the decline in large-cap assets led to the current weakness.

Pandl attributed the drop to two primary factors rather than a single catalyst. The first was stablecoin outflows into fiat currencies, which accelerated after a major security incident at Binance at the end of last month.

"Some investors may be temporarily exiting digital assets for traditional bank-based systems due to high-profile security incidents," wrote Pandl, who added that he views GENIUS Act-compliant stablecoins such as USDC as low-risk.

The second driver was spillover from AI-related equities, including Nvidia. The report said growth portfolios that hold both crypto and tech stocks cut back on risk. It also noted that the two sectors share similar infrastructure needs, such as bitcoin miners providing computing power for AI firms.

"Therefore, a deterioration in the outlook for AI investment could have implications for Bitcoin infrastructure providers and Bitcoin itself," the report said.

Technical factors amplified the move, Pandl said. Bitcoin fell below its 200-day moving average on Nov. 12, a level that was used as a "sell signal" by trend-following funds. The decline also prompted liquidations of leveraged positions in perpetual futures. Outflows from crypto exchange-traded products did not appear extreme, according to the note.

Meanwhile, Grayscale said the pullback did not appear closely tied to the planned Anthropic initial public offering or to pressures on Strategy (MSTR). Its proprietary indicators showed valuations swinging rapidly from overbought to oversold, the report added.

"Admittedly, the macro market backdrop still looks challenging, due to uncertainty around AI investment after the midterms, high inflation and the prospect of more Fed rate hikes, and geopolitical conflict," Pandl said.

However, the researcher said fundamental trends for crypto — such as institutional adoption of stablecoins and tokenized assets — remain strong. Another positive signal in the report was the ongoing deficit spending, which creates continuous demand for alternative stores of value.

"We think digital asset fundamentals look solid, and current prices represent compelling entry points," Pandl wrote.

#4980. Kia EV7 revealed as an upcoming midsize electric SUV

Kia is crushing it with a full lineup of electric vehicles ranging from entry-level SUVs to the three-row EV9 and PV5 van. A leaked document revealed that Kia plans to add the EV7, a new midsize electric SUV, to fill the gap between the EV6 and the three-row EV9.

With 291,219 vehicles sold last month, up 10.3% from October 2025, Kia posted its eighth consecutive month of year-over-year sales growth.

Kia said it plans to build on the momentum by focusing on electrified vehicles in key markets, including hybrids in the US and fully electric vehicles in Europe.

After launching the EV2 earlier this year, Kia now offers one of the most complete EV lineups, including the EV2, EV3, EV4, EV5, EV6, EV9, and PV5 van.

According to a recently leaked document, a new midsize electric SUV will join the lineup. KoreanCarBlog uncovered an internal document from Kia’s Employment Stability Committee that reveals the company’s upcoming production plans.

The report claims “production of mid-range electric SUV ‘EV7’ to proceed” at Kia’s Gwangju factory in South Korea.

Although no other details were revealed, the EV7 will be a midsize electric SUV, slotted between the EV6 and three-row EV9.

During its 2026 CEO Investor Day in April, Kia announced plans to expand its EV lineup to 14 models by 2030.

In Europe, Kia plans for EVs to account for 66% of sales by 2030, with a full lineup that includes the EV2, EV3, EV4, EV5, EV6, EV9, three PBVs, a new C-segment electric SUV, and its first “SDV B-segment hatchback.”

Kia’s upcoming software-defined vehicles (SDVs) are expected to integrate Hyundai’s SDV architecture (CODA), Pleos Connect infotainment system, and Gleo AI, an in-vehicle Agenic AI.They will also have Level 2++ autonomous driving capabilities on highways.

Powered by an 81.4 kWh battery, the Kia’s current EV5 offers a WLTP range of up to 329 miles (530 km).

Not a full lineup, the EV van is still missing. Why offer so many different SUVs between Kia and Hyundai but no van/minivan?

However, unlike the EV6 and EV9, the EV5 uses a 400V electrical architecture rather than an 800V. As a result, charging the battery from 10% to 80% takes about 30 minutes, rather than 18 to 25 minutes. The upcoming EV7 will likely use the 800V system for faster charging.

The document also revealed Kia will begin production of “completely redesigned models” at its Gwangmyeong Factory, including the 2028 Carnival Hybrid and a hydrogen-powered Carnival by 2029.

At its Gyeonggi Hwaseong Factory, Kia will reportedly also produce redesigned models, including the Sorento Hybrid in 2028.

#4978. Binance hack may push institutions to outside custody, FalconX exec says

FalconX’s Joshua Lim says the Binance hack could push institutions to keep crypto off exchanges and change how large investors trade in 2027.

A major shift in institutional crypto trading could be on the horizon following the recent Binance hack, as security concerns may lead to more large investors keeping their assets outside exchanges, according to a FalconX executive.

In an exclusive interview with Cointelegraph at the Digital Asset Forum in New York City on Nov. 13, Joshua Lim, global head of markets at FalconX, a digital asset brokerage for institutional investors, said the Oct. 29 hack could make institutions more cautious about trading crypto and change how they store their assets.

“It’s actually quite bearish for more institutional participation in crypto,” Lim told Cointelegraph. He said traders initially played down the hack but later began to recognize its potential long-term impact.

As a result, Lim expects more institutions to turn to outside custody providers and trading firms that allow them to trade without keeping their crypto on exchanges, a shift he believes could become one of the biggest trends in 2027.

North Korean-linked hackers stole about $6.4 billion in cryptocurrency from Binance, making it the largest crypto theft on record, according to blockchain analytics firm Chainalysis.

The shift toward keeping crypto outside exchanges already appeared to be underway even before the hack. In an October proposal, the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) said industry practices were developing that reduced the need to move investment advisers’ client assets and regulated funds’ crypto holdings out of permitted custody for trading.

Lim pointed to Copper, a crypto custody provider, as one company that could benefit. Its ClearLoop service lets investors trade on supported exchanges while keeping their crypto with a separate company.

Copper said collateral protected by its ClearLoop service supported more than $240 billion in perpetual futures trading volume during the first quarter of 2026, more than double the amount a year earlier.

Lim explained that investors can keep their assets outside an exchange while receiving credited balances that allow them to trade on it. Under the brokerage model Lim described, a hedge fund can place trades through FalconX, which executes them using its own exchange accounts and relationships. Still, investors face risks tied to the firms holding their assets and settling their trades.

“For many players, facing a regulated entity like ours is more palatable,” Lim added.

Related: Crypto hack losses equal 57 cents per $1 earned onchain: Ark’s Valente

Binance recorded large Bitcoin outflows in mid-November, although it is not clear whether they were linked to the hack.

According to CryptoQuant data, Binance recorded net withdrawals of about 2,771 Bitcoin (BTC) on Friday, followed by 2,708 BTC on Saturday and 5,636 BTC on Sunday — totaling about 11,115 BTC leaving the exchange than entered over those three days.

The withdrawals came as the broader crypto market was falling, with Bitcoin trading around $65,000 at 9:41 pm UTC Monday, down about 20% over the past month, according to CoinGecko.

However, the data does not show who moved the Bitcoin or why. Investors could have transferred their funds to personal wallets, companies that store crypto on their behalf, or to other exchanges.

Beyond concerns about exchange security, Lim expects other changes in how large investors trade in 2027. He said institutions want more ways to trade assets such as oil, gold and stock market indexes through crypto platforms, including outside regular market hours.

“You can respond quickly to news events when they happen,” Lim said, citing one of the benefits of 24/7 trading.

That type of trading is already taking place on Hyperliquid, a decentralized trading platform that offers contracts tied to traditional assets. As of Monday, Hyperliquid’s markets for traditional assets and other contracts had about $4.3 billion in open interest and recorded $3.9 billion in trading volume over 24 hours, according to data from Perpys.

But while crypto platforms are giving investors more ways to trade, Lim said the financial services needed to support those trades have not kept up, particularly when it comes to borrowing money and managing risk.

For example, investors can now trade oil through traditional futures markets, investment funds and crypto platforms. But lenders may treat trades on each platform separately, even when those trades balance each other out.

“There’s not enough players that can give you leverage or margin financing against all of these things collectively,” Lim said. “The risk netting is not quite there yet.”

#4976. Trump Backs Jensen Huang’s Return to Nvidia as Sam Altman Says 'Heroes Have Flaws'

Trump Backs Jensen Huang’s Return to Nvidia as Sam Altman Says 'Heroes Have Flaws'

President Donald Trump joined Elon Musk on Monday in supporting Jensen Huang’s return as Nvidia CEO, while OpenAI chief Sam Altman expressed empathy for the ousted co-founder without endorsing his reinstatement.

“I would certainly not mind seeing that,” Trump told reporters Monday as he left the White House for Asia, according to Reuters. “He could return. There’s a big group of people that would like him to return.”

Trump said Huang had “learned a big lesson,” and questioned claims that Alice Huang was a Chinese spy. “Nobody’s proven that she’s a spy,” he said, per The Guardian.

Trump also dismissed concerns that his ties with Huang threatened national security and defended Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick amid questions over enforcement of AI-chip export controls.

Musk also said Friday he was signing a petition demanding Huang’s reinstatement.

“A petition is underway to reinstate Jensen and I’m signing it too,” Musk wrote on X. Epic Games boss Tim Sweeney and Replit CEO Amjad Masad are also listed among the supporters.

More than 182,000 people had signed the open letter by Monday. It seeks Huang’s return during an independent investigation, although signatories’ identities and affiliations are not independently verified.

Speaking at Semafor’s Silicon Valley & The World conference Monday, Altman said Huang had “long been a hero of mine. Heroes have flaws.” He described feeling “personal strong empathy” for the former Nvidia boss.

He has reason to understand a boardroom revolt. OpenAI fired Altman in November 2023, only to reinstate him less than five days later after employee and investor backlash. Still, asked whether Huang should return, Altman said: “I don’t think I know enough to answer that question.”

Altman said he struggled to imagine Nvidia without Huang, but argued the giant chipmaker was big enough to retain investor trust. That is sympathy—not an endorsement.

Huang, who founded Nvidia in 1993, stepped down Thursday after admitting infidelity. Reports later linked him to Alice Huang and two Nvidia employees, one a subordinate.

Alice Huang previously worked at Megaspeed, a Singapore-based Nvidia customer facing scrutiny over possible diversion of advanced chips to China. Megaspeed denies wrongdoing; espionage allegations remain unproven.

Washington is tightening export controls on advanced AI chips, including new licensing requirements for shipments to Singapore and Malaysia, as officials seek to prevent diversion to China.

Trump’s backing also puts him at odds with Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, who said Sunday that Nvidia should look outside its ranks for a replacement. “They need a dramatic shake-up,” Bessent told Fox News.

Nvidia’s board has made no decision to reinstate Huang. CFO Colette Kress is serving as interim CEO, while the Financial Times reports Huang’s allies are lobbying for his return and Arm CEO Rene Haas is a potential successor.

Employees have also pushed for reinstatement. An internal discussion drew more than 1,600 comments, including calls for Huang’s return.

Nvidia shares opened 2.7% lower Monday, wiping out roughly $100 billion in market value at the open.

Huang could face congressional questions on China chip exports in early December. Nvidia reports earnings Tuesday, its first financial update under Kress.

#4973. Puerto Rico tapped Sunrun’s home batteries for 18 straight days

Sunrun has doubled participation in its Puerto Rico virtual power plant to more than 60,000 customers, and the grid is putting those home batteries to work: It tapped into them for 18 straight days in October.

A virtual power plant (VPP) connects batteries across thousands of properties and coordinates them to supply power together.

Sunrun says its VPP network in Puerto Rico now includes nearly 70,000 batteries, frequently delivering 45 megawatts (MW) for four hours at a time. It has dispatched them more than 60 times since April, including daily deployments beginning October 13 during unusually hot weather.

For Puerto Ricans, who pay some of the highest electricity prices in the US, getting reliable power for all that money remains a problem. Residential electricity averaged 26.36 cents per kilowatt-hour in August, compared with a US average of 18.16 cents, according to the US Energy Information Administration (EIA).

That makes the batteries already installed at homes pretty useful. The grid can pay to tap their stored energy when supplies get tight, putting existing equipment to work without waiting for a new power plant to be built.

Sunrun’s network grew from around 4,000 customers in 2024 to more than 30,000 in 2025. It says it’s the largest aggregator in Puerto Rico’s Customer Battery Energy Sharing (CBES) program.

LUMA, the island’s grid operator, calls on CBES when it expects electricity demand or reserve needs to exceed available generation. Companies such as Sunrun then manage the battery response automatically.

Homeowners get paid for sharing energy and choose how much to keep for backup. Sunrun says most share a small portion of their total battery capacity; the company earns recurring revenue for operating the network.

Across the wider CBES program, LUMA reported in July that more than 90,000 participating customers had helped provide an average of 75 MW during generation shortfalls.

Puerto Rico’s reliability problem goes well beyond hurricanes.

Customers averaged 36 hours without power in 2025, even excluding major events, according to the US EIA. That’s up 19% from 2024. Mainland US customers generally experience about two hours a year under the same measure. Major events added another 23 hours of outages on the island.

More batteries can help, but the size of the shortfall still matters. In an April regulatory filing, participating aggregators said the program couldn’t prevent every rolling blackout in 2025 because some supply gaps exceeded its roughly 50 MW of four-hour capacity. They’re looking to enroll more batteries so the program can cover larger shortfalls.

#4972. Former Robinhood engineer pleads guilty to wire fraud over trades ahead of crypto listing announcements

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Xiang admitted to using confidential information about planned Robinhood Crypto listings and agreed to forfeit $125,523 in trading proceeds.

Huaisong Xiang, a former Robinhood Robinhood Markets, Inc. (HOOD) software engineer, pleaded guilty to wire fraud after using confidential information about planned cryptocurrency listings to trade perpetual futures on Hyperliquid.

Xiang agreed to forfeit $125,523 in proceeds from the scheme as part of his plea, the U.S. Attorney’s Office for the Southern District of New York said in a statement on Monday.

According to the statement, Xiang made the trades between 2025 and 2026, ahead of Robinhood's public announcements that those tokens would be listed on Robinhood Crypto.

Prosecutors said Xiang knew about the planned listings before each trade but used that information for his own benefit, making $125,523 in the process.

Xiang was charged in September alongside former Robinhood engineer Hefu Chai. Prosecutors alleged the two separately used confidential information about planned listings to trade token perpetuals on Hyperliquid, with each making more than $50,000.

According to the criminal complaints, Robinhood’s policy barred designated employees from trading on any platform before and for 24 hours after the company publicly announced a new crypto listing or delisting.

Xiang initially denied the charges. His attorney told Forbes in September that his client disputed the allegations. Robinhood told Bloomberg it had investigated the matter and reported it to law enforcement and regulators.

“Robinhood takes market integrity seriously and has zero tolerance for insider trading,” the company said at the time, adding that it had policies covering new crypto listings and would continue cooperating with investigators.

Xiang is scheduled to be sentenced on Feb. 25, 2027. The wire fraud count carries a maximum sentence of 20 years in prison.

#4969. Lighter reopens withdrawals after trader’s API key leak, returns about $5 million in USDC

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Transfers into Lighter's public pools now require a wallet signature, and API key signatures have been made non-public.

Lighter's Security Council has returned about $5 million in USDC to a trader whose API keys were compromised on Monday, the exchange said in a postmortem.

The Ethereum-based perpetual futures exchange initially reported a suspected compromise involving one external party and suspended withdrawals and pool creation as a precaution. It later identified a leaked API key for a specific trader's account and said recovering funds from an additional withdrawal attempt was its priority. The team expected to complete contract upgrades and reopen withdrawals by the end of Monday. In its postmortem, Lighter said the anomaly had triggered its smart contract Guardian, whose temporary pause on withdrawals allowed the Security Council to return the funds.

"Deposits and withdrawals are back to normal following the deactivation of Guardian Mode by the Lighter Security Council," Lighter announced later that day.

Lighter Lighter (LIT) did not disclose the total size of the theft or the name of the affected trader, though onchain analysts Lookonchain and Onchain Lens estimated losses at $15.5 million and suggested that the affected account belonged to Abraxas Capital.

According to Lighter's post-mortem report, the attacker opened a public pool from the compromised account, then minted shares in a separate account they controlled. They then transferred the trader's money into the pool without minting more shares, increasing the value of the shares the attacker held, which they redeemed before withdrawing, Lighter said.

Lighter said the API keys were leaked through the account's own signatures, using signing software that came from outside its official software development kits, and reused nonces, the one-time values required for signatures. This meant that anyone could calculate the private key from two public signatures if both used the same nonce and key, per the report. Lighter said its official SDKs, protocol, smart contracts, and infrastructure were not affected.

Following the incident, Lighter said an API key alone can no longer authorize a transfer to a public pool. Lighter now requires a wallet signature and has made API key signatures non-public. The team said it was also adding IP allowlists and permissions for individual keys, including restrictions on which markets a key can trade.

#4968. Xiaomi’s humanoid robots work a full 8-hour shift in its EV factory

Xiaomi has released footage of its humanoid robots working a full 8-hour shift at its EV factory, sorting center console side covers for its cars. The company says the robots did it on their own, catching and fixing their own mistakes along the way.

If that’s fully autonomous, it’s truly impressive. But we’ve been disappointed by humanoid robot demos before, and Xiaomi left out the numbers that would let anyone check.

Xiaomi Tech posted the video on X on November 3, as the company picked up awards at the World Internet Conference in Wuzhen. It shows the robots pulling side covers from bins, carrying them to storage racks, and placing them into slots, with a running clock of cumulative working time and an “Autonomous, 100x Speed” label in the corner.

8 hours on the production line. 🤖Xiaomi’s full-size humanoid robot takes on an 8-hour task at the Center Console Side-Cover Sorting Workstation at Xiaomi Auto Factory.Beyond sustained operation, it can autonomously detect and correct anomalies while coordinating with the… pic.twitter.com/brpO3S5bCn

According to Xiaomi, the robot covered the full day of production needs at that workstation. The company says it detected and corrected anomalies on its own and coordinated with the production line’s control system, calling it “another step toward real-world industrial deployment.”

This is the third milestone in a program Xiaomi has been running in public all year. In March, it put a humanoid in its Beijing EV factory as an “intern” installing self-tapping nuts. Xiaomi reported a 90.2% success rate over three hours, fast enough to meet the station’s 76-second cycle time. By July, it said that success rate was up to 98%, and it added two harder stations, including the side-cover sorting job from this week’s video.

Last December, Xiaomi’s CEO, Lei Jun, said the company plans to deploy humanoid robots in large numbers across its factories within five years.

Xiaomi also announced that its open-source Xiaomi-Robotics-1 AI model, pre-trained on 100,000 hours of real-world robot data, has started trial runs in Xiaomi staff apartments. In a 30-minute run, it completed more than 50 task chains across three rooms.

Xiaomi didn’t say how many parts the robots sorted, what the success rate was over eight hours, or how many times a human had to step in. It also didn’t explain how the robots stayed powered for a full shift: swapped batteries, a tether, or charging breaks.

And that matters, because “autonomous” in a robot video can mean anything. Tesla’s Optimus robots at the 2024 “We, Robot” event were remotely operated by humans, which Tesla didn’t disclose at the time. Last December, an Optimus took a suspicious tumble in a Miami demo, raising its hands to its head like a teleoperator pulling off a VR headset as it fell.

To be fair to Xiaomi, it has published success rates and cycle times for its earlier factory tests. That’s more than Tesla has ever shared about Optimus doing useful work, and Tesla now says it won’t deliver Optimus to outside customers until late 2027.

The most interesting moment in the video comes around the 1:37 mark. In a segment Xiaomi labels “Dropped Material: Multi-Robot Collaboration,” a humanoid upper body mounted on a wheeled base rolls in to help the two legged robots.

It’s not the only thing on wheels in that scene, either. The bins of parts are shuttled around by autonomous mobile robots, the same kind of wheeled platforms that have been moving materials in car factories for years.

They could have got a lot more done if they'd moved the parts bins closer so the bots didn't have to walk as far. That's a huge open space between the two.

If this demo was truly fully autonomous for eight hours, with the robots handling their own errors, that’s one of the more impressive humanoid demos we’ve seen. Xiaomi deserves credit for putting its robots on a real production line and publishing some real numbers along the way. But we’ve been burned by “autonomous” robot demos before, so we’ll wait for more details before getting too excited.

The wheeled robot is our favorite part of the video, though. It’s a great example of why you shouldn’t bet everything on the humanoid form factor. Wheels are far more efficient than legs on most terrain, and a flat factory floor is the best-case scenario for wheels. A wheeled base doesn’t burn energy just to stay upright, it’s more stable when carrying parts, it has room for a much bigger battery (which would solve that 8-hour power question), and it needs fewer expensive actuators.

The value in these robots is in the hands, arms, and the AI driving them. Legs make sense in homes with stairs, and maybe that’s where Xiaomi wants to take them eventually. But factories were designed around carts and forklifts, not people walking around carrying parts.

As robots and AI put more strain on the grid and electricity rates climb, solar and batteries are becoming essential home infrastructure, not a luxury. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. #4967. German lawmakers back crypto freezes outside criminal cases

Customs authorities could hold “suspicious” assets for 30 days under the bill, with court oversight for extensions and disputed confiscation.

Germany’s lower house of parliament has passed a bill that would let customs authorities temporarily freeze cryptocurrency and other high-value assets suspected of having unlawful origins without first opening a criminal investigation.

The Bundestag passed the customs reform bill on Friday as part of a wider effort to combat money laundering and organized crime. The proposed procedure would target assets worth more than 100,000 euros and explicitly mentions cryptocurrencies.

Under the bill’s text approved by lawmakers, authorities would be able to hold assets for up to 30 days where there are factual indications of unlawful origins, according to a translated version. However, filing an application for confiscation could suspend those deadlines pending a court decision, and in contested cases, a court would have to be satisfied that the assets were of unlawful origin before ordering confiscation, effectively putting the burden of proof on the crypto holder.

German Social Democratic Party lawmaker Ingo Vogel said that the powers would allow authorities to pursue suspicious assets even when there are not yet grounds to open a criminal case, according to the Bundestag’s summary of the debate on the bill.

The bill still requires consent from the Bundesrat, Germany’s upper house of parliament, and is intended to take effect on Jan. 1, 2027.

Germany’s Federal Bar Association, known as BRAK, previously opposed the government’s draft in an Oct. 28 statement, warning that the meaning of unlawful origin is too vague and risks treating missing records as evidence of illicit wealth.

The association pointed to assets inherited through generations as one example where owners may lack a paper trail. It also questioned what the administrative process would add to Germany’s existing criminal confiscation rules.

German blockchain industry association Bundesblock also raised concerns about the treatment of digital assets in a March 31 submission on an earlier draft. It warned at the time that blockchain analysis should not automatically be treated as proof of ownership or control, and that incorrect wallet attribution could even infringe fundamental rights.

Related: Crypto adoption blooming in Germany, while UK is falling ‘behind,’ says CoinShares researcher

Germany has already carried out large crypto seizures under existing law. As Cointelegraph reported, Saxony authorities sold 49,858 Bitcoin (BTC) linked to the Movie2k piracy investigation in 2024, raising about 2.6 billion euros, or $2.8 billion. At the time, prosecutors said the proceeds remained secured pending a court ruling on confiscation.

In a recent development, local outlet MDR reported on Friday that a Leipzig judge proposed ending proceedings against the main defendant in exchange for 40,000 BTC and a payment of 38 million euros for the potential victims. The proposal remains under consideration.

#4966. Clarity Act Defeat ‘Failure of American Leadership’: Trump’s Former Crypto Chief

Clarity Act Defeat ‘Failure of American Leadership’: Trump’s Former Crypto Chief

The Senate's failure to pass the Clarity Act was "a failure of American leadership," according to Patrick Witt, President Donald Trump's former top crypto adviser.

"It was extremely disappointing," Witt said in an on-stage interview at Solana's Breakpoint conference in London. "I was knee deep in Clarity. I lived it for a year plus negotiating that bill," he noted, adding that the bill, "would have been incredibly impactful."

Witt served as executive director of the President's Council of Advisers for Digital Assets until last month and led the White House push for the bill, which would have set out a framework for regulating crypto markets. It failed a key procedural vote in the Senate in September.

"We ultimately lost against the clock," he said, adding that, "The policy was good. We resolved pretty much all of the policy differences." But with the midterm elections approaching, "the political issue of ethics really consumed everything else," he said.

Democrats had raised concerns over the Trump family's crypto ventures. Witt acknowledged that Trump's involvement in crypto was "arguably the issue," but said the president had agreed to two ethics provisions. The second would have barred federal officials, including the president, from issuing or sponsoring digital assets, forced the president to divest certain digital assets or put them in a blind trust, and let state attorneys general enforce violations. "There's no precedent for those kinds of things in American law," he said.

The GENIUS stablecoin bill also lost an initial procedural vote in 2025 but recovered because it came early in Trump's term, Witt noted. With Clarity, "we knew that we were going to get one shot at it, and unfortunately we just came up short," he said.

Looking ahead, Witt said the odds of passing a comprehensive replacement are low. "I would expect that market structure in the United States is going to be less written on Capitol Hill and more written in the Federal Register, which is where the agencies do their rulemakings and issue their guidance," Witt said.

Parts of the Clarity Act could still pass in larger legislative packages, and lawmakers could grant targeted powers, such as giving the Commodity Futures Trading Commission authority over spot crypto markets, he said.

Witt said regulators already have "ample authority," and that the administration had to "hold them back a little bit" while the bill played out. The SEC and CFTC have since moved ahead with their own crypto rules, though House Financial Services Committee Chairman French Hill (R-AR) has said those efforts fall short of what the legislation would have achieved.

SEC Chair Paul Atkins and CFTC Chair Michael Selig "are committed to moving markets on chain," Witt said, framing the issue as one of American competitiveness. "Capital is going to go where it's treated best," he said.

Despite Democrats winning control of both chambers of Congress in this month's midterms, Witt said he is "not bearish on the industry overall," since the regulators and the president remain in place. Democrats are reportedly planning to push their own, broader market structure bill.

He said opposition to crypto is increasingly generational rather than purely partisan. While some Democrats, such as Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-MA), are "going to be 100% opposed to anything that seemingly is favorable to crypto," younger lawmakers on both sides see it as "part of the fabric of financial infrastructure," he said, pointing to Sens. Angela Alsobrooks (D-MD) and Ruben Gallego (D-AZ).

Witt left the administration in October, The Hill reported. He said he has held talks with crypto firms, venture capital firms and defense companies, and hopes to announce his next move in the coming weeks.

#4964. Grvt launches RWA perpetuals with funding tied to traditional markets

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Market makers submit funding rates based on their financing costs in traditional markets, and Grvt adds a markup.

Grvt, a hybrid exchange that runs on its own ZKsync-based Ethereum Layer 2, has launched perpetual contracts tied to real-world assets that link funding rates to market makers’ financing costs in traditional markets.

Dubbed Stable Funding Perps, the product initially covers seven commodity markets, with funding settled in USDC once each weekday at market close, according to a statement shared with The Block.

Rates vary for long and short positions and adjust for holidays and weekend financing, the platform said, adding that traders can pay or receive funding depending on prevailing rates.

Grvt GRVT Token (GRVT) said the contracts use a request-for-quote model where market makers provide live buy and sell quotes while managing their hedges through traditional financial markets. The platform charges no trading fee, while execution costs are added to quoted prices.

The initial rollout carries larger minimum trade sizes, although there are plans to enable 24/7 availability and support smaller minimums over time, per the statement. Most contracts trade about 23 hours a day, five days a week, following the CME Globex calendar and market holidays to align with underlying reference pricing.

"We don't believe every onchain market needs to recreate liquidity that already exists elsewhere," Grvt CEO Hong Yea said in the statement. "If market makers can hedge more efficiently in established markets, they can pass those efficiencies on through better pricing and deeper liquidity."

Grvt raised $19 million in Series A funding in September 2025 as it expanded its platform into areas including options and real-world assets.

In August, the platform also announced a plan to build a $100 million position in Ondo Finance's USDY, a tokenized secured note backed primarily by short-term U.S. Treasurys.

Standard Chartered said Monday that the RWA market, currently about $45 billion, could reach $2 trillion by the end of 2028, with longer-dated government bonds providing another potential source of growth.

Grvt plans to expand SFPs into equities and other RWA markets, with an eventual target of more than 100 markets, according to the statement.

#4962. BYD’s new luxury electric SUV has one big problem: It weighs over 8,200 lbs

BYD’s new full-size electric SUV, the Denza N9 EV, was revealed in a regulatory filing, weighing in at over 8,200 lbs (3,760 kg). That creates one issue.

The Denza N9 first went on sale last March, a flagship plug-in hybrid (PHEV) SUV with a combined CLTC range of about 807 miles (1,300 km) on a full charge and full tank of gas.

BYD introduced the updated Flash Charging version earlier this year, which uses its second-generation Blade Batteries and delivers a pure electric CLTC range of 260 miles (420 km). Combined, it gets up to 944 miles (1,520 km) under CLTC conditions.

In the latest batch of new energy vehicles (NEVs) approved for sale by China’s MIIT (Ministry of Information Technology) this week, a new all-electric Denza N9 appeared.

The filing revealed the Denza N9 EV will be available with three electric motors, delivering a combined output of 1,193 horsepower (890 kW).

A badge on the back reveals it will use BYD’s tri-motor e3 platform. The setup features a single 362 horsepower (270 kW) front motor and two 415 horsepower (310 kW) rear electric motors.

The electric version measures 5,270 mm long, 2,030 mm wide, and 1,830 mm tall, with a 3,125 mm wheelbase, which is the same as the current Denza N9 PHEV Flash Charging model. It will be available in five- and six-seat configurations.

While the filing reveals it will use a lithium iron phosphate (LFP) battery with cells from BYD’s battery unit, FinDreams, it does not provide specifics like capacity or driving range.

One relatively big thing to note: the Denza N9 EV has a gross weight of 3,760 kg, or about 8,289 lbs.

Obscene weight , and that applies to other SUVs as well. Vehicle registration taxes should be based on vehicle weight as it wears out roads and endangers other road users and infrastructure and wastes resources.

As AutoNext points out, this makes it too heavy for an EU category B driving license, which covers up to 3,500 kg (7,716 lbs).

Last year, the EU updated the rules, allowing drivers who have held a category B license for two years to drive alternative-fuelled vehicles with a maximum weight of up to 4,250 kg (9,369 lbs).

While the Denza N9 EV would qualify as listed, newer drivers would still need a commercial EU category C1 driving license to drive it.

BYD launched its luxury sub-brand, Denza, in Europe and the UK in March, starting with the Z9 GT and the D9 van. More recently, it introduced the Bao 5 PHEV SUV and the Denza Z electric supercar.

#4961. Stablecoin infrastructure firm Avenia raises $25 million in Series A extension

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Lightspeed Faction led the equity round, bringing Avenia's total funding to $44.6 million as it plans new products and acquisitions.

Avenia, a Brazilian stablecoin infrastructure firm, has raised $25 million in a Series A extension round as it plans to launch new products and looks for acquisitions.

"The terms made a lot of sense, so we opened an extension of $25 million," Noel said.

The extension follows a $17 million round announced in February, bringing funding this year to $42 million. Including its pre-seed and seed rounds, Avenia has raised $44.6 million since its founding in 2023, Noel said.

He declined to disclose the valuation, but said it was "a very significant step-up from our previous round."

Founded as BRLA Digital and based in São Paulo, Avenia lets businesses receive, convert, hold, and send funds between local currencies and stablecoins through an API or dashboard. It also issues BRLA, a stablecoin backed by the Brazilian real. The company reports 7 billion reais (about $1.2 billion) in monthly transaction volume for BRLA.

Most of Avenia's clients are in Brazil, but the company supports payouts to more than 100 countries, Noel said. It uses payment systems including SWIFT, ACH, wire transfers, and SEPA internationally, and Pix, TED, and boleto in Brazil.

Its clients include companies managing international treasury operations; investment advisers, brokerages, securities distributors, wealth managers and family offices; and banks and financial institutions seeking technology to offer virtual asset services, Noel said.

Products under development include merchant acquiring, a product offering returns on Brazilian real balances, and a Visa card program. Merchant acquiring is due to launch this year. The yield product will start with four existing clients, while the card is in the final design approval stage with Visa, Noel said.

"They're all at an advanced stage," he noted. "There's more on the way, but we'd rather share it as each one goes live."

Avenia is allowed to operate as a virtual asset service provider while its application is under review under Brazil's central bank Resolution 520, Noel said. Its final license is still pending.

New products require regulatory approval, making licenses part of Avenia's acquisition plans, Noel said. It is looking at payment institutions, including electronic money issuers, as well as brokerages, securities distributors known as DTVMs, and possibly banks. Companies licensed elsewhere in Latin America or the U.S. could also be targets, he added.

"The idea is complementarity, not competition: to verticalize Avenia and expand the products we can offer," he said.

Avenia currently has 43 employees, almost three times its headcount at the start of the year, and expects to reach 50 by year-end, Noel said.

#4958. Germany’s EV share hits record 37% as BEVs beat gas and diesel combined

Battery-electric cars took a record 37% of Germany’s new car market in October, according to data released today by the Kraftfahrt-Bundesamt (KBA), the country’s federal motor vehicle authority. That’s 94,086 BEVs, up 79.5% from 52,425 a year earlier.

For the second month in a row, German buyers registered more fully electric cars than gasoline and diesel cars combined. And with two months left in the year, Germany has already blown past the auto industry’s EV forecast for all of 2026.

The overall market barely grew in October. Total new car registrations rose 1.7% to 254,492 units, which means the EV growth came straight out of combustion cars.

Gasoline car registrations crashed 40.5% to 38,516 units, a 15.1% share. Diesel fell 21.6% to 23,882 units, or 9.4% of the market. Add them up and pure combustion cars made up just 24.5% of new registrations, 12.5 points less than BEVs alone.

Plug-in hybrids slipped 7.9% to 28,499 units (11.2%). So plug-ins accounted for about 48% of the German market last month, and the average CO2 emissions of new cars dropped 21.1% year-over-year to 79.4 g/km.

As we reported this summer, BEVs became the most popular powertrain in Germany for the first time in June with a 28.4% share. They’ve kept climbing every month since: 32.4% in August, 34.5% in September, and now 37% in October.

Through the first ten months of the year, Germany has registered 698,230 BEVs, up 60.7% year-over-year. That’s about 28.2% of the 2,476,290 new cars registered in 2026 so far, up from roughly 19% for the full year 2025.

And the industry didn’t see it coming. In December, the VDA, Germany’s auto industry association, forecast about 693,000 BEV registrations for all of 2026. Germany passed that in October. At the current pace, it’s on track for somewhere around 875,000 by the end of the year.

The biggest driver is Germany’s new EV incentive, which applies retroactively to cars registered since January 1. It pays €3,000 for a new BEV, bought or leased, and up to €6,000 for lower-income households with children. Households with taxable income above €80,000 (€90,000 with kids) don’t qualify.

The program targets private buyers, and that’s exactly where the KBA data shows the jump. Private registrations rose 20.6% in October, while commercial registrations fell 8.2%. Private buyers now make up 40.7% of the market.

Germany has been here before. The government killed its previous EV incentive overnight in December 2023, and BEV sales fell off a cliff in 2024. This time, the money is going to the buyers who need it most, and it’s moving the needle fast.

Tesla’s German numbers swing wildly with its quarterly delivery cycle. In September, the end of the quarter, it registered 12,552 cars (+268.7%) and the Model Y was the best-selling car in Germany. In October, it registered only 2,932, which is still up 290.9% but from a tiny base, for a 1.2% market share.

Year-to-date, Tesla is up 206.1% to 47,742 registrations. That’s a big rebound from a terrible 2025, but BYD is now slightly ahead with 47,961 (+216.1%). BYD registered 5,173 cars in October (+54.3%), though the majority of its German sales are plug-in hybrids, not BEVs.

The other Chinese brands are growing even faster. Leapmotor registered 3,661 cars in October, up 288.6%, and is at 19,705 for the year. Xpeng was up 284.4% to 1,380 units, and SAIC’s MG was up 48.5% to 4,140.

Volkswagen Group still owns the top of the EV charts, though. Through September, the Skoda Elroq was Germany’s best-selling EV with 30,845 units, ahead of the Tesla Model Y (29,464) and the VW ID.3 (24,352).

The EV boom is happening during one of the worst years in the history of Germany’s auto industry.

In October, the VW brand’s German registrations fell 4%, Audi dropped 8.4%, Porsche lost 5.7%, and Opel crashed 24.1%. BMW was flat (+0.1%), and Mercedes was the exception, up 8.5%. VW is still by far the biggest brand in Germany with an 18.2% share, but it’s down 5.6% for the year.

Last week, Volkswagen Group reported a €5.9 billion operating loss for the third quarter, its biggest quarterly loss since Dieselgate, driven by a writedown of its Porsche stake, weak sales in China, and thin EV margins. The group has already approved a plan to cut 100,000 jobs and close several German plants. Mercedes cut its 2026 car margin guidance to 2-3% last week, mainly because of China.

It’s not a product problem. VW’s new ID. Polo is sold out with over 30,000 orders, BMW has over 50,000 orders for the Neue Klasse iX3, and the Elroq leads the German EV market.

Germany is proving what we’ve been saying for years. Give buyers EVs they can actually afford, and they switch fast. Fully electric cars went from about 19% of the market last year to 37% in October, and the country beat the industry’s full-year forecast with two months to spare. That’s not a slow transition.

One thing to consider when talking about EVs not being Germany-made is that Germany car makers can't make them fast enough. A lot of the models are sold out for months. It's not an excuse but something to keep in mind.

Compare that with North America, where EV sales dropped 44% after the US killed its federal EV tax credit. Same technology, sometimes the same cars. The difference is policy.

Germany’s problem is who’s capturing the demand. Hint: it’s not German automakers.

But whose fault is that? You can’t spend years dragging your feet on electrification and lobby government back and forth on EV incentives and then complain when those who are all-in on EVs are able to take better advantage of the customer shift amid another oil crisis.

If you’re buying an EV, powering it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. #4957. Bernstein sees upside for bitcoin miners Riot and TeraWulf, citing discounts to implied AI data center values

Bernstein put Riot's discount at about 50% and TeraWulf's at 22% in an assessment of the bitcoin miners' data center contracts.

Applying that rate to its annual earnings estimates implies values of about $25.9 billion for Riot and $17.7 billion for TeraWulf, while their enterprise values, including expected additional borrowing, totaled $13.1 billion and $13.8 billion, respectively.

#4955. Tom Lee Blames Nvidia and Anthropic IPO for Ethereum Plunge as Bitmine Buys the Dip

Tom Lee Blames Nvidia and Anthropic IPO for Ethereum Plunge as Bitmine Buys the Dip

Bitmine Immersion Technologies kept buying Ethereum through a punishing selloff, adding 7,653 ETH worth roughly $14 million, as chairman Tom Lee argued the token’s steep decline runs counter to improving fundamentals and echoes a dip that preceded a major tech IPO earlier this year.

The NYSE-listed company said Monday its holdings reached 6,077,745 ETH, worth about $11.1 billion at a reference price of $1,823 per coin, far below where the firm’s stack was valued just weeks ago.

That marks a stark drop in paper value: Bitmine’s total crypto, cash and “moonshot” holdings now stand at $11.8 billion, down from $14.3 billion a week earlier, almost entirely because of ETH’s price slide rather than any selling.

The position still represents 4.97% of Ethereum’s 122.2 million token supply, leaving Bitmine 99% of the way to its goal of owning 5% of the network, the target it calls the “Alchemy of 5%.”

Lee did not sugarcoat the move. “ETH tumbled 28% in the past two weeks,” he said, calling the “waterfall decline” inconsistent with what he views as positive fundamentals, including banks and financial institutions announcing tokenization projects built on Ethereum. (Disclosure: Tom Lee is an investor in Dastan, Decrypt’s parent company.)

He pointed to external pressures weighing on the price, including the fallout from Nvidia’s turmoil triggering margin selling in Asia and investors raising cash ahead of Anthropic’s initial public offering, whose pricing is reported for Nov. 18.

Lee also drew a historical parallel, arguing the drop mirrors the sell-off ahead of SpaceX’s $75 billion June IPO, when ETH fell 39% over eight weeks and bottomed three trading days before pricing. Lee suggested the current slump, down a similar amount ahead of the Anthropic listing, could follow the same pattern.

Bitmine remains the world’s largest Ethereum treasury and the second-largest crypto treasury overall, behind Michael Saylor’s Strategy, which now holds 849,327 BTC. Strategy also kept buying through the downturn, with Saylor nudging the market to rally with the quip “this dip could use a little orange.”

Bitmine has 5,227,037 ETH, about 86% of its holdings, staked through its MAVAN platform, with projected annualized revenue of roughly $245 million at current levels.

It also holds 216 Bitcoin, $448 million in cash and marketable securities, a $180 million stake in Beast Industries and a $67 million position in Eightco Holdings.

#4954. Custodia Bank loses Supreme Court bid for direct Fed payment access

The Supreme Court refused to hear Custodia Bank’s appeal, meaning a lower court ruling allowing the Fed to reject account applications will stand.

The United States Supreme Court on Monday declined to hear an appeal from crypto-focused bank Custodia, effectively ending the bank’s latest legal effort to gain direct access to the Federal Reserve’s payment system.

Custodia is a Wyoming-chartered bank founded by Caitlin Long. The bank has been trying for a Fed master account since 2020, which would allow it to process payments directly without relying on third-party financial institutions.

Now, the Supreme Court’s Monday decision means an earlier ruling allowing the Fed to reject applications from eligible banks will remain in effect.

The case highlights some of the challenges crypto-facing banks face in gaining direct access to the US payment system. Still, even if state-chartered banks qualify for master accounts, the Fed does not have to approve every application.

In a dissent issued in March, US Circuit Judge Timothy Tymkovich criticized the appeals court’s decision to give Federal Reserve banks discretion over master accounts.

“Without a master account, a bank cannot operate in the modern banking system,” Tymkovich wrote, arguing that the ruling effectively gives the Fed the power to override state banking decisions.

Custodia first sued the Federal Reserve in 2022 after waiting around two years for a decision on its master account application. The Federal Reserve Bank of Kansas City ended up rejecting the request in January 2023, citing concerns about risks linked to the bank’s crypto-focused business model.

In October 2025, a federal appeals court ruled against Custodia, finding that Federal Reserve banks did not have to grant master accounts to every eligible institution.

Related: Crypto among sectors ‘debanked’ by 9 major banks: US regulator

Cointelegraph previously reported that the court declined to reconsider its decision in March. This led Custodia to take its case to the Supreme Court.

The bank’s appeal also received support from the Blockchain Association, which argued that the Fed’s ability to reject applications could weaken the authority of state banking regulators.

Senator Cynthia Lummis and former Senator Patrick Toomey also filed a brief with the Supreme Court in August, supporting Custodia’s appeal.

Today’s decision comes as other crypto-focused banks seek access to the Fed’s payment system. In March, Kraken Financial received approval for a restricted account from the Federal Reserve Bank of Kansas City — the same regional bank that rejected Custodia’s application.

Notably, Kraken’s account was approved for an initial one-year period and allows it to send and receive payments through the Fed’s Fedwire system. However, the account has restrictions and does not offer all the services of a full master account.

Looking ahead, Custodia may still be able to apply for another master account or seek limited access to the Fed’s payment system under a separate proposal.

Bitwise Asset Management launched the actively managed Bitwise AI Cyber Defense ETF, which invests in companies developing defenses against AI-enabled cyberattacks and tools to secure AI systems. The fund marks the crypto asset manager's first exchange-traded product outside of digital assets.

The fund began trading on the NYSE under the ticker ZDAY. It carries a 0.75% management fee and targets 10 to 30 holdings, including Palo Alto Networks, CrowdStrike, Cloudflare, and Zscaler.

"Crypto has lived at the leading edge of cybersecurity since its inception," Bitwise CIO Matt Hougan said in a release. "We've watched a multibillion-dollar security industry assemble around it in five years. The same thing is now happening to AI, on a much larger scale."

The growing security risks associated with AI also helped shape Bitwise's decision to launch the fund, with the company pointing to a July incident involving Hugging Face as an example.

#4951. Range Rover is axing its two cheapest SUVs, making way for a new entry-level EV

For decades, the Range Rover name was viewed as a status symbol, mostly used by wealthy buyers, high-powered executives, and even royalty.
