In the US, the 2026 Range Rover Evoque starts at $49,900, and the Velar starts at $61,600. That’s considerably less than the Sport and flagship Range Rover SUVs, which start at $83,700 and $107,400, respectively.

The Range Rover GT is expected to start at around $90,000 with an EPA range of at least 300 miles when it goes on sale as a 2028 model. In the UK, it will likely be over £75,000 ($100,000).

Range Rover’s flagship electric SUV starts at $140,450 in the US, and the smaller Sport EV will likely be a bit cheaper at around $120,000 to $130,000.

At $90,000, the electric GT isn’t what most would consider an “entry-level” vehicle, but it’s part of Range Rover’s push into higher-end, more profitable models.

#4950. Hackers Redirected Bitcoin Miner Rewards via AI Attack, Luxor CEO Says

Hackers Redirected Bitcoin Miner Rewards via AI Attack, Luxor CEO Says

Hackers briefly hijacked computing power from Bitcoin miners using Luxor’s fleet management software, in what the company’s CEO called an AI-enabled attack.

Luxor Technology said Sunday that its internal monitoring flagged an unauthorized redirect of hashrate through Commander, its tool for managing mining machines.

Hashrate is the computing power miners devote to securing Bitcoin and earning rewards. Just over a dozen client accounts were affected, with their machines pointed to a different mining pool for a limited window, effectively sending the value of their work elsewhere.

Luxor said it patched Commander within two hours and began restoring affected machines to their original settings. No data was leaked or taken from its systems, and the issue was isolated to Commander, according to the company.

Luxor Pool and its LuxOS software weren’t affected, the company said, adding that every affected client will be paid in full for the redirected hashrate, as if their machines had mined normally.

Luxor CEO Nick Hansen said the compromise began around 6 p.m. Pacific time Saturday through “a sophisticated AI-enabled vector.” He didn’t detail how the attack worked. “You trust us with your fleet, and we need to be better,” he wrote on X.

Around 6:00p PDT yesterday, a subset of Commander customers were compromised, through a sophisticated AI-enabled vector. We were able to identify and patch quickly so that it didn't continue, then the hard work of reverting started.…

In a follow-up post, Hansen said crypto businesses are now “inundated with AI-enabled attacks,” with abliterated models, or AI models stripped of their safety guardrails, probing systems around the clock for weaknesses.

Luxor has responded by buying its own bare-metal GPUs to run such models defensively. It has also put most new feature work on hold to harden its infrastructure by rotating keys, updating kernels and pinning dependencies. Hansen urged other companies running similar services to do the same.

Bitcoin developers are grappling with the same threat. Bitcoin Core updated its security policy to address AI-driven threats, and an AI “red team” effort has turned up critical vulnerabilities throughout the year.

The incident also follows other attacks on Bitcoin infrastructure. Blockstream’s Liquid Network restored full Bitcoin backing earlier this month, two months after a $320 million exploit.

Luxor said it would soon publish a full post-mortem of the hack.

#4949. Solana’s Trump memecoin launch tested infrastructure ahead of institutional push, Lily Liu says

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Liu said the launch tested Solana's capacity under heavy activity, with the network reaching 60,000 transactions per second during a marketwide liquidation wave.

Solana Solana (SOL) Foundation President Lily Liu said the January 2025 launch of the TRUMP memecoin and the October 2025 crypto liquidation wave served as live stress tests for the network’s financial infrastructure ahead of its institutional momentum.

Speaking to The Block's Gareth Jenkinson at Solana Breakpoint in London, Liu described the token's launch as the decade's biggest trading event, arguing that the network's decentralized infrastructure handled the activity even as centralized financial platforms struggled to keep pace.

She said trading began on a Friday evening and continued through a weekend when traditional markets were closed, with Solana's validators, wallets, liquidity pools, and transaction routers processing activity independently.

“You can't stage a test like that of infrastructure,” Liu said. "To be literally pounced upon on a Friday evening and for all of that to go perfectly seamlessly.”

Liu acknowledged that investors have different views about memecoins as an asset class but argued that their trading activity should also be considered separately from the infrastructure processing it.

"You're not going to test that infrastructure with Apple stock, you're just not going to," she said.

She also pointed to the challenge of supporting potentially thousands of new assets, with unpredictable trading activity around the clock.

Liu also recapped another major test she said Solana faced during the Oct. 10, 2025, crypto market selloff, when approximately $20 billion in positions were liquidated across the market within two hours. The network briefly reached 60,000 transactions per second during the episode without experiencing any downtime.

"Last year when there was the 10/10 event — that was a big stress, it was a very stressful event for everywhere in blockchain," she said. "But then if you looked at how Solana handled it, nothing went down."

Liu said infrastructure could only be properly tested when markets were live, with trading activity unfolding at a scale no one could reliably predict.

Liu's comments come as traditional finance institutions are advancing their onchain initiatives, with major names taking them to Solana.

Last week, the Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation announced plans to bring tokenized versions of eligible securities to the network in the second half of 2027. The DTCC’s tokenization service launched on Oct. 28 on Canton Network and its private Besu network.

Schroders also deployed its first tokenized fund on the network in November, and Formation Securities has notified the SEC of its plan to operate a tokenized securities venue on Solana, targeting an early 2027 launch.

#4945. Bitcoin's $2 Billion Liquidation Weekend Leaves Traders Watching $60,000

Bitcoin's $2 Billion Liquidation Weekend Leaves Traders Watching $60,000

Bitcoin fell to around $62,100 on Sunday as roughly $2 billion in crypto derivatives positions were forcibly closed, mostly bets that prices would rise. The weekend slide sent the cryptocurrency to its lowest level since July 14, adding another ugly chapter to six consecutive weeks of losses.

Bitcoin had recovered to around $64,300 early Monday. But the options market is showing why traders aren't necessarily treating that bounce as an all-clear.

Liquidations happen when an exchange closes a trader's position because the money posted to support a borrowed bet is no longer enough to cover its losses. CoinGlass figures from Saturday showed $1.9 billion liquidated in one 24-hour period, about $1.8 billion of it bullish positions across cryptocurrencies, not just Bitcoin.

U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs had already been losing investors before the weekend. Data from Decrypt’s ETF tracker show $185.4 million in net outflows on Friday, extending their losing streak to seven trading sessions and bringing withdrawals since November 5 to approximately $1.91 billion.

BlackRock's IBIT accounted for $143.5 million of Friday's withdrawals, while Fidelity's FBTC lost $41.9 million. The funds' earlier weakness had helped explain the broader exodus, although ETF flow totals measure fund creations and redemptions rather than every trade between investors.

Those numbers also come with a timing distinction: U.S. ETFs do not trade over the weekend, while Bitcoin trades around the clock. Friday's outflows show weakened demand before the crash, not that ETF shareholders were redeeming shares during Sunday's plunge.

Bitcoin's technical indicators suggest the selling may be overstretched, but that doesn't necessarily mean the worst is over.

The daily Relative Strength Index sat near 24 in oversold territory on the daily charts. Meanwhile, the Average Directional Index stood above 33, indicating a strong prevailing trend, with bearish directional pressure still dominating. The weekly RSI, near 38, pointed to a weak longer-term picture, although it had not reached oversold territory.

The cryptocurrency bounced after approaching support around $62,000. A recovery would face potential resistance near $66,500 to $67,200, followed by another obstacle around $69,300, based on the chart's Fibonacci levels that measure support and resistance marks.

A decisive break below $62,000, meanwhile, would put the psychologically important $60,000 level back in focus—precisely where options traders have accumulated significant downside exposure.

Bitcoin's next test will be whether fresh U.S. ETF flow data show more withdrawals for Monday and whether traders keep paying for downside protection. The heavily watched November 27 options expiration is still ahead, with $50,000 puts among the contracts drawing the most recent trading activity.

#4943. GM is recalling more than 67,000 Cadillac and Chevy EVs over a brake software defect

General Motors (GM) issued a recall affecting more than 67,000 Cadillac and Chevy EVs because a software glitch can cause a temporary loss of braking.

The recall comes after a 2024 Cadillac Lyriq ran into a garage (at a low speed). On April 10, 2026, a GM tech expert submitted a report to GM’s Speak Up for Safety (SUFS) program for review.

After internal testing, GM was able to replicate the glitch, similar to a recall it issued in 2024. GM said the issue stems from a software glitch with the brake system control module (BSCM) that can cause a temporary loss of braking while driving under certain conditions.

When drivers lightly tap the brakes during a sharp turn and the vehicle hits a bump, the software’s slip calculation may misinterpret wheel movement.

In some instances, it can lead to a temporary loss of braking. The glitch only affects pAWD vehicles, including certain Cadillac Lyriq, Celestiq, Vistiq, and Chevy Blazer EV.

In a letter sent to the NHTSA on October 29, GM said the recall impacted 67,380 vehicles total, including certain:

According to documents filed with the NHTSA, GM found 24 field complaints related to the issue. Of these, 20 involved minor accidents, and two involved allegations of minor injuries.

The complaints were received between February 24, 2024, and June 11, 2026. GM said it has implemented software corrections in production over the past few months.

GM will update the BSCM software via an over-the-air (OTA) update or through a dealer, free of charge.

Owner notification letters are expected to be mailed out on December 14, 2026. In the meantime, you can contact Cadillac’s customer service at 1-800-333-4223 or Chevrolet’s customer service at 1-800-222-1020. GM’s recall number is N262570900.

You can also contact the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration Hotline at 1-888-327-4236 or visit the nhtsa.gov website for more information. The NHTSA campaign number is 26V717.

#4942. Bitcoin Wallet Maker Trezor Says Customer Data Was Exposed Again

Bitcoin Wallet Maker Trezor Says Customer Data Was Exposed Again

The “days since last third-party breach” counter at Trezor has reset to zero once again.

This time, hackers accessed shipping partner Skladon's systems and likely exported information tied to approximately 27,000 hardware-wallet orders, according to a company disclosure published Monday.

The intrusion occurred November 7, but Skladon did not notify Trezor until November 15. The exposed records cover orders placed between August 9 and November 7, including customers' full names, phone numbers, delivery and email addresses, plus the products they bought and their prices.

Trezor said its own systems were not compromised, and no wallet backups, PINs or passphrases were involved. The breach does not, by itself, let an attacker move a buyer's coins, but it gives them a potentially dangerous combination: a home address and evidence that someone bought a device designed to store crypto.

The latest incident follows an August shipping partner hack and a September email provider breach, making three separate third-party security incidents in a little over three months.

Trezor said some customers have already reported fraudulent phone calls. Criminals could cite the exact model and price of a wallet to make an unsolicited call, text or letter seem legitimate, or use a home address to identify someone who may hold cryptocurrency.

In August, logistics provider ShipMonk suffered a breach that initially appeared to affect fewer than 14,000 Trezor customers. An updated investigation uncovered records for another 67,000 U.S. buyers from 2019 to 2021 that ShipMonk had retained despite assurances they had been deleted, bringing the total to 80,689 people.

September brought another incident, this time at marketing email provider Brevo. Hackers used Trezor's legitimate email channel to distribute a fake security warning urging recipients to install an app that requested their wallet backups. Trezor later said 347,149 email addresses had been exported.

These weren't Trezor's first brushes with the problem. A Mailchimp insider attack targeted Trezor users in 2022, and a 2024 breach of its external support portal put the contact details of up to 66,000 people at risk.

The fallout can go beyond inbox spam. In February, fake letters were sent to hardware-wallet owners, complete with Trezor branding, holograms and QR codes leading to phishing sites.

Asked why an order from Trezor's official store involved an outside company, Trezor responded that it’s basically a logistics question, not a tech issue. “Companies of our size and global reach can’t run efficiently without third-party service providers,” the company replied to a comment on X.

Hi, companies of our size and global reach can’t run efficiently without third-party service providers. Handling as much as possible internally is the goal, but doing every part of the business ourselves isn’t realistic. Skladon is one of the partners that delivers Trezor orders,…

It said Skladon held only the information needed to deliver parcels, and handling every stage of global distribution in-house was not realistic.

Trezor says Skladon does not ship to the United States, so American buyers were not affected by this incident. Affected customers have been notified where possible, though some people who ordered between August 9 and 14 may not receive an email because Trezor had already anonymized their records.

The company has introduced anonymous pickup at parcel lockers or collection points in 18 European countries, allowing buyers to avoid supplying a home address. It is also working to shorten retention of order data from 90 to 45 days and says shipping partners will have to follow the same policy.

Trezor advises customers to ignore unexpected calls and messages about their orders, check any concerns through its official support site and never share a wallet backup. It has notified a data protection authority, but has not yet disclosed how attackers got into Skladon's systems.

#4939. Hackers inject credit card, crypto-stealing malware into 178 major websites

Ledger and Coinpass were among the businesses whose websites served malware designed to steal visitors’ crypto and credit card details, according to security researchers.

Update (Nov. 17 at 4:43 am UTC): This article has been updated to include a statement from Salesforce and analysis of the stolen funds by Galaxy Digital’s Alex Thorn.

Major global brands including Ledger, Salesforce and BBC served cryptocurrency and credit card-stealing malware to website visitors on Monday, according to security researchers, the latest in a wave of wide-reaching cyberattacks.

Dubbed “Optimjack” by researcher Amirul Iman, the campaign delivered malicious code through website scripts, including those hosted by Optimizely, a website optimization platform. The malware can replace legitimate cryptocurrency wallet addresses with attacker-controlled alternatives when visitors copy addresses. It also captured credit card details entered into website forms, according to a separate analysis by cybersecurity firm StepSecurity.

The campaign affected website scripts belonging to 178 organizations, including hardware wallet Ledger, OANDA / Coinpass and trading platforms IG, Interactive Investor and TMS Brokers, according to Report URI’s investigation.

Other affected brands included IKEA, Salesforce, Zoom, the BBC and CNN — websites that collectively attract millions of visitors a day.

A spokesperson for Optimizely confirmed to Cointelegraph on Monday that an unauthorized party exploited a vulnerability in its Experimentation account signup flow to add malicious JavaScript to some customer projects.

“We have blocked access to the vulnerable path and deployed a fix,” they said. “We have removed the injected code from affected projects. We have also ended active sessions, revoked tokens, and reset access for affected user accounts. Finally, we have blocked any code from being delivered in Cloudflare,” they said, adding that they’re continuing to investigate and have notified all affected customers.

BBC was among the first websites seen serving the Optimizely injection at 4:21 am UTC on Monday, according to Report URI CEO Scott Helme. By 9:52 pm UTC, only one of the 188 infected snippets still contained malicious code, according to Report URI, with Ledger’s snippet cleaned at about 8:41 pm UTC.

Cointelegraph reached out to Ledger, Coinpass, and a number of other major businesses reported as impacted by the malicious injection.

A spokesperson for Salesforce told Cointelegraph it was investigating unauthorized modifications to Optimizely scripts used on certain Salesforce websites.

“As a precaution, Salesforce is confirming Optimizely scripts are disabled across preview and production instances.”

Optimizely lets businesses run experiments and custom code on their websites through JavaScript snippets. Attackers inserted malicious code into those customer projects, causing otherwise legitimate scripts to load the malware when visitors opened affected pages.

Once loaded, the malware could replace legitimate cryptocurrency wallet addresses with attacker-controlled alternatives, including addresses copied to a visitor’s clipboard. A user could then unknowingly paste the attacker’s address when making a transfer.

Related: PHP flaw draws millions of exploit attempts, Cloudflare CEO warns

The script also watched for credit card numbers, expiry dates and security codes entered into website forms and sent captured details to an attacker-controlled server. However, it could not read payment fields isolated inside third-party payment providers’ cross-origin frames, StepSecurity said.

Visitors using iPhones and iPads also faced an additional threat: the malicious script loaded code linked to the Coruna and DarkSword iOS exploit kits, according to Helme.

Researchers have yet to establish how attackers obtained access to modify the Optimizely projects.

Despite the widespread attack, crypto addresses identified as linked to the malware received only around $7,100 in cryptocurrency on Monday, according to blockchain records checked by Cointelegraph.

StepSecurity’s separate analysis put funds received or held across the wallets at roughly $6,600, noting that some addresses had activity predating the attack.

Alex Thorn, head of firmwide research at Galaxy Digital, estimated that victims have only sent around $2,300 in total to the addresses. In an analysis posted on X, Thorn said most of the Bitcoin receipts appeared to be the attacker’s own funds routed through an exchange back to their wallet, rather than proceeds from victims.

The campaign follows a string of recent threats to website security. In October, government agencies and other organizations took websites offline as a precaution over critical PHP vulnerabilities that could allow attackers to take control of servers. Polymarket also temporarily suspended trading on Oct. 29 after a third-party provider was compromised, amid reports of users’ accounts being drained.

#4938. First: Volvo CE Electric equipment does the work, without disturbing the wildlife

For the first time, electric equipment assets are being put to work in the National Wildlife Refuge System, where a battery-powered, zero-emission ECR25 Electric compact excavator and L25 Electric compact wheel loader made by Volvo CE are making the wild more accessible to everyone.

Construction work isn’t exactly welcome in the wilderness – between diesel exhaust, engine noises, and the constant rumble of heavy machinery, even a relatively small project can have a big impact on the native wildlife. To minimize that impact, US Fish and Wildlife Service is turning to Volvo CE and its compact excavators and wheel loaders to get the job done, building out a new, ADA accessible trail in Virginia’s Occoquan Bay National Wildlife Refuge.

“We are excited to partner with the US Fish and Wildlife Service on this project to demonstrate Volvo’s commitment to lowering emissions and working to build the future we all want and need,” said Dr. Ray Gallant, VP of Product Management and Productivity, Volvo CE. “Projects like the one at the Occoquan Bay National Wildlife Refuge give a glimpse of how electric machines can create a more climate-friendly future for the construction industry.”

The refuge is home to a diverse array of grasslands, wetlands, and woods that attract red foxes, painted turtles, osprey, river otters, and countless other rarely seen species – and the ADA accessible path being laid down by the L25 Electric compact wheel loader will allow wheelchair-bound visitors to travel a half-mile-long trail around one of the pond. Meanwhile, the ECR25 Electric compact excavator will be digging trenches to improve drainage from the pond and helping to place a viewing platform that extends out over the water.

Keeping the machines running is a portable EV ARC solar-powered charging system from Beam Global that’s designed to drive down the project’s carbon footprint even further.

#4936. Alibaba's Qwen 4 Max Leads China's AI Agent Race—But You Can't Download It

Alibaba's Qwen 4 Max Leads China's AI Agent Race—But You Can't Download It

Alibaba released Qwen 4 Max on Monday, its most powerful AI model yet and the first flagship built on its new Qwen 4 architecture.

It is also the highest-ranked Chinese model on Arena's agent leaderboard, putting the company ahead of Tencent, Moonshot AI and DeepSeek in that particular test.

The new model has 2.4 trillion parameters, can process text, images and video, and accepts up to one million tokens of context. Parameters are all the different dials a model can handle and tweak when processing information. Tokens are basically the smallest unit of information a model can handle. Its current configuration is enough room for lengthy codebases or stacks of documents.

Alibaba uses a mixture-of-experts design, which keeps most of those parameters idle for any given word it processes. Qwen 4 Max activates 71 billion at a time, compared with 95 billion for Qwen 3.8 Max, allowing a very large model to use less computing power per token.

On Agent Arena, Qwen 4 Max placed 14th among individual models and made Alibaba the fifth-ranked laboratory overall.

The leaderboard evaluates real agent sessions using task completion, user satisfaction and how well models respond to corrections, then scores each model by its net percentage improvement over an average competitor. Qwen 4 Max posted a preliminary 7.46% score across 14,727 sessions, the highest among Chinese entries.

Its visual work may be the more interesting part for regular users. The Code Arena web-development ranking compares websites built by AI models using head-to-head user votes and a points-based rating system. Qwen 4 Max debuted sixth globally at 1,750 points, up 74 from its predecessor, and placed eighth in categories for branding and reference-based design.

That puts it in the same neighborhood as the best American models at turning a request, a screenshot or a rough idea into a working interface that actually looks good.

Alibaba's demos go further, showing the model building game environments and translating factory layouts into 3D models, although those are company-selected examples rather than independent tests.

The visual capability is for understanding images and video and producing code or other text-based outputs. It is not the same as releasing a new image generator.

Qwen 4 Max costs $1.50 per million input tokens and $4.50 per million generated tokens through QwenCloud, a 25% reduction from the previous flagship's $2 and $6 rates. Reused input served from an automatic cache costs $0.075 per million tokens, which can make repetitive workflows substantially cheaper.

Alibaba says its internal testing across hundreds of professional specialties found that the model used 35% fewer tokens and reduced average task costs by roughly 60% compared with Qwen 3.8 Max. In other words, it should be cheaper to execute tasks on this model even against competitors with a similar price..

The bigger limitation for the community is that Qwen 4 Max is closed-weight. Developers can use it through an API or Alibaba's apps, but they cannot download its internal parameters, modify it or run it privately on their own hardware.

Qwen has become a foundation for the open-model ecosystem. An August report from Hugging Face counted 151,448 Qwen-based derivative models on its platform, about 4.7 times the number built specifically on Meta's Llama family. Those include community fine-tunes and optimized versions that power local assistants and specialized tools.

Alibaba opened the weights of Qwen 3.8 Max earlier this year, despite having kept some previous Max models proprietary. It also previewed elements of the Qwen 4 architecture with smaller models months before Monday's release.

The launch lands in a crowded Chinese race. Moonshot's Kimi K3 pushed enormous open-weight models toward Western performance, while DeepSeek has been competing hard on API prices.

Qwen 4 Max leads some Chinese rankings, but American labs still hold the top spots in Arena's overall agent and web-development tables. They are also considerably more expensive.

Alibaba says smaller Qwen 4 models will be released with open weights, giving developers a chance to build on this generation rather than simply rent its biggest model. It has not announced their sizes or release dates.

#4932. High gas prices are pumping up used EV sales: these are FLYING off the lot

A new car affordability crisis and punishing gas prices are driving an unprecedented number of used car buyers to consider going electric, and that increased demand without a corresponding increase in supply means these used EVs are flying off dealer lots.

Days on the lot. That’s dealer-speak for the average number of days a given vehicle typically sits in inventory before finding a buyer. That’s critically important to dealers, since the vehicles on their lots are commonly financed through something called floorplan credit, and every additional day a car spends on a dealer’s lot can mean another day of interest payments and another day closer to a curtailment. Days on the lot can be even more important to dealers when it comes to cars owned outright, as well, as the dealers can have significant cash tied up in vehicles they’ve purchased outright, and every day those cars sit is another day that money isn’t working for them elsewhere.

That’s what makes this month’s fastest-selling used EVs particularly interesting, because these used EVs aren’t just selling fast for electric vehicles – they’re selling fast, period.

Dealer news sources Automotive News and Cox Automotive report that the average new car spent 82 days on the lot in October, with a 75-day target being an industry-wide benchmark. At the same time, the Audi Q4 Sportback e-tron Quattro with its 258-mile EPA-estimated range is turning in just 29.8 days. (!)

That Audi’s not alone. In a recently-released vehicle pricing study, iSeeCars reported that the Toyota BZ4x, Lexus RZ300e, Honda Prologue, and Borrás-family favorite Polestar 2 are all turning in an average of 35 days or less, with the BMW i4, iX, Kia EV6, and Chevrolet Equinox EV just a day or two behind.

“The results reveal a market where EVs are appreciating in average asking price much faster than other drivetrains, while also spending less time waiting for buyers,” said iSeeCars Executive Analyst Karl Brauer, who found that average transaction prices for EVs rose 9.8% last month as ICE-powered vehicles stayed steady, rising just 0.3%. “Hybrids are experiencing moderate improvements in price and selling speed, while gasoline-powered vehicles have seen their selling times increase,” he adds, “despite relatively stable prices.”

@Jo Borras , you forgot to mention about one more factor (besides: "A new car affordability crisis and punishing gas prices..."). Back in September last year there was still electric vehicle tax credits in the United States that has ended on September 30, 2025.

Used electric vehicles averaged a price of $32,533 in September 2026, up $2,896 from the $29,637 ATP recorded a year earlier.

#4931. Solana Foundation Backs CoinGecko as Crypto Data Giant Eyes Trading

Solana Foundation Backs CoinGecko as Crypto Data Giant Eyes Trading

CoinGecko, one of crypto’s most widely used price-tracking sites, wants to do more than show users where markets are heading.

The crypto data platform said Tuesday that it has received backing from the Solana Foundation, the Switzerland-based nonprofit that supports the Solana network, to speed its expansion into market intelligence, new digital asset products and, potentially, trading.

The announcement came on the final day of Solana’s Breakpoint conference in London.

The Solana Foundation did not specify the size of the commitment, but a representative for the foundation told Decrypt it is taking a majority stake in CoinGecko. The terms were not disclosed.

CoinGecko said it will focus on three areas: expanding data coverage and analytics across blockchain ecosystems, exploring trading features that let users act onchain, and building simpler experiences for users worldwide.

“We’ve helped millions of people discover and understand this industry, but the opportunity ahead is much bigger,” said Bobby Ong, CoinGecko’s CEO and co-founder.

Vibhu Norby, chief product officer at the Solana Foundation, said the foundation believes the “next wave of adoption will come from making crypto dramatically easier to use.” (Disclosure: Decrypt’s parent company, Dastan, is building products on Solana, and Decrypt uses CoinGecko market data.)

Founded in 2014, CoinGecko says it provides data on more than 50 million crypto assets across more than 2,000 exchanges.

CoinGecko said it will keep serving users with the same commitment to “reliable, comprehensive market coverage and transparent methodologies.” Co-founder and President TM Lee said its commitment to accurate, reliable data “remains unchanged.”

The deal is one of several Solana announcements timed around Breakpoint. DTCC said last week that it plans to connect its tokenization service to Solana, with tokenized stocks, ETFs and Treasuries expected on the network in the second half of 2027.

At a Breakpoint panel, executives from DTCC and Barclays said tokenization’s biggest near-term payoff lies in putting idle collateral to work.

#4928. Trezor customer data exposed in 3rd partner breach this year

Trezor warned an attacker has been able to access customer names, phone numbers and delivery addresses linked to 27,000 orders from one of its shipping providers.

Hardware wallet maker Trezor has disclosed its third partner data breach this year, warning that an attacker accessed personal information linked to approximately 27,000 orders held by shipping provider Skladon.

The exposed information includes customers’ names, phone numbers, delivery addresses, email addresses and purchase details for orders placed between Aug. 9 and Nov. 7.

Trezor said Monday that it believes the attacker exported the data. Its own systems were not compromised, and customers’ devices remain secure, the company said.

The disclosure follows an August breach at shipping provider ShipMonk that initially affected about 13,700 customers. Trezor later said another approximately 67,000 US customers were affected, including people whose records ShipMonk had assured it were deleted.

In September, a separate breach at marketing platform Brevo exposed 347,149 email addresses from Trezor’s newsletter list.

“Treat any unexpected call, text, email or letter about your order as suspicious, and be alert to potential risks to your physical security,” the company said on X. “Trezor will never cold-call you or ask for your wallet backup.”

The disclosure came as Trezor launched its anonymous pickup service in 18 European countries, allowing customers to order devices without giving out home addresses.

“For crypto holders, a leaked shipping record does more harm than most. It links a person, a home address and the fact that they own crypto,” it said.

#4927. BLUETTI’s flexible $1,099 Apex 200 backs up your home – and powers your RV

What do you actually need to keep running when the power goes out? For most households, the fridge, wifi, phones, and a few other essentials come first. Buying enough battery storage for every appliance in the house can come later – and that’s the appeal of BLUETTI’s latest launch, the Apex 200 portable power station.

The Apex 200 starts with 2.56kWh of storage and 3.2kW of continuous AC output, with the option to add batteries when you need longer backup. Built-in RV connections also mean it can earn its keep between blackouts.

BLUETTI launches the Apex 200 on November 4, with standalone super early-bird pricing starting at $1,099 through November 18. Electrek readers can get an extra 5% off with code ELECTREK5OFF.

Here’s how to work out which setup is worth buying for your home or RV.

The Apex 200 takes the expandable design of BLUETTI’s flagship Apex series and puts it in a smaller package.

You get a 2,560Wh lithium iron phosphate (LFP) battery. The 3.2kW of continuous AC output increases to 3.6kW when an expansion battery is connected or the unit is actively charging.

The enclosure holds all that in 36.2 liters and weighs 26.9kg (59.3 lb). You’ll want two hands to move it, but it’s convenient to move it between home and your camper.

BLUETTI’s UltraCell technology delivers an industry-leading energy density of 70.7 Wh/L, yet the Apex 200 is more than 50% smaller by volume than class competitors. For an RV owner, that smaller footprint frees up room for other gear.

There’s also a tilted display and four app-controllable AC outlets. That might not sound like a big deal, but when the battery is on the floor or under your desk and you want to check or redirect its charge, it means you don’t have to crouch.

BLUETTI calls its approach “Start Small. Scale Big. Power More.” BLUETTI says Apex 300 users reported liking the modular design but wanted something easier to handle day to day. The Apex 200 offers them a lower entry point, with room to add capacity rather than replace the main unit.

Before buying a portable power station, you’ll want to know how much power your appliances draw and how much energy they use over time.

For example, a 150W appliance running continuously for 10 hours uses 1,500Wh. A 1,500W appliance gets through that same amount in one hour. Running a coffee maker briefly is a different proposition from keeping a high-draw appliance on all afternoon.

A rough runtime calculation is: Usable battery capacity in watt-hours ÷ average load in watts = hours of operation.

Divide the Apex 200’s nominal 2,560Wh by a steady 150W load, and you get about 17 hours before accounting for conversion losses and the battery system’s own power use. Real runtime will be lower.

Refrigerators complicate that calculation because their compressors cycle on and off. Check measured daily energy use where possible, along with startup demand – motors can briefly draw more power when they kick on.

If your usual blackout lasts four to 12 hours, start by looking at the Apex 200 on its own.

BLUETTI estimates it can run a 150W refrigerator for 10–12 hours, or support low-draw essentials such as CPAPs, wifi routers, and mobile devices for two to three days, depending on the equipment and how it’s used. That doesn’t mean every device on that list will run together for three days. Your combined load determines the runtime.

The Apex 200 also gives you more control over where that energy goes. Four independently controllable Smart AC outlets built right into the unit – no need to buy smart plugs! – let you switch individual devices on and off through the BLUETTI App, set schedules, and apply state-of-charge controls. So if something doesn’t need power overnight, you can turn off its power at the outlet and save the fridge’s battery.

BLUETTI calls the feature an industry first for portable power stations. The system also works with Amazon Alexa, Google Home, and Home Assistant.

A UPS function switches connected equipment to battery power in 15 milliseconds. Check that switching time against your equipment’s requirements, especially for a CPAP or other sensitive device.

With AC output on and the display off, idle consumption drops to just 10.5W.

A winter freeze or severe storm can leave you without grid power for days. That’s when adding extra storage starts to make more sense.

For typical power outages, adding one or two BLUETTI B300K batteries can extend essential home backup to two to three days.

But you can go further. One Apex 200 supports up to six B500K expansion batteries, bringing its maximum capacity to 33.28kWh.

But a bigger battery still needs recharging eventually. The Apex 200 accepts up to 3kW of solar input across two independent channels:

With a correctly configured array of SORA 500 portable solar panels making full use of those inputs, BLUETTI says the base battery can charge from 0 to 80% in 50 minutes, or reach 100% in 1.7 hours using solar alone.

A solar generator setup lets you recharge during daylight. The Apex 200 can also be charged with wall AC, vehicle charging, and compatible gas-generator charging. A suitable 30A generator connection can help replenish the battery when solar production falls short.

The Apex 200 can also form part of a larger home battery backup system through BLUETTI’s Hub A1 parallel box.

BLUETTI specifies up to 6kW of continuous AC output and approximately 100kWh of storage across a parallel system using up to three Apex 200 units.

That’s a significant increase over the standalone battery, but your daily consumption still matters. A steady 1kW load uses 24kWh in 24 hours. Heavy-draw appliances can make a large battery feel much smaller.

BLUETTI positions its larger configurations for three to four days of essential-panel backup. How close you get depends on your batteries, appliances, and ability to recharge.

If you want automatic backup through your home’s electrical panel, confirm the compatible transfer equipment, supported circuits, voltage, and output for the exact bundle. Those details determine what you can actually keep running.

Start with your most likely outage, then decide whether you need more runtime, a charging source, or both.

Extra batteries extend runtime. Solar panels or another charging source recharge them. For outages that stretch into days, both can be worth having.

When shopping for a camper van or travel trailer, choosing a battery comes down to more than just capacity. Among its 10 output ports are two connections RV owners will appreciate having built in.

The native 30A TT-30R RV outlet plugs directly into travel trailer shore power inlets for 120V power.

The 12V/30A Anderson port can directly power compatible DC equipment, including portable fridges and communications gear. This avoids converting battery power to AC and then back to DC, cutting out unnecessary conversion steps.

For charging between campsites, the system can pair with the BLUETTI Charger 2, which combines alternator and solar charging support. It lets you use driving time to replenish the battery through faster vehicle alternator charging, then use solar when you’re parked.

BLUETTI’s recommended mobile bundle is the Apex 200 + SORA 500 solar panel + Charger 2. You get storage for camp, solar charging during your stay, and alternator charging on the road. Compatible campground AC gives you another option.

The Apex 200 is the better fit if you value a smaller enclosure, easier transport, native RV ports, and a lower upfront cost. You can start with 2.56kWh and expand to 33.28kWh on one unit, or approximately 100kWh across a fully expanded parallel system.

The Apex 300 is worth considering if your priority is higher native output and capacity for a system that will mostly stay at home. It offers 3,840W of output and 2,764.8Wh of battery storage, and BLUETTI positions it for more advanced home backup, including compatible 120V/240V configurations.

For someone who wants a battery for both blackouts and RV trips, the Apex 200’s size and connections make a useful combination. You can buy the storage you need now and add more when your power needs change.

The Apex 200 launches November 4, with the standalone unit starting at $1,099 during the November 4–18 super early-bird promotion.

Founded in 2013, BLUETTI allows millions of users across more than 120 countries to stay safe and prepared. Its in-house R&D supports a range of solar generators and home backup systems for outage preparation, RV travel, and off-grid living and recreation.

Shop the Apex 200 and launch bundles, and use ELECTREK5OFF at checkout for an extra 5% savings.

#4925. Ex-Robinhood engineer pleads guilty to $125K Hyperliquid trading scheme

The former Robinhood engineer used confidential information about upcoming Robinhood token listings to trade on Hyperliquid.

Former Robinhood software engineer Huaisong “Jerry” Xiang has pleaded guilty to wire fraud after using confidential information about upcoming crypto listings on the platform to trade perpetual futures on the decentralized exchange Hyperliquid, earning $125,523 in illicit profits.

On Monday, the US Department of Justice said that Xiang entered the plea before US District Judge Colleen McMahon and agreed to forfeit $125,523. Xiang faces a maximum sentence of 20 years in prison and is scheduled to be sentenced on Feb. 25, 2027.

Prosecutors said the software engineer had access to nonpublic information about which tokens Robinhood Crypto planned to list and when. Between 2025 and 2026, he repeatedly bought perpetual futures tied to those tokens before the announcements were made public.

The plea follows September charges against Xiang and another former Robinhood engineer, Hefu Chai. Both were accused of exploiting internal listing information to trade on Hyperliquid, with prosecutors initially alleging that each made over $50,000.

The DOJ’s Monday announcement did not provide an update on Chai’s case.

In May, crypto analytics firm Kaiko flagged suspicious Hyperliquid trading ahead of Robinhood’s Jan. 15 listing of Lighter (LIT). One of the wallets identified in its investigation matches an address that prosecutors later linked to Chai, who allegedly used it to trade LIT perpetuals ahead of the listing.

#4923. Judge Acquits MIT Brothers in $25M Ethereum MEV Case, Ending Prosecution

Judge Acquits MIT Brothers in $25M Ethereum MEV Case, Ending Prosecution

A federal judge in Manhattan has acquitted Anton and James Peraire-Bueno, the MIT-educated brothers accused of exploiting Ethereum's transaction ordering system to take $25 million from automated trading bots.

U.S. District Judge Jessica Clarke granted the brothers' motion for a judgment of acquittal on Monday, clearing them of wire fraud, conspiracy to commit wire fraud and conspiracy to commit money laundering, according to her ruling. The decision closes the criminal case without a second trial.

"The question before the Court is not whether the Peraire-Buenos' conduct was right or moral, or even whether it violated uncharged crimes," Clarke wrote. To convict on wire fraud, prosecutors had to show the brothers made a material misrepresentation, and none of the government's theories did so, she found. Ethereum, she noted, is a "trustless, highly adversarial environment with no terms of use."

While the brothers' scheme "may have frustrated the spirit" of MEV-Boost, software that lets Ethereum validators outsource the ordering of transactions to specialized block builders, that was not enough to make out fraud, Clarke said. In a footnote, she added that the prosecution "raises serious due process concerns," though she declined to rule on that question.

Because the money laundering charge relied on the proceeds of the alleged wire fraud, it fell with the fraud counts.

The case stemmed from an April 2023 exploit targeting so-called sandwich bots, automated traders that profit from maximal extractable value (MEV) by placing their own trades on either side of other users' transactions.

According to the ruling, the brothers placed bait transactions designed to lure specific bots, then exploited a bug in a relay's code to see the bots' bundled trades before committing to a block. They then swapped the bait transactions for trades that sold the tokens instead, leaving the bots with about $25 million in losses in roughly 12 seconds. Authorities arrested the pair in May 2024, saying they spent months planning the attack and took steps to hide the proceeds.

At trial in Manhattan last year, an insider testified that the brothers had allegedly planned the attack, and the defense had earlier sought to block evidence of their Google searches. Clarke declared a mistrial in November 2025, after the jury deadlocked.

The case was closely watched in the crypto industry. Clarke's ruling described it as a "first of its kind" prosecution, a label the government itself used in its indictment.

#4921. Aniioki A9 Pro Max review: Basically an electric motorcycle that you can pedal

There are electric bikes, there are electric motorcycles, and then there are machines like the Aniioki A9 Pro Max that plant a flag somewhere in the increasingly large no-man’s-land between the two.

Technically, it has pedals. You can pedal it fairly effectively too, with the actually adjustable seat. You can even pedal it for fun, which is more than I can say for some of the increasingly motorcycle-shaped “e-bikes” I’ve tested lately.

But let’s get something out of the way right at the beginning: this isn’t a street-legal electric bicycle in the US. Not when we’re talking about dual motors producing up to 6,000W of peak power and speeds of around 45 mph.

And you know what? That’s fine. As long as we’re all honest about what this thing is.

The Aniioki A9 Pro Max is absolutely massive, and the spec sheet makes that abundantly clear.

The version I tested uses a pair of 2,500W direct-drive hub motors, one in each wheel, with Aniioki claiming 3,000W peak power from each motor. That gives the bike a combined peak figure of 6,000W and up to 250 Nm of combined torque. It’s hard to overstate how massive that is.

Then there’s the battery. You can choose between a 60V 70Ah pack with 4.2 kWh of capacity or an even larger 60V 80Ah pack packing 4.8 kWh. For comparison, many typical commuter e-bikes have somewhere around 500-700 Wh of battery. The smaller battery here is roughly six to eight times that size. That’s absolutely in the territory of legit light electric motorcycles.

Aniioki claims up to 230+ miles (370 km) of pedal-assist range, though as usual, you should take maximum e-bike range figures as best-case numbers. Using the throttle and enjoying those big motors is going to dramatically reduce that figure. Even so, with more than 4 kWh of battery underneath you, range anxiety isn’t exactly the first thing that comes to mind.

All that hardware comes with a predictable penalty: weight. The 70Ah model tips the scales at around 188 lb (85 kg), while the 80Ah version approaches 193 lb (87.5 kg). Those aren’t e-bike weights. Those are motorcycle weights. Again, you can see the direction this is going.

Here’s where the A9 Pro Max gets more interesting to me.

A lot of high-powered e-motos include pedals seemingly as a regulatory fig leaf. They’re technically there, but the geometry is so bad that nobody in their right mind would want to pedal the thing farther than across a parking lot. Even some smaller and presumably Class 2 legal e-bikes that have moto-style frames are nearly unpedalable for actual riding over any reasonable distance.

But the A9 here is actually pedalable thanks to having something that more closely resembles a bicycle seat, plus adjustability that lets riders dial in the correct seat height.

The A9 Pro Max feels more like a strange combination of an oversized fat-tire e-bike and a small electric motorcycle. There’s a Shimano 7-speed drivetrain and a huge 72-tooth chainring, meaning the pedals aren’t entirely ornamental.

I actually enjoyed pedaling it with the pedal assist engaged. You’re obviously not going to mistake a nearly 200-pound machine rolling on enormous 26 x 4.8-inch tires for a lightweight fitness bike, but there’s something fun about contributing some human power while two giant hub motors do most of the heavy lifting.

And when you don’t feel like pedaling, there’s a full-twist throttle waiting for you.

That combination is really the appeal here. You can ride it more like an e-bike when the mood strikes, or twist the throttle and treat it more like a lightweight e-moto.

And again, I don’t want to beat a dead horse here, but remember: the pedals don’t make this an electric bicycle. It’s still an e-moto, but it’s one that you can actually have fun pedaling. You can treat it like a mech suit, where pedaling translates into crazy power, as if you have super-human legs. And with the insanely large 72t chainring, pedaling at higher speeds is actually doable. Though I’ll tell you right now, pedaling at 40 mph (64 km/h) is not exactly a normal or natural feeling.

And it’s worth pointing out that at a starting price of US $2,899 (or more for the larger battery capacities), that’s a fairly reasonable price compared to most e-motos that can be more limiting in how and where you can ride them.

Fortunately, Aniioki seems to understand that putting this much power on two wheels requires more serious components than the average bicycle.

The braking setup is particularly wild. There are two massive 300mm brake rotors on the front wheel and another 300mm rotor out back, along with a linked CBS braking system. The front uses custom eight-piston calipers (the pair of quad-piston calipers) while the rear gets a custom six-piston setup.

There’s also an adjustable air-suspension fork with 110mm of travel, rear suspension to smooth out bumps at higher speeds, integrated lighting and turn signals, and a large five-inch display that even incorporates features like Bluetooth audio and a recording camera.

That brings us to the elephant in the room.

In the US, consumer-product rules generally define a low-speed electric bicycle around a motor of less than 750W and a maximum motor-powered speed below 20 mph (32 km/h), while individual states use their own operating laws that typically match the familiar Class 1/2/3 framework. A 6,000W machine capable of around 45 mph (72 km/h) blows straight through normal limits.

So please don’t see pedals and assume this belongs on your local bicycle path.

Where and how you can legally operate something like the A9 Pro Max depends on your local laws and whether it can qualify under another vehicle category. For many owners, the sensible place to enjoy its full performance is going to be private property or other areas where such vehicles are permitted.

That caveat doesn’t make the bike itself bad. It just means buyers need to understand what they’re buying.

And judged on what it actually is rather than what the word “e-bike” on the product page suggests, I had a lot of fun on it.

There are certainly riders who would look at a nearly 200-pound, 45 mph machine and say, “Why not just buy an electric motorcycle?” That’s a perfectly reasonable question.

In addition to kW power limits, the legal boundary for what is an e-bike should also include weight. Anything over 100lbs including battery is no longer an e-bike and it carries a dangerous amount of momentum once it is cruising. 190 lbs is ridiculous.

But I also understand the appeal of this weird middle ground. The A9 Pro Max retains enough bicycle DNA that you can pedal it around and enjoy pedal assist, yet has enough battery and motor underneath you to deliver something much closer to small-motorcycle performance when you twist the throttle.

Imagine going off-road and getting to fly up hills while pedaling hard, but then being able to switch to throttle once your legs get worn out. Sure, it’s not everyone’s cup of tea, but it’s a cup of tea.

This bike is big. It’s heavy. It’s absurdly powerful by e-bike standards. And it absolutely isn’t the machine I’d recommend for someone looking for a normal street-legal electric bicycle.

But if what you actually want is a big, powerful electric two-wheeler that still lets you turn the pedals when the mood strikes, the Aniioki A9 Pro Max makes a surprisingly compelling argument for this increasingly bizarre category of pedal-equipped e-motos.

#4918. Nvidia Board Not Considering Jensen Huang's Return: Report

Nvidia Board Not Considering Jensen Huang's Return: Report

Nvidia's board is not considering bringing back Jensen Huang as CEO, despite a growing campaign for his return led by some of the biggest names in tech and backed by President Donald Trump.

The board is focused on its investigation into Huang and is not in discussions to reinstate him, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC on Monday. The independent investigation, led by law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz, is examining Huang's conduct and whether it compromised national security or company secrets.

Huang resigned as Nvidia's CEO on Thursday, after 33 years at the helm of the company he co-founded. His departure followed his admission of an affair and reports that he had been romantically involved with Alice Huang, an executive at an Nvidia customer whom U.S. officials suspect of spying for China, as well as with Nvidia employees. The week of revelations saw hundreds of billions of dollars wiped from Nvidia's market value.

The report cuts against a weekend of growing pressure for Huang's return. The Financial Times reported that Huang had discussed a comeback with allies, though Altimeter Capital's Brad Gerstner, one of the investors urging the board to keep "all options" open, told CNBC that Huang had not discussed returning with him. A representative for Huang declined to comment on the speculation.

An online petition calling for Huang's reinstatement had drawn 191,996 signatures by Monday. Its backers include Elon Musk, who said on Friday that he was signing it, as well as Y Combinator CEO Garry Tan and Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney. The site notes that signatories' affiliations are self-reported and unverified. Nvidia employees have also pushed internally for his return.

Trump said on Monday that he would welcome Huang back. "I would certainly not mind seeing that," he told reporters, adding that national security had not been put at risk, as Decrypt reported. That puts him at odds with Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, who said on Sunday that Nvidia should look outside the company for its next CEO.

Others in tech have stopped short of backing a return. OpenAI CEO Sam Altman, who was briefly ousted from his own company in 2023, said at a Semafor conference on Monday that Huang had "long been a hero of mine," but that he did not know enough to say whether Huang should come back.

Nvidia shares closed down 4.1% at $156.14 on Monday, and were down a further 0.5% at $155.67 in premarket trading early on Tuesday, according to Yahoo! Finance data.

Nvidia's board said on Friday that it was in the process of selecting a search firm for the CEO role. Chief financial officer Colette Kress, the interim CEO, will lead the company's quarterly earnings call on Tuesday.

#4916. DeepWay electric semi arrives in New Zealand, and gets a new name

New Zealand’s Foodstuffs North Island Limited has given its latest electric semi truck an electrifying new identity, courtesy of a second-grade student at a local elementary school.

The Chinese-built DeepWay Star began delivering chilled and frozen groceries across New Zealand’s lower North Island earlier this year – and, after a naming competition organized with students at Russell Street School in Palmerston North, the big HD EV has got a new name: Evie.

The winning name came from Year 2 (read: second grade) student named Madison, who beat out suggestions including Buzzy Bee, Lightning, and Bolt. Madison got to officially unveil the truck’s new name alongside her classmates, local officials and Foodstuffs representatives.

“Our trucks are out on the road every day delivering food to stores and communities, so we thought it would be great to get the kids involved and give our first electric truck a bit of personality,” explains Foodstuffs North Island General Manager Supply Chain, Yang Zhang. “There were some brilliant ideas, but Evie was a pretty fitting name for an EV. The best part is knowing Madison and the other students will be able to spot Evie out on the road and know they played a part in it.”

Madison was allowed to do the unveiling honors, revealing Evie’s new name alongside her other classmates, Palmerston North’s Mayor, Grant Smith, local MP Tangi Utikeri, and local PAK’nSAVE, New World, and Four Square grocers who will be receiving deliveries from Evie.

In its first few months on the road, Evie’s travelled nearly 45,000 kilometers (~28,000 miles) and avoided more than 81,000 kg of harmful carbon emissions – which the company claims is roughly equivalent to taking 35 ICE cars off the road for a full year.

As for the truck itself, it has a sort of Tesla-ish, anteater-y look, and significantly longer range than the StarWay, offering more than 500 km (~310 miles) of range from its 600 kWh CATL-built LFP battery packs that can plug into charge ports two at a time for lickety-quick charging in about 30 minutes. If that’s too long of a wait, the Star can swap its tired batteries for fresh ones in special battery-sway stations in just six minutes.

The DeepWay’s electric motors put out 491 kW (~660 hp) of power, and a positively tectonic 75,000 (!) Nm (~55,000 lb-ft) (!!) of torque – more than enough to get the 50 tons (100,000 lb.) of GVWR it’s rated for up to speed.

It is great to see electric trucks replacing Diesel. I just can’t understand why EV adoption is so slow to take off in New Zealand. It has plenty of great low cost EVs and petrol is all imported and expensive.

You can check out the pictures, above, then let us know what you think of the big Chinese BEV and it’s fun new name in the comments.

#4915. Goldman Sachs reports $1.22B in Ether ETFs as ETHA share count jumps

Goldman Sachs increased its Ether ETF shares in Q3, bringing its reported holdings to $1.22 billion, while reshuffling its Bitcoin ETF positions.

Banking giant Goldman Sachs reported $1.22 billion in Ether exchange-traded fund (ETF) holdings at the end of September, up from $211.6 million three months earlier, as it increased positions across several funds throughout the third quarter.

The bank increased its holdings in BlackRock’s iShares Ethereum Trust ETF (ETHA) by 352% to 29.96 million shares, worth $602.9 million, according to its Q3 Form 13F filing with the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC).

The company also shifted its Bitcoin ETF holdings, reducing its stake in BlackRock’s Bitcoin (BTC) fund while increasing its position in Fidelity’s competing ETF.

The increase coincided with a strong quarter for Ether (ETH), which surged nearly 71% in Q3, according to CoinGlass. US spot Ether ETFs attracted more than $3 billion in net inflows, their strongest quarter of 2026, according to SoSoValue.

BlackRock’s ETHA was not the only Ether ETF in which Goldman Sachs increased its holdings during Q3.

The bank also increased its holdings in Grayscale’s Ethereum Staking Mini ETF (ETH) by 180% to 5.68 million shares, lifting the position’s reported value to $144.9 million from $30.5 million in Q2.

Its holdings in BlackRock’s iShares Staked Ethereum ETF (ETHB) rose 62% to 8.18 million shares, worth $281 million, up from $102.3 million in the previous quarter.

Goldman also reported four additional Ether ETF positions worth a combined $195.4 million, led by $169.6 million in Fidelity’s Ethereum Fund (FETH).

Including the new positions, Goldman’s reported Ether ETF holdings reached approximately $1.22 billion at the end of Q3, up from $211.6 million in Q2.

Goldman reduced its IBIT holdings by about 792,000 shares to 16.44 million, although the position’s reported value increased 36% to $778.3 million as IBIT’s price surged 42% over the quarter.

Meanwhile, its FBTC holdings surged more than 50-fold, from 105,840 shares to 5.48 million, bringing their reported value to $397.9 million from just $5.4 million.

Camran Khosravi, research analyst at global asset manager Bitwise, told Cointelegraph that market makers such as Jane Street can shift between Bitcoin ETFs to meet client demand and hedge options positions without necessarily changing their overall Bitcoin exposure.

Related: Jane Street boosts Fidelity Bitcoin ETF holdings nearly 8x, trims IBIT

“13F filings can make routine market-making look like a big bet on one fund,” Khosravi said, noting that the disclosures omit short positions and other instruments that may offset reported holdings.

While Goldman trimmed its IBIT shares, Wells Fargo more than doubled its holdings in the BlackRock Bitcoin ETF. The bank’s Q3 filing showed its IBIT position rising 103% to 49.58 million shares, worth around $2.35 billion, while its ETHA holdings increased 125% to 22 million shares.

#4913. Optimizely Hack Hijacked Ledger, BBC and 176 Other Sites to Swap Crypto Wallet Addresses

Optimizely Hack Hijacked Ledger, BBC and 176 Other Sites to Swap Crypto Wallet Addresses

An attacker hijacked Optimizely, a platform widely used by companies to run experiments on their websites, to serve malware that swapped visitors' crypto wallet addresses for their own, on sites including hardware wallet maker Ledger.

Optimizely said in a status update on Monday evening that an unauthorized party had exploited a vulnerability in its Experimentation product to add malicious JavaScript to some customer projects. The company said it had fixed the flaw, removed the injected code, secured affected user accounts and added protections to stop unauthorized code being delivered.

Earlier today, Optimizely identified and contained a security incident affecting some Optimizely Web Experimentation customers. The vulnerable path is blocked, the injected code was removed, and customers have been contacted directly.

— Optimizely (@Optimizely) November 17, 2026

Researchers at security firm Report URI, which sells tools to detect malicious website scripts, found the malicious code in 188 of the 1,611 Optimizely scripts they examined, belonging to 178 organizations, according to a blog post by founder Scott Helme. The affected sites included Zoom, the BBC, CNN, Salesforce, GitLab, IKEA and HSBC, as well as crypto and trading firms such as Ledger, IG and OANDA.

Because the code was served from Optimizely's own trusted servers, affected websites loaded it without their own systems being breached.

The malware's main purpose was to steal crypto. The script scanned web pages for valid wallet addresses on networks including Bitcoin, Ethereum, Tron, Solana and TON, and replaced them with addresses controlled by the attacker, according to Report URI. It also swapped addresses copied to a user's clipboard, meaning anyone who copied an address while an affected page was open could have pasted the attacker's instead.

The attacker's wallets received about $7,150 in crypto, mostly in Bitcoin, Litecoin and USDT, according to Report URI's update on Tuesday. The attacker emptied the wallets early on Tuesday. One Tron address used to cash out had also been used in earlier operations in March and on November 3, allowing Report URI to link the attacker to other attacks, the firm said.

The script also skimmed card details typed into some sites' own payment forms, though not those entered into hosted payment fields such as Stripe's, and on iPhones and iPads it attempted to load exploit kits known as Coruna and DarkSword, which target older versions of iOS. Report URI said it could not see how many card numbers were stolen or how many devices were compromised.

The injected code first appeared early on November 16 UTC, and had been removed from all of the scripts Report URI could find by late that evening.

Optimizely spokesperson Victoria Johnson told Gizmodo that an unauthorized party had "exploited a vulnerability in the Experimentation account signup flow," and that the company had blocked access to the vulnerable path, deployed a fix and notified all affected customers. The company has not said how the attacker gained access to other customers' projects.

Helme said visitors to affected sites on November 16 should check any crypto transfers they made, update their iPhones and iPads, and watch their card statements. Website operators should review custom code in their Optimizely projects and rotate credentials, he added.

#4910. Electric cars are about to overtake petrol in the UK, data shows

Battery-electric cars took 32% of the UK’s new car market in October, while petrol cars fell to just 37.2%, according to new data from the SMMT. That’s the closest the two have been.

At the current pace, electric cars will outsell petrol in the UK within a year. And it could happen as soon as this December.

The UK registered 152,730 new cars in October, up 5.4% year-over-year and the 11th straight month of growth for the overall market.

All of that growth came from plug-ins. BEV registrations jumped 32.7% to 48,858 units, and plug-in hybrids rose 30.1% to 22,898. Meanwhile, petrol fell 11.6% to 56,876 units, diesel dropped 15.9%, and even conventional hybrids were down 5%.

Through the first 10 months of the year, the UK has now registered 503,805 BEVs, up 30.4%, for a 26.6% market share. Petrol’s year-to-date share is down to 41.1%, from 47.2% at the same point last year.

We pulled SMMT’s monthly fuel-type data going back to March 2024 to see where this is heading. Note that SMMT counts mild hybrids as petrol (or diesel) in its headline categories, and that’s generally how we count them at Electrek.

In March 2024, petrol held 55.9% of the market and BEVs 15.2%. In October 2026, it was 37.2% vs 32.0%. Petrol’s share is shrinking by about half a point per month on a linear trend, while BEVs gain about 0.36 points a month. Extend both lines and they cross around August 2027 at roughly 33% each.

But that’s the slow scenario, because it ignores December.

UK BEV share spikes every December as automakers push volume to get closer to their ZEV Mandate targets. BEVs hit 31.0% in December 2024 and 32.2% in December 2025, when petrol was at 39.4%. Over the last five months, BEV share has run about 5 points higher than a year earlier, and petrol about 7 points lower. If December follows that pattern, BEVs land around 37% and petrol around 32%.

That would be the first time BEVs beat petrol in SMMT’s headline numbers, mild hybrids included.

However, SMMT’s own forecast of 27.2% BEV share for the full year implies roughly 31% for November and December combined, which would make it a much closer call. And on a stricter definition that puts mild hybrids with hybrids instead of petrol, BEVs already outsold every other powertrain in August.

Zoom out to full years and the picture is just as clear. Cars that run only on petrol or diesel, mild hybrids included, made up 72.5% of the UK market in 2021. This year, they’re at 45.5%.

None of this is good enough for the SMMT, which is using the release to push the government, again, to cut the ZEV Mandate targets.

The mandate requires 33% of 2026 sales to be zero-emission. SMMT now forecasts 27.2%. It expects 32% next year against a 38% target, and 37.5% in 2028 against 52%, a 14.5-point gap.

The trade group says automakers have spent “billions” on discounts, 0% financing, deposit contributions, and even subsidized charging to move EVs, on top of the government’s Electric Car Grant. It also slammed last week’s Budget, which confirmed a new EV-specific road tax (eVED) starting in 2028 and kept 20% VAT on public charging.

“Higher targets may force supply, but they do not create demand,” SMMT chief executive Mike Hawes said.

We’ve heard this one before. The SMMT made the same argument after September’s record 99,199 BEV registrations, and the government already opened a review of the mandate this year.

This might be the main reason why the trade group wants to slow things down. The brands growing fastest in the UK are mostly Chinese. BYD registered 6,065 cars in October, up 73%. Jaecoo more than doubled to 6,089 units, Chery nearly tripled to 3,707, and Leapmotor doubled to 1,773. MG was up 85%.

Tesla, on the other hand, registered just 282 cars in October, down 45% from 511 a year ago. The first month of the quarter is always slow for Tesla in the UK, and the brand is still up 31% year-to-date at 46,526 units after a big September. But it didn’t have a single model in October’s top 10 BEVs, which was led by the Skoda Enyaq (1,568), Mini Countryman (1,494), and Jaecoo 5 (1,465).

The SMMT’s framing is that there’s a “demand gap.” The data shows the opposite. BEV registrations are up 30% this year while petrol is shrinking every single month. That’s not a market rejecting EVs.

The SMMT is not a neutral organisation, they have been briefing against EVs for years and continue to do so.

The ZEV mandate is NOT a measure of how many pure BEVs are sold, and the SMMT know it, it's a measure of REDUCING emission against a level set in the past.

Those emissions reductions can be met by selling BEVs, or selling more efficient PHEVs or even selling more efficient petrol or diesel cars! The manufacturers can also buy credits if they choose to.

NO manufacturer has paid any ZEV fines to date (DfT announcement March 2026)

As for the "heavy discounts" and "loss making sales", well I have two data points on that.

1. The retail price of EVs has continued to climb in the UK over the past 6 years, despite the cost of any average 50kWh battery pack dropping several thousand dollars.

2. There are hundreds of main dealerships who are buying cars from the manufacturers, driving them for the minimum 3 month period, often with just a few thousand miles on the clock and then selling them with a 30-35% retail price discount, and then repeating ad infinitum. The dealerships are still making a few thousand with each transaction, so we know where the manufacturers price really sits.

And the discounts automakers keep complaining about? That’s the mandate doing exactly what it was designed to do. It forces automakers to sell EVs, so they price them to sell, and buyers respond. Hawes says targets don’t create demand, but cheaper EVs clearly do. October proves it.

Now, is the jump to 52% in 2028 steep? It is steep, but it looks achievable to me. If anything is hurting demand, it’s the government’s own moves: a new EV-only road tax and 20% VAT on public charging. That’s what the industry should be fighting. It’s too early for that.

Either way, petrol’s days as the UK’s default car are numbered. The only question is whether the milestone comes in December or sometime in 2027. Our bet is December.

#4909. US judge clears brothers in $25M Ethereum MEV case after mistrial

Judge Jessica Clarke said the transactions did not involve false promises, but the ruling did not determine whether the brothers’ conduct was ethical or violated other laws.

A US federal judge acquitted two brothers accused of extracting $25 million from Ethereum trading bots, ruling that prosecutors failed to prove they made material misrepresentations during the alleged exploit.

In a Monday ruling, Judge Jessica Clarke acquitted Anton and James Peraire-Bueno on wire fraud and related charges. Prosecutors alleged that the MIT-educated brothers exploited a flaw in maximal extractable value (MEV)-boost software in April 2023 to lure sandwich-trading bots into transactions they then disrupted, securing $25 million in about 12 seconds.

The judge said the transactions did not amount to false promises or constitute criminal fraud, and that the case was not about whether the brothers’ actions were ethical or potentially violated other laws.

Their first trial ended in a mistrial when jurors failed to reach a verdict in November 2025.

Coin Center executive director Peter Van Valkenburgh called the acquittal “excellent news,” saying prosecutors’ honest validator theory risked allowing the government to impose its own standards of conduct on Ethereum validators through wire fraud laws.

“Anyone participating in an open network would have to wonder whether prosecutors might later declare their behavior ‘dishonest’ and haul them into court,” Valkenburgh said.

The ruling also cited a 2025 decision involving Mango Markets trader Avraham Eisenberg, where a judge found insufficient evidence of false representations.

#4908. 'The Biggest Opportunity in Crypto Is Already Underway': Solana Bets Big on AI Agents

'The Biggest Opportunity in Crypto Is Already Underway': Solana Bets Big on AI Agents

AI agents are becoming the internet's main users, and the Solana Foundation wants its blockchain to be how they pay for things.

"The biggest opportunity in crypto is already underway," Rishin Sharma, who leads AI at the Solana Foundation, said at the Breakpoint conference in London.

Sharma used his presentation to showcase Solana.ai, a new homepage for the network's AI ecosystem, which the foundation launched last week. More than 120 teams are now building AI products on Solana across payments, trading and infrastructure, he said.

Bots and other automated software accounted for more than 53% of global web traffic in 2025, up from 51% the year before, according to cybersecurity firm Imperva's latest Bad Bot Report, which Sharma cited. Agents have also come to dominate AI usage: API keys classified as agentic accounted for 76% of the tokens processed through model marketplace OpenRouter in October 2026, up from 8% a year earlier, according to the company's monthly data recap.

At the same time, AI is getting much cheaper. Sharma's slides showed the price of a million tokens falling from $37.50 for GPT-4 to $0.14 for GPT-5 nano, a 270-fold drop, while monthly token use has risen around 330-fold.

"Which model is smarter matters a little bit less, and what matters more is what the model can actually do," Sharma said. "What are the tools it's using? What are the data it's able to access? And what money can it functionally use?"

Payments are where that shift is showing up first, Sharma said. The x402 standard, which lets AI agents pay for online services, has processed more than 400 million payments and $100 million in volume, up from zero a year ago, he said. Solana-based BlockRun, which he described as the largest x402 server, has handled more than 60 million transactions in the past two months.

The foundation has also launched payment channels that it says can handle 1 million payments per second, a private AI service offering attested private inference on Solana, and Pay.sh, a server integrating more than 100 APIs that agents can pay for, Sharma said. He added that the foundation is working with companies including Google, Alibaba and ByteDance to let them charge for their AI models through agent payments.

Next, the foundation plans to publish benchmarks for how well AI models perform financial tasks, and to launch an on-ramp that lets users convert cash to stablecoins and hand them to their AI assistants, he said.

Macro investor and Real Vision founder Raoul Pal, speaking earlier at the conference, argued that agents are not yet real economic actors. "They're like kids," he said. "These are young kids who spend nothing on a few sweets and stuff." But as stocks and other assets move onchain, he said, agents will start using blockchains to trade and settle, and "the value accrues to the underlying layer one."

Solana has been building toward this for months. The foundation teamed up with Google Cloud on a stablecoin payments service for AI agents in May, while the x402 Foundation, backed by Coinbase and the Linux Foundation, is developing the payments standard. Franklin Templeton has described agentic AI as a killer use case for crypto.
